Appendix D
METHOD OF ESTIMATING DISTRIBUTION (EMPLOYMENT)
OF JAPAN'S COMMERCIAL SHIPPING
Table 5a.
1. Totals (mumber and gross tonnage) are derived from Table 1
and from Kaiun Oyobi Keisai Chosa Hokoka (Survey of Shipping and Marine
Economy) of Jamuary-June, 1938.
2. The estimate for shipping employed in the overseas area is
the sum of (a) shipping employed on regular overseas routes at the end
of 1937, as given by Kaiun Oyobi Keisai Chosa Hokoku (Survey of Shipping
and Marine Economy) of July-December, 1937, plus (b) shipping engaged
on irregular runs in the overseas area at the beginning of Janmary, 1938,
given in deadweight tons by the Oriental Economist of November, 1940.
Conversion to gross tonnage is made on the basis of an analysis of
deadweight-to-gross ratios for Japanese ships, 1/ which indicates an
average of about 1.5 (d.w.t. to 1 g.t.) for the small-to-medium sise
ships generally used within the nearseas area, and about 1.4 for the
above-medium size vessels generally employed in overseas service.
3. The estimate for shipping engaged "within the nearseas area"
is made on the same basis as that for the overseas area, with the
arbitrary addition of all sailing vessels 20 g.t. and over (other than
known fishing vessels and those reported as laid up), and of all vessels
less than 20 g.t. (after deducting Imom fishing vessels and 10% of the
remaining total, the estimate assumed for layupa).
4. The estimate for "other disposition" is explained in Table 5c.
and its footnotes.
1/ The Dook and Harbor Authority, December, 1940; Military
Intelligence Photo Interprotation Report No. 51,397, February
20,1945。
16-000 Pr or 4 bu--

Table 5b.
1. Total shipping estimated to have been employed within the
limits of the nearseas area is derived from Table 5a., and is
sxplained in paragraph 2, above.
2. Estimates for the "ealm water", "coastal", and "nearseas"
areas were arrived at by applying to the total for "within nearseas"
(end of 1937) the average pereentage of the corresponding total
empleyed within each of the three areas in earlier years. An analysis
was made of date from Kaiii Yoren (Maritime Affairs) for 1936, showing
employment of stean and motor vessels (1916-1934) and sailing vessels
(1916-1927); combined totals for 1925-1927 (inolusive) were taken to
calculate the average percentages. Satisfactery data for later years
are lacking.

Appendix E
NOTES REGARDING CARGO TONNAGE ESTIMATES
USED IN TABLE 6a
1. Evaluations for cargoes between (a) Japan and foreign
countries, (b) Japan and Korea, and (c) Japan and Formosa were
made on the basis of statistics appearing in the trade returns
cited in the table's list of sources. Quantities in units other
than long tens were converted by using standard conversion factors
appearing in Far Eastern Meagurement Units (mimeographed pamphlet
issued by F.E.A.; undated). The figures are not complete to the
last ton. For some commodities no quantity statistics are included
in the trade returns, and no satisfactory formula is available fer
converting valme figures to quantity figures for the commodities
in question.
2. Estimates for cargoes between Japan and Karafuto and Japan
and the Mandated Islands (Nanyo) are explained in footnote "o" of
the table.

Appendix F
METHOD OF ESTIMATING
BULK CARGO CARRYING CAPACITIES
(Table 7)
1. "Basic" eapacity estimates were made by the Joint Target
Group in the report eited wnder Table 7. The methodology employed
by J.T.G. is explained thoroughly and at some length in the report.
The salient points are as follows:
(a) For each kind of vessel the approximate deadweight
to gross ton ratio and average speed (kmots) is given;
(b) For each area of operation the average mumber of
operational days per year is given, based upon
meteorological information, and with variations for
full-powered and anxiliary-powered vessels;
(c) For each sample run the round trip distance (nautical
miles) estimated time in port (in days) are given;
(d) Number of round trips per year is then calculated
as follows:
(1) Round trip distance divided by speed in kmots
gives running time;
(2)
Running time plus time in port equals turn-
around time;
(3)
Operational days per year divided by turn-
arond time (in days) gives number of round
trips annually.
(e) Annual bulk cargo carrying capacity is then calculated
as: gross tonnage (of shipping allocation) times
deadweight-to-gross ratio times mumber of round trips
per year.
2. "Probable maximum" capacity estinates are 1l0% of "basic"
estimates, as explained in footnoted "a" of Table 7.

JOINT CHIEFS OF STAFF
DISPOSITION OF CAPTURED JAPANESE MERCHANT VESSELS
References:
a. J.C.S. 1500 Series
b. a.c.s. 928
Roport by the Joint Logistics Committee in collaboration
With the Joint Military Transportation Committee
THE PROBLEM
1. To submit recommendations on a memorandum from the War
Shipping Administrator concerning the legal aspects of
divesting the interests of Japanese nationels in Japanese
merchant vessels (J.C.S. 1500/2).
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. The Supreme Conmander for the Allied Powers (SCAP) has
estimated that approximately one and one-half million tons of
shipping (vessels over one hundred gross tons) were under
Japanese control upon the cossation of hostilities. It is
estimated that this tonnage comprises vessels numbering upward
of 600.
3. There are three possible procedures to divest Japanese
interest:
. Prize procedure in United States courts or in the
ports of consenting co-belligerents or in territory which
is in the temporary or permanent possession or occupation
by U.S. armed forces.
b. Requisition by the Japanese Government of the interest
of its nationals in Japanese merchant vessels and thereafter
the transfer of title in such vessels es desired by SCAP.

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
S E C R ET
C
c. Provision in the treaty of peace with Japan similar
to that of the Treaty of Versailles with Germany, ratifying
the requisition and transfer of title of Japanese merchant
vessels.
DISCUSSION
4. Appendix "c" (page 14) sets forth the details of the
procedure under these three alternative methods.
CONCLUSIONS
5. SCAP should direct the Japanese Government to requisition
the title of all Japanese merchant vessels which it is desired
be appropriated and then transfer the requisitioned interest to
SCAP in behalf of the United Nations. This transfer of interest
should be without prejudice to agreements ultimately to be
made among the Allied governments concerned as to the
disposition of title to such vessels.
6. The treaty of peace with Japan should contain an appropriate
provision ratifying this transfer of interest.
7. Prize proceedings should be initiated in particular cases
where difficulty or uncertain ownership appears.
8. In view of its political implications, this matter should
be referred to the State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee before
instructions are transmitted to SCAP.
RECOMMENDATIONS
9. It is recommended that:
a. The memorandum
in Appendix "A" (page 12) be forwarded
to the State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee.
b. Upon the concurrence of the State-War-Navy Coordinating
Committee, the message in Appendix "B" (page 13) be dispatched
to SCAP.
C. A copy of this report be furnished to the Administrator,
War Shipping Administration.

MEMORANDUM FOR THE STATE-WAR-NAVY COORDINATING COMMITTEE
Enclosed are copies of J.C.S. 1500/2 and J.c.S. 1500/3
concerning the legal aspects of divesting the interests of
Japanese nationals in Japanese morchant vessels. It is
requested that the State-War-Navy Coordinating Committee concur
in the dispatch of tho message in Appendix "B" to J.c.S. 1500/3
to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
It is roquested that when a treaty of peace with Japan
is drafted provision be made for ratifying the transfer of
interest as set forth in J.c.s. 1500/3.

APPENDIX "B"
D R A F T
MESSAGE FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED
POWERS
Reference is WARX 64268 (Appendix "A" to J.C.S. 1500
as amended by J.c.S. 1500/1). The Joint Chiefs of Staff have
studied the most offective and oxpeditious method of divesting
the interest of Japanese nationals in Japanese merchant vessels
and consider the following procedure as the most offective:
a. The Supremo Commander for the Allied Powers should
direct the Japanese Government to requisition the title of
all Japanese merchant vessels which it is desired be
appropriated and then transfer the requisitioned interest
to the Supreme Commander. The Supreme Commander is to
receive and hold such interest in bohalf of the United
Nations. This transfer of interest is to bo regarded as
being without prejudice to agreen nts ultimately to be mado
among the Allied governments concerned as to disposition of
title to such vessols.
b. The treaty of peace should contain an appropriato
provision ratifying this transfer of interest.
c. Prizo proccedings should be initiated in particular
cases where difficulty or uncertain ownership appcars.

APPENDIX "C"
PROCEDURE
FOR
DIVESTING
JAPANESE INTEREST
IN
JAPANESE MERCHANT
VESSELS
1. There are three methods of divesting the interest of
Jepanese nationals in Jepanese merchant vessels, which under
International Law could be thc subject of prize proceedings:
a. By prize proccedings in United States District Courts:
(1) Under the former Pr e Law (34 USC 1131 et seg.):
This involves bringing the Japanese vessel to the United
States or its tcrritories end instituting prize pro-
ceedings through the filing of a libel and the attach-
ment of the vessel by a United States marshal.
This
was the proccdure followed in connection with the SS
EUROPA.
The ncarest Federal Court for Pacific Ocean
cases would be Hawaii.
(2) Through resort to the Prize Law of 1942 (34 USC
1159 et seq.), the so-called co-belligerent port prize
procedure: This would involve sailing Japanese vessels
to ports of a consenting co-belligerent, such as Grest
Eritain, Australia or New Zealand, where initial prize
proceedings are had before specicl Prize commissioners.
(The availability of Hong Kong as a forum is in doubt.)
(3) By a procedure similar to (2) in territory in the
temporary or permanent possession or occupation of armed
forces of the United States: This is provided for in 
pending amendment to the 1942 Prize Law and is specifi-
cally designed to care for problems arising in the
Pacific Ocean areas, where vessels cannot be conveniently
brought to the ports of a consenting co-belligerent.
This bill No. S 1420, as of this date, has passed the
Senate and has been favorably reported by the House

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
S E C R E T
Judiciary Committee. When enacted it would permit
initial prize proceedings to be conducted in any occupied
territory. Final disposition of the case would be in the
Northern District of California. Anticipating the
possibility of prize procedurc in the Pecific Ocean areas,
a Special Prize Commissioner (Lt. Comdr. Harry L. Hachl,
USNR), who could function under (2) and (3) has been
designated by the Judge Advocate General of the Navy
and is now attached as Admiralty and Prize Officer to
the Staff of SCAP.
The number of Japanesc vessels, which would be subject to
prize (apart from the fact that proceedings would not be
terminated for a year) make this procedurc of dealing with
each individual ship through condcmnation in prize extremoly
cumbersome and would involve considerable time and labor.
However, in the ovent of controversy as to the status of 
vessel, it is the safest procedure since prize proceedings
convey a title which should be universally recognized.
b. Requisition and transfer of interest:
Instructions to the Jupanese Government to requisition all
the interest of Japanese nationals in Japenese merchant
vessels which it is desired to cppropriate. The Japanese
Government would then be directed to transfer this requi-
sitioned interest as desired by the Supreme Command.
The
Commander, Marianas, has suggested to the Commander in Chief,
U.S. Pacific Fleet, on the recommendation of the Judge
Advocate General of the Navy, considoration of this pro-
cedure (Annex, page 18). The procedure in this paragraph 
should be buttressed by a provision in the treaty of peace,
paragraph c below
c. Peace Trcaty:
By the insertion of a provision in any treaty of peace with
Japan whereby the Japanese Government conveys to the United

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
States or other countries, as desired, the title and all
interest of its nationals in any Jepanese vessel which it
is desired be appropriated. This procedure is that which
was adopted in World War I to divest any interest of German
nationals in the German merchant marine. The provisions in
the Treaty of Versailles were:
1
"Germany recognises the right of the Allied and Associated
Powers to the replacement, ton for ton (gross tonnage)
and class for class, of all merchant ships and fishing
boats lost or damaged owing to the wer.
"Nevertheless, and in spite of the i&ct that the tonnage
of German shipping at present in existence is much less
than that lost by the Allied and Associeted Powers in
consequence of the German aggression, the right thus
recognised will be enforced on German ships and boats
under the following conditions:
"The Gorman Government, on behalf of themselves and so
as to bind all other persons interested, cede to the
Allied and Associated Gevernments the property in all
the German merchant ships which are of 1, 600 tons gross
and upwards, in one-half, reckoned in tonnage, of the
shins which are between 1, 000 tons cnd 1, 60c tons gross;
in one-quarter, reckoned in tonnage, of the steam trawl-
ers; and in one-quarter reckoned in tonnage, of the other
i ishing boets."
2
"The German Government will ithin two months of the coming
into force of the present Treaty, deliver to the Repara-
tion Commission all the ships and boats mentioned in
paragraph 1."
3
"The ships and boats mentioned in paregraph 1 include
all ships and boats which (a) fly, or mey be entitled
to fly, the German merchant fleg: or (b) ere owned by
any German nationel, company or corporetion or by any
company or corporation belonging to a country other than
an Allied or Associated country and under the control or
direction of German nationals; or (c) cre now under con-
struction (1) in Germany, (2) in otner than Allied or
Associated countries for the account of any German nation-
al, company or corporetion."
4
"For the purpose o. providing documents of title for the
ships and boats to be handed over as above mentioned,
the German Government will:
"(a) Deliver to the Reparation Commission in respect
of each vessel a bill of sale or other document of title
evidencing the transfer to & Commission of the entire

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.96)
S E C RE T
property in the vessel, free from all encumbrances,
charges and liens of all kinds, as the Commission may
require;
"(b) Take all measures that may be indicated by the
Reparation Commission for ensuring that the ships
themselves shell be placed at its disposal."
*****************
S
"Gormany waives all cleims of any deseription egeinst
the Allied and Associated Governments and their nationals
in respest of the detention, omployment, lose or damage
of eny German ships or boats except on being made of
payments due in respoet of the employment of ships in
conformity with the Armistice Agreement of January 13,
1919, and subscquent Agreoments.
"The handling over of the ships of the German mercantile
marine must be continued without interruption in eccord-
ance with the said Agreement."
"Germany waives ell cleims to vessels or cargoes sunk
by or in consequenee of naval aetion and subsequently
salved, in which any ci the Allied er Assosieted Govern-
mente or their nationale mey have an. interest either
as owners, charterers, insurers or ctherwise, notwith-
standing any deeree of condemnation whicn may have been
made by e Prize Court of Cormary or of hor allics."
2. It is thought that eitner procedure i L or 1 c. or fer
preferably their combinet on, will be the most effeetive and
expeditious method of divesting the interest of Japanese
nationals in appropriating Jepanese merchens vessels. Any
cases of apparent uncertain ownership or diificulty could be
the subject of speciel prize proceedings.

received in reply ComMarianas 180719, both September. CINCPAC/
PoA information addressee on ComMarianas dispatch but not on
JAG's, toxt of which follows:
Ur 18&719 JAG view is that any prize procedure adopted
for Japanese vessels must be controlled by operating situation
as required by military considerations. Necessity of or con-
venionce in handling prize proceedings should not be advanced
as  factor controlling Japanese vessel operation or dis-
position. Plan of operation specified your despatch affords
additional support for pending amendment to prize laws which
would permit necessary preliminary proceedings by prize com-
missioner in occupied Japanese territory. Approve suggested
procedure of direction by Supreme Commander that Japenese
Government requisition merchant vessels desired by United
Nations and transfer title if possible of accomplishment.
Suggest commander Marianas make this recommendation to CINCPAC
for consideration by Supreme Commander. Pending determination
this procedure and amendment of prize laws consider prize
commissioner should take any preliminary steps where situation
is s ch that prize commissioners action would confer juris-
diction on court. Suggest decision transfer prize commissioner
to Pcarl or Japan await further developments and
clarification
situation.
2. Suggestion made by ComMarianas and approved by JAG is
based on assumption United Nations intend acquire title to
some if not all Japanese merchant vessels.
Such title normally

S E C R E T
acquired during hostilities by seizure, adjudication and for-
feiture as prize of war. After cessation hostilities but
before termination of war, title may be acquired in same
manner or as term of international agreement between victor
and vanquished nation. After World War I, title to Gorman
merchant vessels not acquired under prize procecure but is
understood to have been transferred by internetional agree-
ment, Whereas after cessation  ostilities in Europe in World
War II prine proceedings were commonced as to such vessels.
While consicerations leadin: to decision emplo prize
machinery in European Theater not known here, it is believed
that absence of clearly established German government follow-
ing cessati on hostilities and consecuent impossibility of
obtainine thtle by aetion or cermar government whiel would
be immune to subsecuent attaek as unauthorized, may have con-
tribute to ecision.
3. Prise proceedings are necessarily sumbereome and long
drawn out dus to necessity of rivins possible claimants to
vessel full opportunity to appoar and contost validity of
prize seirrre. Henee, if methoe other than formal prise
proceedings is available to secure title to desired vesscls,
it would be more expeditiouf to employ it if no pre judice
to legality of title so ecquired were involved.
4. Present Japanese Government believed to possess all
powers formerly held except in so far exeercise thereof now
subordinated to Seap direetion. No question exists as to
which of soveral groups is the authorized government. Hence,
it is bolievei present Japanese Government, if so directed by

acquiro as agreed upon, by the Allied Governments concerned.

strong Swedish protests. As it seemed unavoidable that the

charter agreement they were at least bound to pay for its
use.
Charter hire was paid until September 9, 1944.
A brief history cf the seizure of the MIRAlAR and its
.1.
charter to the Japanese Taikoku Steamship Company is here-
with attached.
According to information given to the Swedish Legation
in Tokyo by the American authorities in Japan the above
mentioned Japanese shipping company was controlled by the
Japanese government.
As, furthermore, the MIRAMAR had been
utilized by the Japanese navy beforethe charter arrangement
was made it has been indicated to the Swedish Legation in
Tokyo that the vessel may be liable to seizure as a Japanese
vessel.
It has been indicated to the Swedish Legation in Tokyo
that the case will be referred to the State Department and the
Navy Department in Washington for consideretion. The Swedish
Legation has, therefore, been instructed by the Swedish
Ministry for Foreign Affairs to recall the abovementioned
circumstances under which the MIRAMAR was seized by the
Japanese in violation of the Swedish flag and existing rights
of navigation. The later charter arrangement was the only
course open to the Swedish Authorities for avoiding the
confiscation of the vessel, which was already then under
Japanese control. In view thereof it is sincerely hoped that
the U.S. Authorities will see their way clear now to release

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
strong Swedish protests.
As it seemed unavoidable
Japanese otherwise would confiscate the vessel the S
Legation in Tokyo entered into negotiations with the Japan
authorities and succeeded in the latter part of 1942 to
at an arrangement by which the vessel was chartered to the
Japanese Taikoku Steamship Company, upon condition that the
vessel only should be used in commercial traffic. By this
arrangement it was prevented that the ship was altogether
lost for the Swedish Company. Certainly, the Japanese would
not have hesitated to use the vessel in any case but by the
charter agreement they were at least bound to pay for its
use.
Charter hire was paid until September 9, 1944.
A brief history of the seizure of the MIRAMAR and its
charter to the Japanese Taikoku Steamship Company is here-
with attached.
According to information given to the Swedish Legation
in Tokyo by the American authorities in Japan the above
mentioned Japanese shipping company was controlled by the
Japanese government. As, furthermore, the MIRAMAR had been
utilized by the Japanese navy beforethe charter arrangement
was made it has been indicated to the Swedish Legation in
Tokyo that the vessel may be liable to seizure as a Japanese
vessel.
It has been indicated to the Swedish Legation in Tokyo
that the case will be referred to the State Department and the
Navy Department in Washington for consideretion. The Swedish
Legation has, therefore, been instructed by the Swedish
l'inistry for Foreign Affairs to recall the abovementioned
circumstances under which the 1RAl AR was seized by the
Japanese in violation of the Swedish flag and existing rights
of navigation.
The later charter arrangement was the only
course open to the Swedish Authorities for avoiding the
confiscation of the vessel, which was already then under
Jepanese control.
In view thereof it is sincerely hoped that
the U.S. Authorities will see their way clear now to release
the MI ANAR to the Swedish owners.
Washington, D.C., December 18, 1945.

Our Norwegian friend formerly owned the first
whaling ship on the attached list which the Japanese later
acquired and duplicated in larger vessels. Our friend is
extremely anxious to acquire one or two of these vessels for

experienced. He believes that a number of them have been
lost, but one or more are extant and would like to get the
following information:
1. Which vessels, if any, are still in existence?
2. Where are they located?
3. Approximately, what is their condition?
4. Does the United States have jurisdiction over
their disposition?

for purchase?

Tokyo, January 14th, 1942.
Monsieur le Ministre,
I have the honour to inform Your Excellency of the follo
ing events.
On the 9th instant I received a telegraphic report from
Saigon to the effect that a Swedish steamship s/s "Miramar" had
been seized in that port and ordered to leave for unknown desti
nation, the Swedish officers being forced to remain on board an
to follow the ship. As this action for obvious reasons must ha
been committed by Japanese military forces in connection with ti
arfare in the South Pacific, I imrediately celled on the Forei
Vice-! inister to lodge a complaint and to ask for the release of
the vessel.
Under the days which followed a few more telegraphic repor
reached me, confirming the seizure by the Japanese and making it
possible to reconstilute the occurences in detail and to judge
true import of this very regrettable incident.
The s/s "Liramar" belonging to the Reuter Steamship Com-
p: ny in tockholm was chartered until the end of February 1942 by
the Persian firm Menazee. 1t was actually en route from Bangkok
to Lacao with a crago consisting only of rice. The ship celled
at Saigon for instructions and was then seized by the Imperial
Japanese Naval Authorities. ost of the cargo was ordered to be
discherged at Saigon and the ship was com anded by the Japanese
Naval Authorities to sail on January 1gth for unknown destination
with a new cargo taken in. The officers of s/s "Miramar" of whon
five were "edish and one Norwegian were forced to remain on duty
The Svedish captain, however, was not admitted on board.
Fron the facts which have just been related it can be con-
cluded that there did not exist any justified reason for the
seizure of the ship. The action taken by the Imperial Naval Au-
thorities therefore without any doubt constitutes a violation of
the neutrality of the Swedish flag, as well as a violation of pri
vate £wedish property and the right of navigation. It seems that
His Excellency
Monsieur Shigenori Togo,

the action also implied a measure of constraint upon the
liberty of the officers belonging to the ship.
Acting upon instructions thet I have received from
Government I am obliged to protest to the Imperial Governm
against the above-related action of the Japanese Naval Aut
At the same time I wish to ask Your Excellency to be good
to take the necessary steps in order to have the ship in qu
s/s "Miramer", released without delay. I hope Your Excelle
will kindly give me ? reply to my request at the earliest
date.
I avail myself of this opportunity, Monsieur le Minis
to renew to Your Excellency the assurances of my highest co
ration.
(sgd) W. Bagge

ENCLOSURE
STATEMENT OF U.S. POLIOY
A. The control of SCAP over former Allied vessels
recaptured from the Japanese should be extended to
authority to use former Allied war and other publle
vessels and miscellaneous water-borne craft of all types.
b. SCAf should be authorized to release all former
Allied war or other public vessels regardless of size
(except public merchant vessels) and all former Allied
water-borne craft of any type of less than 500 gross
tons direot to olaimant nations when these vessels
are no longer needed by SCAP for any task conneoted
with the implementation of the Japanese surrender.
c. The CSAB should be designated as SCAP's agent
for the purpose of disposing of former Allied publio
merchant or privately o'ned merchant vessels and other
privately owned water-borne craft of all types of 500
gross tons and over.

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
ENCLOSURE
ARRANGEMENTS FOR RELEASE TO CLAIMANT NATIONS OF
FORMER ALLIED VESSELS CAPTUHED BY JAPAN
Report by the
State-War-Navy Coordinating Subcommittee for the Fer East
THE PROBLEM
1. To consider draft directives to the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers regarding arrangements for the release
to claimant nations of former Allied vessels captured by the
Japanese.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. See Appendix "A".
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
4.
It is concluded that!
a. The control of SCAP over former Allied vessels
recaptured from the Japanese should be extended to author-
ity to use former Allied war and other public vessels and
miscellaneous water-borne craft of all types.
b. SCAP should be authorized to release all former
Allied war or other public vessels regardless of size
(except public merchant vessels) and all former Allied
water-borne craft of any type of less than 500 gross tons
direct to claimant nations when these vessels are no
longer needed by SCAP for any task connected with the
implementation of the Japanese surrender.
c. The CSAB should be designated as SCAP's agent for
the purpose of disposing of former Allied public merohant
or privately owned merchant vessels and other privately
owned water-borne craft of all types of 500 gross tons
and over.

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.98)
B E C R E T
RECOMENDATIONS
It is recommended that:
a. SWNCC approve the conclusions in paragraph 4 above.
b. The memorandum in Appendix "C" (together with the
Annex to Appendix "C") be forwarded to the JCS.
c. Upon dispatch of the directive in the Annex to
appendix "C" to SCAP, the JCS notify the CSAB of its
designation as SCAP's agent for the purpose of disposing
of former Allied public merchant or privately owned mer-
chant vessels and other privately owned water-borne oraft
of all types of 5o0 gross tons and over.
d. Upon notification by the JCS that the direotive
in the Annex to Appendix "C" has been dispatched to SOAP,
the Conclusions in paragraph 4 and the message in the
Annex to Appendix "C" be forwarded by the Department of
State to the members of the Far Eastern Commission.

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.99)
SEC RET
APPENDIX "A"
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. CCS 928/1 provided that SCAP should control all ship-
ping recovered from the Japanese as a matter of military ex-
pediency, in the absence of complete information about shipning
recovered from the Japanese and until the situation had cleared.
2. JCS 1500/3 states:
"a. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers should
direct the Japanese Government to requisition the title of
all Japanese merchant vessels which it is desired be ap-
propriated and then transfer the requisitioned interest
to the Supreme Commander. The Supreme Commander is to
receive and hold such interest on behalf of the United
Nations. This transfer of interest is to be regarded as
being without prejudice to agreements ultimately to be
made among the Allied Governments concerned as to disposi-
tion of title to any such vessels.
"b. The treaty of peace should contain an appropriate
provision ratifying this transfer of interest.
"c. Prize proceedings should be initiated in particular
cases when difficulty or uncertain ownership appears."
3. SWNCC 227/3, Request from Chinese for Return of Dredger
Chien Sho and Light Cruiser Yat Sen: Interim Procedures for
Restitution of Identifiable Looted Property, presently before
the Joint Chiefs of Staff for their comment, provides interim
procedures for restitution of identifiable looted property found
within Japan (with the exception of gold and other precious
metals, tin, precious gems, foreign exchange and other securi-
ties, and ships and water-borne craft of all kinds).
4. In SWNCC 227/5/D the JCS have requested the State,
War and Navy Departments' approval of directives to SCAP on the
subject.
5. Informal agreement was reached on January 15, 1946,
between the War Shipping Administration and the British Ministry
of War Transport to the designation of CsAB as the ageney of
SCAP for the disposal of Allied public merchant or privately
owned merchant vessels

FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. CCS 928/1 provided that SCAP should control all ship
ping recovered from the Japanese as a matter of military ex
pediency, in the absence of complete information about shipning
recovered from the Japanese and until the situation had cleared.
2. JCS 1500/3 states:
"a. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers should
direct the Japanese Government to requisition the title of
all Japanese merchant vessels which it is desired be ap-
propriated and then transfer the requisitioned interest
to the Supreme Commander. The Supreme Commander is to
receive and hold such interest on behalf of the United
Nations. This transfer of interest is to be regarded as
being without prejudice to agreements ultimately to be
made among the Allied Governnents concerned as to disposi-
tion of title to any such vessels.
"b. The treaty of peace should contain an appropriate
provision ratifying this transfer of interest.
"c. Prize proceedings should be initiated in particular
cases when difficulty or uncertain ownership appears."
3. SWNCC 227/3, Request from Chinese for Return of Dredger
Chien Sho and Light Cruiser Yat Sen: Interim Procedures for
Restitution of Identifiable Looted Property, presently before
the Joint Chiefs of Staff for their comment, provides interim
procedures for restitution of identifiable looted property found

metals, tin, precious gems, foreign exchange and other securi-
ties, and ships and water-borne craft of all kinds).

SCAP for the disposal of Allied public merchant or privately
owned merchant vessels and other privately owned water-borne
craft of all types of 500 gross tons and over.

Appendix "A"

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.99)
B E C R ET
APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers should have
the full use of all former Allied vessels recaptured from Japan
for as long as he deems it necessary to accomplish the objeo-
tives of the occupation, particularly in effecting repatriation
of Japanese Armed Forces and civilians overseas. It is con-
sidered, however, that there is need for some provision, by
which SCAP may release former Allied vessels after he dètermines
that he has no need for such vessels, without prejudice to final
title and disposition pending governmental agreements on those
matters.
2.
Where the title of vessels held by SCAP is aoparent,
the most expeditious method of disposal would be direct release
by SCAP, when the military situation permits, to the claimant
nation. However, the legal aspects of the transfer of former
Allied public merchant or privately owned merchant vessels or
other privately owned water-borne craft of all types of 500
gross tons and over would be complicated and would be an.undesir-
able burden to place on SCAP. In view of the fact that the
CSAB is a thoroughly competent agent to carry out negotiations
necessary for the disposal of such vessels and the execution
of agreements to protect the Allied Powers from future claims
by other nations, it is considered that the CSAB is the desir-
able agent to act for SCAP in these matters. The obvious dis-
advantage to the designation of the CSAB as the agent of SCAP
in the disposal of such vessels is the fact that CSAB is not
an agency of all the Allied Powers. However, since shipping
matters of general interest to the Allied Powers during the
war were handled by CSAB, it is not considered that serious
objection will be made by other nations to this designation.
3. In view of the fact that informal

MEMORANDUM FOR THE JOINT CHIEFS OF STAFF:
Subject:
Arrangements for Release to Claimant Nations
of Former Allied Vessels Captured by Japan.
1. Reference J.C.S. memorandum dated 15 January 1946 (SM-4726)
the State, War and Navy Departments concur in the draft direotive
to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, subject to the
amendments indicated in the Annex.

War Shipping Administration and the British Ministry of War Trans-
port provides adequate authority for the designation of the CSAB
as the agency of the Supreme Commander to dispose of former Allied
public merchant or privately owned merchant vessels and other
privately owned water-borne craft of all types of 5o0 gross tons
and over. Therefore the supplementary information dispatch to
SCAP is not required.

the Joint Chiefs of Staff concur in the revised directive to SCAP
and dispatch same to SCAP and that the Secretary, SWNCC, be in-
formed when the revised directive has been approved and dispatched
to SCAP in order that the Secretary of State may advise the nations
represented on the Far Eastern Commission.

further requested that the JCS notify the CSAB of its designation
as SCAP's agent for the purpose of disposing of former Allied
public merchant or privately owned merchant vessels and other
privately owned water-borne craft of all types of 5oo gross tons
and over.

The State, War and Navy Departments have approved the
policy that the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers (SCAP)
shall utilize fully all former allied ships and water-borne
craft of all types recaptured from Japan for as long as SCaP
may deem it necessary for the discharge of his responsibilities
with regard to repatriation of Japanese nationals and the main-
tenance of a minimum Japenese economy. Any Allied agreements
on these matters will be negotiated on this basis.
You will note that this policy extends the principles or
WaR 64268 of 14 September 1945 to provide that war and other
public vessels and miscellaneous water-borne craft of all types
as well as merchant vessels may be employed by SCP on Japanese
movements.
Relative to the disposition of such ships and craft when
SCAP no longer deems them necessary, you are now authorized to
release any former Allied war or other public vessel regardless
of size (except a public merchant vessel) and all former llicd
water-borne craft of any type of less than 5o0 gross tons
direct to claimant nation where title to vessel is apparent,
provided that an agreement with such nation on'release of
vessel or craft is executed which will provide adequate proteo-
tion to SCAP and the Allied Powers from any liability that may
arise out of the disposition or use of such vessels or oraft.
With respect to release of former Allied public merchant
or privately owned merchant vessels of 5oo gross tons and over.

suBJEcT: Division of Japanese Fleet.
PARTiciPAnTs: Mr. Graves, Counselor of British Embassy
Mr. Vincent - FE
Mr. Emmerson - JA

1-1493
Mr. Graves left an aide-mémoire on the subject of the
division of the Japanese fleet. He stated that his Government
was seeking assurance that the United States did not propose
to apply the same principle of four-way division to both the
merchant and naval fleets. With reference to division of the
merchant fleet he stated that it was the position of his
CS/A
Government that the Norwegian Government, as well as such
countries as Denmark, Greece, Yugoslavia and Egypt, should be
entitled to a share of the Japanese merchant fleet. He had
been instructed to sound out the Department as to whether
we intended to limit the division of the merchant fleet to
countries which are members of the Far Eastern Commission.
Mr. Graves was assured that the United States Government
regarded the division of the Japanese naval and merchant fleets
as two distinct problems. While we were committed to a division
8
of the remaining combatant vessels of destroyer or lesser
6
tonnage, we have taken the position that division of the
merchant fleet depends upon decisions to be made by the Far
S
Eastern Commission on the subject of reparations and the future
9
economy of Japan. Mr. Vincent stated that we were therefore
agreed with the British position that the disposal of the
SEP-91946
merchant and fishing fleets should be undertaken by the Far
Eastern Commission.
1246
Mr. Graves
3

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Mr. Graves explained that his Government was of the
opinion that since losses in the Far Eastern war had been
taken into consideration in conneotion with the division
of the German fleet, that losses in the European war should
affect the final decision with regard to the Japanese merchant
fleet. The British Government had accepted this principle
at the request of the American Government and as a consequence
its share of German vessels was less than it would have been
otherwise. It was therefore felt that in settling claims
for Japanese ships one could not separate losses incurred
in Europe and in the Far East.
Mr. Vincent stated that it was his impression that once
the claims of the nations suffering the most losses had been
satisfied there would be relatively little to divide among such
powers as Greece or Egypt. Nevertheless, 1t seems a rational
assumption that nations not belonging to the Far Eastern Com-
mission would not be excluded from presenting claims. He
said that we would study this matter and would be able to
give him our views on another occasion.

Ir. Graves referred to the aide-mémoire of August 12, 1946
which he had left with the Department and to the Department's
(0
reply of September 3, and presented a memorandum on the subject
1
of principles for the division of the Japanese merchant and
246
fishing fleets.
Mr. Graves reiterated whet had been expressed in the
Embassy's note of August 12, namely, that the British Government
proposed that the allocation of Japanese merchant ships should
be made on the same basis as that adopted for the division of
German shipping. The principle used in the case of German
ships was an allocation in proportion to over-all losses on
a gross tonnage basis and regardless of whether losses occurred
in the European or the Far Eastern war.
Mr. Vincent inquired what would be the statistical result
C
of such an allocation in the case of Japan. Mr. Graves replied
S
FW 894.85/8.1246
that his Government did not know what division would result but
he felt that the British might receive more ships under this
20
principle than if the Far Eastern war alone were taken into
consideration. He said that by agreeing to the principle of
allocation

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.98)
12
-2-
allocation on the basis of both wars for the division of German
shipping, the British had received slightly less than would
otherwise have been the case. Mr. Graves said that the method
he was suggesting would permit allocation of shipping tonnage
to countries not directly participating in the Far Eastern
war, such as Egypt, Yugoslavia and others.
Mr. Vincent said that he was under the impression that
the agreement in connection with the German fleet was taken
without necessarily intending that the same principle should
be applied in the case of the Japanese fleet. He stated that
the British note would be referred to the appropriate divisions
in the State Department and a reply prepared for the British
Embassy.

ir. Graves' statement that the ritish had concurred in

not be allowed to reengege in whaling operations; permitting
them to do this sets a bad precedent. Furthermere, there is
no room for the Japanese in this industry. The British and the
Norwegians are of course vitally interested in whaling opera-
tione in the Antarotic and themselves have not enough ships
at the present time. Finally, the British are apprehensive at
the possibility that whale oil, bedly needed throughout the
world, will go to Japan. Mr. Graves asked fer assuranees that
allocation of such oil would be made by the IEfc (International
Emergenoy Food Counoll).
Mr. Graves was informed that the Department understood that
SCAP was permitting the Japanese to engage in sertain limited
whaling operations prinoipally for the purpose of supplying badly
needed food in Japan. SCAP had given assurances that all such
whaling operations would be carried on in striet complianee with
the terms of the International Conventions for the Regulation
of Whaling. Mr. Vincent said, however, that we would explore the
matter further and would be glad to disoues the question with
Mr. Graves at a later date.

AIDE HEMOIRE
In the State Department's Aide Memoire of the 30th
January it was stated that:
"With reference to the Japanese merchant and fish-
ing fleets, it is the view of the United States Govern-
ment that units of these fleets should be disposed of
in accordance with decisions of an Allied reparations
commission operating under general olicies laid dowm
by the Par Eastern Commission. It is assumed that this
question will be considered by the Commission in due
course."
2.
His Majesty's Government assume the agreement of the
United States Government in the understanding that the system
of allocation envisaged for the Japanese Naval fleet has no
application to the sharing of the Japanese Merchant and Fish-
ing fleet and that such disposition is not circumscribed by
the decision on the sharing of the Japanese Naval vessels.
They would however be grateful to learn that the position is
as stated herein.
3.
His Majesty's Government wish to add that, because of
their Merchant Shipping losses and of the role which their
Merchant Navy played in the war against Japan, the Norwegian
Government are, in the view of His Majesty's Wovernment, en-
titled to a greater share of the Japanese Merchant fleet than
are certain members of the Far Eastern Commission. There are
also other countries, notably Denmark, Greece, Yugoslavia and
Egypt, which lost tonnage at Japanese hands. In the light of
the foregoing His Majesty's Government draw the conclusion that
the/

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
the United States proposal quoted in paragraph 1 of this Aide
Memoire is not intended to limit the division of the Merehant
Pleet to those States which are members of the Far Eastern
Commission but only to provide & forum where the claims of all
States, whether members or not, will receive proper consider-
ation.
4.
Subject to confirmation by the State Department that
the foregoing is also the view of the United States Government,
His Majesty's Government agree in principle that the disposal
of the Japanese Merchant and Fishing fleets should be under-
taken by an Allied Reparations body operating under the general
policies laid down by the Par Eastern Comnission.

0-2546
principles which proved acceptable to the principal Mari-
time Nations and which, in their opinion, would be fair
and proper in relation to the allocation of Japanese
tonnage. The basis on which the Inter-Allied Reparations
Agency worked was as follows:-
(a) that the allocation of ships should be in pro-
portion to the overall losses of Merchant Shipping
by acts of war, on a gross tonnage basis of the
countries participating, whether those losses
occurred in the European or the Far Eastern war.
(b) that the allocation should take account of the
quality, age and type of these losses so that,
CS
so far as practicable, countries received like
for like.
594.85

method of distinguishing between losses in the two wars
and also because it provided a fair measure of the contribu-
5
10.254
tion of each country to the war at sea.

The Secretary of State refers to the Politioal Adviser's
despatch, No. 791, of December 30, 1946, subject: Restitution
of S. S. Hirondelle.
According to the information furnished the Department by
the Maritime Commission, the vessel in question was of Ameri-
can registry until March 28, 1941. She was then transferred
to Philippine registry, having been acquired by Vicente Madrigal,
Manila, and taken to the Philippines. The vessel sailed to
HongKong from Manila, arriving in October 194l, and mas seised
in HongKong at the time of Japanese occupation of that coleny.
The Department is, therefore, of the opinion that the vessel
should now be treated as Philippine, regardless of the Japanese
Government's report that the veseel was flying the American
flag when seised. However, should the Political Adviser feel
that there is sufficient importance to the Japanese Govern-
ment's report, the case should be reviewed.
If no further review is necessary, the request for claim
for restitution should be transmitted to the representative
of the Philippine Republic attached to SCAP's staff.

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.95)
ADXP
DIECHMSION
1. Merchamt shipping represemts a category of reparations asset of
special and particular interest to claimant countries, both because of
large losses of suoh shipping suffered during the war and of the importamce
of shipping in the imediate post war revival of trade and indestry. For
these reasons, available German marehant shipping has been distributed in
proportion to losses of shipping, rather than as a part of the gemeral re-
paratioms poel. It is believed that Japanese merchant shipping similerly
is of such importemce to claimante as to warrant special distributiom apart
from general Japanese assets.
2. In the division of German reparations approved by IARA, it was
finally decided to distribute merchant shipping in proportion to total
merohant shipping lesses inourred in the war without respect to theatre.
At that time, the Umited States specifically entered on the record a
statement that it did not consider this decision necessarily to be a
precedent for Japanese reparations.
3. In an Aide-Memoire, dated August 12, 1946, the British Government.
requested that Japanese merchant shipping be distributed in proportion to
total losses, as was dome in the cass of Germany.
4. Present estimates are that the raximm tonnage of Japanese merchant
shipping available for reparations will be very small. Although statisti-
cally, adoption of the British recommendation would inorease the claims base
of the U.K., Norway, and other nations, and would parmit the presentation of
claims by nations which inourred no shipping losses in the war with Japen,
the effect of such additional claims against the small tonnage available
would not increase substantially the actual tonnage assigned to those
countries.
5. In contrast with the small advamtage to Britain and other comtries
with large Atlantic losses which would result from the adoption of the
British proposal, is the possible great disadvantage of opening the door to
the presentation of claims to othor rep ratione goods on a total loss basis.
- 5-

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.95)
If Russia, fer exsmple, were to present claims fer industrial fesilities
on a tetal loss basis, the distribatien of Japamese reparatiams desired by
the U. S. migtt be cempletely upeet. Sinee it is alreedy kmomm that Mexice
will attempt to present claims on a total loss besis, and simee it is to the
ebvious advantage of others to de likewise, it is inadvisable to make any
eoncessions which might weaken the opposition of the U. S. to sush a basis
ofclaims.
6. Since total losses of merekemt shipping inourred in the waer with
Japan are far in excess of probable reperations in kind, it is highly um-
likely that any shipping will be left over for distribution in accerdanee
with general reparations shares.
7. Some diffioulty may be encountered in distinguishing shipping lesses
incurred in the war with Japan from losses resulting frem Germam or Italiam
action. While it is not believed that the preblem of identificatiom will
present major diffieulties, it is believed advisable to request the sub-
mission of claims by other coumtries at an early date, in order te afferd
them suffisient time fer preparation, as well as an edequate time for review.
8. The invitation to submit claime would be issued only to countries
at war with Japan, and only for the period of their belligerency. This
latter restriction is necessery to prevent, for instance, the presentatiom
of Prenoh claims for merchant ships lost as a result of Allied action in
Indo-China while engaged in transporting goods to Japan in support of the
Japanese wer effort.
9. It is desirable that, in the current consultations with other
comtries regarding Japanese reperetions, as wide agreement as possible
be secured regarding the specific settlement of the merchant shipping
problom.

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
ENCLOSURE
STATEMENT OF U. S. POSITION WITH RESPECT TO
REPARATIONS CLAIMS TO JAPANESE MERCHANT 3HIPPING
Report by the
State-War-Navy Coordinating Subcommittee for the Far East
THE PROBLEM
1. To formulate the U. S. position with respect to:
a. Determination of the basis of reparations claims for
Japanese merchant shipping to be recognized in any repara-
tions settlement.
b. Establishment of procedures for reviewing and ap-
proving such claims.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. See Appendix "A".
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
4. It is concluded that:
a. No final determination can now be made of the amount
of Japanese merchant shipping, if any, that may be declared
available for distribution as reparations.
b.
Existing merchant ships of Japanese registry which
are declared to be available as reparations should be dis-
tributed among reparations recipient countries in proportion
to proven losses of merchant shipping incurred during
their respective periods of belligerency in the war against
Japan, and as a result of participation in action against
Japan, up to and no more than the total value of such losses.
c.Any existing merchant ships declared available as
reparations from Japan but not distributed under b above
should be distributed according to the same percentages as
those computed for industrial facilities.
SWNCC 236/42
381

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.96)
CONFIDENTIAL
a.
The Secretary of State, after determination of
the amount, if any, of Japanese merohant shipping which may
be made available for reparations, should invite countries
at war with Japan who suffered merchant shipping losses as
defined in b above to submit claims for reparations from
Japanese merchant shipping based on such losses.
e. The Secretary of Stete, through the FEC, or through
such other channels as desired, should invite the views and
attempt to secure the agreement of other nations eligible
under b above concerned regarding the methods for deter-
mination of reparations from available Japanese merchant
shipping, called for in b, c, and d above.
RECOMMENDATIONS
5. It is recommended that:
B。
This report be transmitted to the JCs for comment
from the military point of view.
b. The SWiCC approve the conclusions in paragraph 4
above.
1.
After approval by the SWNCC, the paper be trans-
mitted to the Secrtary of State for implementation.

APPENDIX "A"
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. Basic general policy to be adhered to by the United
States with respect to reparetions from Japan has been set forth
in paragraph 1 of the Potsdam Declaration:
"Jepan shall be permitted to maintain such industries
as will sustain her economy and permit the exaction of just
reparations in kind, but not those which would enable her
to rearm for wer....
2. SWNCC 52/7, Basic Directive for Post-Surrender Military
Government in Japan Proper, provides in Section III, that it shall
be a policy of the United States:
"C. To execute such program of reparations end restitu-
tion as may be decided upon by the appropriate Allied
authorities."
The SCAP is thereby directed as follows:
"28. You will assure the execution of programs of
repar tions in kind and of restitution of identifiable
looted property in acccrdance with decisions of the approp-
ricte Allied authorities transmitted to you by the Joint
Chiefs of Staff. Reperations will be accomplished;
"a. Through the transfer of Jepanese property loc
ated outside of the territories to be retained by Japan;
"b. Through the transfer from Jepan of goods,
existing plant equipment, and f&cilities that are not
necessary to the operation of a pecceful Jap nese
econony, or the supplying of the occupying forces.
"Ail requests received by you, for reparstions or
restitution from the United Nations which ve been victims
of Jap nese aggrescion will be reported with your recom-
mendations to the Joint Chiefs of Staff."
3. SWNCC 150/7/D, Post-Surrerder P-licy for Jepan, and
corrigenda as provided ir SWicc 150/8, refers to the general
problem of rep rations and its conrection with occupation costs
as follows:
"b. Through the transfer of such goods or capital
equipment and facilities :s should under policies set by
Allied authorities, be made available for this purpose.
No form of reparation shall be exected which will interfere
with or prejudice the program for Jepan's demilitarization,
defraying the cost of the occupation end the maintenence of
a minimum civilian stand:rd f living."

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
é
CONFIDENTIAL
APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. Merchant shipping represcnts a category of reparations
asset of special and particular interest to claimant countries,
both because of large losses of such shipping suffered during
the war and of the importance of shipping in the immediate post
war revival of trade and industry.
For these reasons, available
German merchant shipping has been distributed in proportion to
losses f shipping, rather than as a part of the general repara-
tions pool.
It is believed that Japanese merchant shipping
similarly is of such importance to claimants as to warrant
special distribution apart from general Japanese assets.
2.
In the division of German reparations approved by IARA,
it was finally decided to distribute merchant shipping in pro-
portion to total merchant shipping losses incurred in the war
without respect to theatre. At that time, the United States
specifically entered on the record a statement that it did not

asily realize, therefore, that Japan's return to international
commerce will surely meet with immense obstruction in the field
of sea transportation. It will be impossible for us to meet

report, prepared by Overseas Consultants, headed by Mr. Clifford
Strike, recommended that Japan be permitted to "retain a new
ship construction capacity of 400,O00 tons annually to build up
her merchant fleet to a minimum aggregate tonnage of 4,000,000"
and to retain a drydock capacity sufficient to service this
fleet. This is a most gratifying step for Japan.
The United States of America which possesses the
ingredients of power and the willingness to use economic res-
ources, in support of international policy, may eventually
decide to take measures in putting dollars back into foreign
circulation in order to alleviate the one-sided world trade and
balance the growing disparity. The really important moves in
that direction must be the spending of American tourists abroad,
and the sending of capital abroad in the form of foreign inves-
tments. But if the United States could return some of its ocean
trade to foreign bottoms, and pay for the service in dollars,
much might be done to the advantage of most countries in finding
their chief source of dollars to save their slim dollar assets.
As for Japan, her national economy cannot possibly

an adverse trade balance. In order that she may restore her
commercial normalcy and develop her international receipts,
Japan should have to try every conceivable means to get the
maximized operation of avairable way and to the best advantage.
It is for this, and this al one, that we beg to present to you
our statement on the future of Japanese merchant marine for
your kind consideration.

SOME OBSERVATIONS CN
THE FUTURE CF THE JAPANESE MERCHANT MARINE
By Aumekichi Nakajime,
resident, Japan oreign Trade Association.
FART I. LEVEI. OF THL JAPANESE SHIPPING
A U.F. despatoh, dated eshington, ebruary 12, reported
that the Far Eastern Commission would, according to an informed
official, set the level of shipping to be permitted Japan 'some-.
what below' the 3,ooc,Ccc gross tons, which had been consistently
advocated by the United States. The same report said that there
vas a virtual agreement on 1, soc,Ccc tons of shipping for the
coastwise trade; but the nited States insistence on 1,50c,000
additioral tons for oversees services vas opposed by most of the
other ten nations, which believed that 50o,ccc tons woula be
sufficient for this purpose. h compromise between 2,ccc,Cco and
,cCc,Coc tons for the entire Japanese shipping appeared likely,
according to the press report, with s good possibility that it
would be ,50c,c0c gross tons.
assuming for the moment that the foregoing report gives an
accurate account of what is going on in the Ec., I wish to be
allowed to offer a few observetions on this problem, in the belief
that the probable compromise figure of 2,500,000 tons, certainly
the lower limit of ,cC0,ccc tons, vould seriously impede the
economic recover of Japen, and would not be consonant with the
basic Allied policy. Indeed it would not be an over-statement
to say that even the United tates proposal of 3,ccc,coc tons,
while far more realistic than other levels mentioned, would still

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.95)
- 2 -
fall short of achieving the objective of making this country
self-supporting economically at an early date.
Iet it be recslled at the outset that Japan had in 1930-34
approximately 4,000,000 gross tons of shipping, exolusive of
fishing vessels, wooden ships, and other miscellaneous oraft,
when her population, not including the Koreans and Formosans,
was around 60,000,000. During these years, about 60,000,000
tons of cargoes of all descriptions were transported annually
by coastwise shipping, while export shipments averaged some
9,000,000 tons per annum, es against about 27,000,000 tons of
imports that moved yearly.
By projecting these facts and figures into the future,
it has been caloulated that the nation of 32,500,000 souls,
which will be reached before 1955, will require not less than
1,200,00c tons of shipping for her coastwise trede, and a little
over 3,200,000 tons of vessels for overseas services, or the
total of some 4,500,000 tons of merchant shipping, if she is
to achieve an economic stebility on the living standard of the
years 1930-34.

SIR:
letter dated May 3, 1947, addressed to the United States
Maritime Commission by its Regional Director at Yokohama
concerning the employment of Japanese Shipping Control Ad-
ministration (SCAJAP) vessels in Japanese trade with foreign
countries and for shipment of GARIOA (Army Relief in Occu-
pied Areas) cargoes, together with a copy of confidential
radio WARX 97574 from the Department of the Army (TCWTSOT
280) to the Commander-in-Chief, Far East, information, Com-
00
800.8870
manding General Eighth Army, and a copy of confidential
6
radio no. 674 of March 16, 1948, to the United States Mari-
4
time Commission, Washington, from its Regional Director at
Yokohama on the same subject.
08
5
The problem set forth in those communications apparent-
ly arises from the Army desire to utilize SCAJAP vessels
33
as fully as possible in Japanese trade in order to curtail
dollar expenditures required for employment of American
2
vessels. According to the Regional Director, the employment
348
of Maritime Commission vessels on loan to SCAJAP is subject
to the concurrence of the Maritime Commission which has not
approved their use in overseas trade, and therefore the
Army plans to employ the Maritime Commission vessels in
Japanese coastal trade in order to free Japanese vessels
for foreign trade. The Regional Director states that under
these conditions it is impossible for American lines and
vessels to compete for cargo that can be carried by SCAJAP
vessels, and that it therefore results in Japanese vessels
resuming foreign trade under non-competitive conditions and
to the detriment of American shipping interests.
The enclosed radio from the Department of the Army, it
is understood, is in reply to a radio with regard to attempts
to arrange space at Manila for shipments from South-East Asia
to Japan. It is enclosed because there is also set forth in
paragraph (C) of Part 2 thereof the position of the Depart-
ment of the Army with regard to giving priority to SCAJAP
vessels for the movement of cargo from abroad.

UNITED STATES MARITIME COMMISSION
APO 343
CONFIDENTIAL - SECRET
Yokohama, Japan
May 3, 1947
Letter No. W-441
Mr. Huntington T. Morse
Special Assistant to the Commission
U. S. Maritime Commission
Washington 25, D. C.
Dear Mr. Morse:
This will confirm our message #275 of April 29th to
Mr. Helmbold, copy attached, which was sent in view of immi-
nent developments which wili affect these and other phases
of transportation in and around Japan. Could not communicate
full details as Tokyo's staff study is still in classified
status, but the projected ideas will certainly be very dis-
ruptive of established customs and practices and generally
inimical to the operators of American shipping, even contrary
to national policies possibly, that we feel we must alert you
more fully.
The predominant line of thinking in the highest quarters
in Tokyo today is exactly this with respect to commercial
cargoes:
1st Priority - SCAJAP tonnage American owned and Korean
Baltic Coasters
2nd Priority - SCAJAP shipping Japanese owned
3rd Priority - Army Transport Vessels
4th Priority - Wold Charter Market, New York
5th Priority - Any cut-rate operator
6th Priority - Conference lines
The reason given by Tokyo for the authority or basis for
this line of effort, which disregards all our protests and
advice, is that the War Department have instructed all SCAP
departments to exercise the utmost economy. Whether or not
there still remains proponents for the rehabilitation of Japan-
ese world wide shipping in foreign trade, is difficult to
trace. The fact remains that schemes and plans of all sorts,
some practical, others visionary and fantastic, are emanating
daily from SCAp and unfortunately, being given serious con-
sideration, which strongly point in that direction.
/This
CONFIDENTIAL
Senet

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
Senet
CONFIDENTIAL
Page 2 of Enclosure No. 1 to
Despatch No. 49 of March 23, 1948,
from Yokohama, Japan
This economy in the Army's budget is one thing. When
it is directed to the increase or augmentation of the credit
side of SCAP's Foreign Trade Branch, it would seem to be
another. Tokyo contend that the enormous amount of food-
stuffs being imported for the Japanese, must be balanced by
exports and any other revenue that can be concocted. One
source of revenue is definitely considered to be obtainable
from private or commercial American shipping through rate-
cutting. We contend that the imports and allocations of raw
materials and foodstuffs, is for purposes entirely separate
from commercial transactions, that it is a political gesture
which requires that the Japanese become self-supporting for
high level long range strategic reasons. The balancing of
imports and exports is and will be a physical impossibility
for years to come, in any event there is no sound basis for
reasoning that the two should be considered together.
As and when you are consulted in the matter which may
easily be quite soon, trust you will scrutinize all proposals
very thoroughly and be fully on your guard against plausible
arguments that should not be taken at face values. We can see
no place for considering Japanese domestic economy at the ex-
pense of American and Allied shipping, nor any reason why the
Army, while trying to make the Japanese self-sufficient, ig-
nore principles of sound finance or business.
It occurs to us that if the Army's proposals are as we
believe them to be, they may have to be brought to the cog-
nisance of the Far East Commission and the Department of
State. In any event, the British will doubtless solicit
your support as the Army's plans will affect their coastal
services drastically.
This study when forwarded to the War Department should
precipitate a show-down. It would seem to us a most opportune
time to not only indicate to the Army what they can and cannot
do with respect to commercial and similar cargoes, but to at-
tempt to draw up certain procedures and understandings which
all shipping should recognize and live up to; lines of demarka-
tion of ocean and coastal; conference rights and the innumer-
able problems that will arise from time to time due to traffic
being largely one-way and trade in general still undergoing
growing pains.
Please treat this letter as classified until we advise
you by signal that classification is lifted.
Very truly yours,
R. E. Norris, Captain, USMS
Regional Director

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.96)

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TRC - Mr. W. A. Radius
TO
DATE: April 21, 1948
FROM : OE - Mr. R. H. Vhitman
sUBJECT: Japanese Shipbuilding and Merchent Shipping
894.642
Present U. S. policy (SVNCC 236/43) sets a 1evel of 150,000
gross tons arnuelly for Japanese shipbuilding capacity. This posi-
tion was based on security conciderations which can no longer be
considered valid. The Japanese fleet non-operable or readily
repairable is only 1,300,000. Most of it is inefficient war time
production. Japan needs at least 3,000,000 tons to avoid a net
loss on shipping account. Thus a 300,000 ton capacity figure
which we are now recommending seems easily justified on economic
grounds. Under existing Far Eastern Commission policy, the level
894.85
to be currently fixed would be a ceiling only until the end of the
present phase of the occupation or Oct. 1, 1949, whichever is the
earlier. If, therefore, the level fixed is lower than required
by the Jepanese to meet their minimum peaceful needs, uneconomical
rebuilding of shipbuilding capacity would be required within a
very short period.
Under existing U. S. policy, 100,000 gross tons or more of
Japanese ship ing would become available for reparations on the
2148
basis of the individusl ships' exceeding the size and speed limi-
tations (6000 gross tons and 15 kmots) regardless of the present
size of the Japanese fleet. As in the case of shipbuilding, this
policy was based on security considerations which no longer appear
valid. Janan has urgent current need for all her existing merchant
vessels to assist in the revival of the Japanese economy. We there-
C
fore reconmend that none of the present merchant fleet be made avail-
able for reparation removals.
F
PHDhitma
894.85
DC/R
SEOR
Rev.
4.2148

"Japanese Shipping" prepared by the Japanese Ministry of Foreign
894.85/5-2948
Affairs and published in March of 19l8, concerning the present
condition of Japanese shipping and the necessity for the early
establishment of an ocean-going merchant marine together with an
estimate of requirements.
Takiny the years 1930-1934 as a representative peri, the
A
Japanese Ministry of Foreign Affairs estimates that about 5 perm
cent of Japan's foreign trade tonnage was carried in Japanese
ships plying the world's sea lanes. During this same period, net
receipts from marine transportation almost equaled or exceeded the
deficit in commodity trade created by an excess of imports over -
ports. In addition, the importance of shipping as a coastwise and
FE
inter-island means of communication and transportation is ressed,
the estimate being made that, during these same years, 20,000,000
tons of cargo were carried between Japanese ports. Furthermore,
about one million persons, includin families, depended directly
or indirectly upon the shippin industry for their income.
TRe
As is indicated in the enclosed brochure, Japanese shipping
has dropped rom the peak of 6,329, 000 tons in 1941 (not includ-
OE
ing fishing vessels and special craft such as dredgers and tugs)
ED
to 1,540,000 tons at present, most of which is under the opera-
tional control of a semi-governmental body, the Civilian Merchant
CS/A
Marine Committee. Approximately 66 per cent of the existing ships
DC/R
are of wartime design and are considered to be inefficient and un-
economical by commercial standards, while the remainder have an
average age of 22 years. Internal transportation needs cannot be
met, it is stated, by the 10,000,00) ton per year carrying capaci-
ty of ships now held by Japan. The point is made that,as a re-
sult of the dissolution of the Zaibatsu and the passage of anti-
trust measures following the war, Japanese shipping is entering
upon a new era under new leadership and upon a new basis. Fur-
thermore, in the opinion of the Japanese Yinistry of Foreign Af-
894.85
fairs, the provisions of the Seaman's Law, enacted in 1947, are
even more favorable to the worker than those of the Internation-
al Convention on Haritime Labor.
AUG 16 1948
With
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No. 313
May 29, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-2-
With respect to the future, the position is taken in the en-
closed study that Japan cannot rely upon foreign ships alone to
carry needed imports, which, it is estimated, will amount to about
32,000,000 freight tons by 1955. The first reason given is that it
would cost too much. y way of illustration, since approximately
20,000,000 tons of such imports will have to be brought from dis-
tant points, it is estimated that freight charges will amount to
about 400,000,000 (on the basis of 20.00 per ton average). This,
taken in conjunction with Japan's prolable excess of imports over
exports, makes even more dim the hope of restoring equilibrium to
Japan's international balance of trade and payments. Furthermore,
to rely solely upon forein bottoms would expose Japan's foreign
trade to interruntion and possible suspension, and would make it
difficult for the country to project effectively or conduct effi-
ciently its foreign trade. As a consequence, the Japanese Minis-
try of Foreign Affairs is of the opinion that Japan will be per-
mitted to ovm and operate such shipping as will permit its foreign
trade to be conducted econonically and free of external interference.
Exclusive of fishin vessels, the estimate is made that Japan
will require a total of 4, 030,000 ross tons, of which 2,870,000
tons represent foreign routes shipping, 870,o00 tons domestic ship-
ping, and 690,000 tons miscellaneous shipping (includes railroad
ferries, dredgers and tugs). The requirement stated envisages car-
o ships of moderate speed and size, not exceeding 10,000 gross
tons, with the exception of a few passenger and cargo ships of
from 2000 to 5000 tons for trade with China and other neighboring
countries. With respect to the extent to which Japanese shipping
should participate in Japan's foreign trade, the Japanese Ministry
of Foreign Affairs is of the opinion that it should be 50 per cent
where the exporting nations are maritine nations, 90 per cent in
the case of Korea and Sakalin, and 8o per cent in the case of all
other non-maritime nations. In this connection, Japan's need for
adequate shipbuilding and repair facilities is pointed out, as well
as the inadequacy of existing shipyards, which, it is stated, would
not suffice for repair work alone. Although recognizing the desir-
ability of attaining an annual shiphuilding capacity of approximate-
ly six per cent of Japan's total tonnage, early realization of such
an objective is not anticipated by the Japanese Ministry of Foreign
Affairs.
The views of this lission regarding Japan's shipping needs
and the manner in which Japan's merchant marine is being administer-
ed at the present time, will be submitted to the Department for its
consideration upon the completion of a definitive study of those
problems currently in process of preparation.
Respectfully yours,
h.VBecaee.
W. J. Sebald
Acting Political Adviser

Oliver Zi on Japanese Shipping
Ta Kung Pao (Iniependont, Political Sojenca Croup). Shanghai
July 16. 1943
of the China Mercharts Steem Navigation Company, who was sent to Tolyo on
July 5 to study Japanese shipping and to take charge of the inauguration of
the Tokyo branch office of the Company;, returned here by plane yesterday
afternoon. He told our reporter the following facts:
1) Japanese shipping: Japan's shipyards have sterted building 28 new
vessels with a total tonnage of more than 5,600 tons for domestic uses.
Japan now possesses 1, 300,000 tons of shipping bottoms and she is continuing
the building of new vessels.
2) Japanese fishery: Japan possesses a large number of fishing boats
which are sufricjent to mect her domestic needs. Hovever, various shipyards
are still busy building now fishing boats which will soon be put to use,
The Government should pay close attention to the trespassing on /territorial
vaters by Japanese fishing boats, and should take effective steps to prevent
them from doing so.
3) Japan's shipping reperations; Though Japan possesses 1, 300,000 tons
of shipping bottoms, yet judging by the present situation, the existing ship-
ping bottoms are still insufficient to meet her needs, so the demand that
Japan should surrender to China 500,000 tons of reparations ships vill not be
easily met with. Honce, it needs greater efforts on the part of the diplomatic
authorities to achieve this end.
4) Ordering of new vessels from Japan: In vien of the present shortage
of passenger boats in China and of the faot that it would take too much time
to order new ships from European and American countries, the China Merchants
Steam Navigation Company has proposed to SCAP orders for four up-to-date
passenger boats with a tonnage of 5, O00 tons each, In principle, SC AP accepte
the proposal but suggested that Hainan iron ores be shipped to Japan in
exchange for new vessels. Therefore, a final decision cannot be made until
after consultations with Premier Wong Wen-hao in this regard.
5) Sino-Japanese trade: Businessmen of various nationalities in Japan
such as those from America and Hongkong have their orm organization. Further-
more, their governments are doing all they can to help their traders. As for
our traders in Japan, they do not have much influence and the Government has
given them very little aid. That is more, the business of the Chinese traders
there has been greatly affected by the debt of US10,000,000 whioh the
Central Trust has owed Japan. Therefore, representatives of Chinese traders
in Japan have asked Zi to carry for them letters to the All-China Federation
of Chambers of Commerce and the Shanghai City Chamber of Commerce, request-
ing them to petition the Government to immediately improve the existing policy
of trade vith Japan so as to help promote the existing trade relations with
that country.
