(SCIPIN - 677)
29 January 1946
MEMORANDUM FOR:
IMPERIAL JAPANESE GOVERNMENT.
THROUGH:
Central Liaison Office, Tokyo.
SUBJECT:
Governmental and Administrative Separation of
Certain Outlying Areas from Japan.
1. The Imperial Japanese Government is directed to cease
exercising, or ttempting to exercise, governmental or edminis-
trative authority over any area outside of Japan, or over any
government officials and employees or any other persons within
such areas.
2. Except as authorized by this Headauarters, the Imperial
Japanese Government will not communicate with government offisials
and employees or with eny other persons outside of Japan for any
purpose other than the routine operetion of authorized shipping,
communications and weather services.
3. For the purpose of this directive, Japan is defined to
include the four main islands of Japan (Hokkaido, Honsnu,
Kyusnu, and Shikoku) and tne approximately 1,O0o smaller adjacent
islands, including the Tsushima Islands and the Ryukyu (Nansei)
Islands north of 300 Nortn Latitude (excluding Kuchinoshima
Island); and excluding (a) Utsuryo (Ullung) Island, Liancourt
Rocks (Take Island) and Juelpart (Saishu or Cheju) Island, (b)
the Ryukyu (Nansei) Islands south of 30° North Latitude (inclu-
ding Kuchinoshima Island), the Izu, Nanpo, Bonin (Ogasawcra) and
Volcano (Kazan or Iwo) Island Groups, and all other outlving
Pecific Islands / including the Daito (Chigashi or Oag ri) Io-
land Group, and Parece Vela (Okino-tori), Marcus (Min n:-tori)
and Ganges (Nakano-tori) Islands/, and (c) the Furile (Chishima)
Islands, the Habomai (Napomaze) Island Group (including Suishc,
Yuri, Akiyuri, Chibotsu and Taraku Islands) and Chikotan Island.
4. Further areas specifically excluded from the govern-
mental and administrative jurisdiction of the Imperial Japanese
Government are the following: (a) all Pacific Islands seized
or occupied under mandate or otherwise by Japan since the be-
ginning of the World War in 1914, (b) Manc uria, Formosa and the
Tescadores, (c) Korea, dnd (d) Karafuto.
5. The definition of Japan contained in this directive
shall also apply to all future directives, memoranda and orders
from this Headquarters unless otherwise specified therein.
6. Nothing in this directive shall be construed as an in-
dication of Allied policy relating to the ultimate determination
of the minor islands referred to in Article 8 of the Potsdam
Declaration.
7. The Imperial Japanese Government will prepare and submit
to this Headquarters a report of all governmental agencies in
Jepan the functions of which pertain to ereas outside of Japan
as defined in this directive. Such report will include a state-
ment of the functions, organization and personnel of each of the
agencies concerned.
8. All records of tne agencies referred to in paragraph 7
above will be preserved and kept available for inspection by this
Headquarters.
FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDERP

pair of their ships. There are also enclosed copies of this Head-
quarters' Memorandum of September 2, 1948 to the Japanese Government
(SCAPIN 1931), directing that lovernment to effect the transfer of
responsibilities referred to above.
At the end of the war, privately-owmed merchant vessels were,
and during the past three years have remained, under the control
and operation of the Japanese Government on a bareboat charter
basis whereby the Government assumes all responsibility for the
operation and maintenance of the vessels. Under this arrangement,
Japanese shipowners were in the position of landlords receiving
A/MJS
nominal rentals barely sufficient to retain their bookkeepers to
record rental receipts or to maintain organizations capable of per-
forming all the functions of the ship-owning and ship-operating
branches of the industry. Shipowners, as a result,have been re-
DENTIAL FILDOV
duced to financial impotency and their organizations weakened to
the extent that the ultimate recovery of the industry will be both
difficult of achievement and time-consuming.
According to the directive handed to the Japanese Government
by this Headquarters, referred to above, privately-owned merchant
vessels will remain on a time charter basis to the Civilian Mer-
chant Marine Committee (Clac). The latter is an agency of the
Japanese Government created after the surrender to continue the
wartime control and operation of privately-owned vessels on a bare
boat charterage basis, under the supervision and direction of the
Shipping Control Authority of the United States Navy for the Japa
5
nese Merchant Marine (SCAJAP). Under the terms of the directive,
SCAJAP will continue to control the utilization of merchant vessel
specifying
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)

September 9, 1948
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
specifying assignments and issuing sailing orders.
The change-over, according to the press release, is designed
to introduce the incentive of private operation in order to utilize
more effectively the existing merchant fleet of some 1,367,200
gross tons and to effect budgetary economies. Under the new ar-
rangement, the owmer is paid only while the vessel is operating.
This step has been made possible, it is further stated in the re-
lease, since the purge of shipping executives, dissolution of ship
control associations and Zaibatsu holding companies has now been
completed.
The decision by General Headquarters to effect the change-over
referred to above is predicated upon a staff study of more than a
3/
year ago, dated May 2?, 1947, copies of which are enclosed, pre-
pared by the Civil Transportation Section of this Headquarters
reconmending that such action be taken on the ground that "suffi-
cient incentive for private enterprise must be established to cre-
ate competitive efficiency for the fullest utilization of vessels
in the Japanese Herchant  arine today." Although recommending the
return of Japanese merchant ships to private enterprise, the Civil
Transportation Section has taken the position that certain interim
government controls must be maintained such as the central booking
V
of all cargoes under planned transportation programs and the cen-
tral allocation of all time chartered ships in order to obtain
maximum utilization of bottoms.
In recommending the transition to time charterage, in May of
1947, the staff study pointed out that the operation of merchant
vessels on a bareboat charter basis by the cilC was undesirable
for the following reasons:
"a.
Poor government control and operation of vessels.
b.
There is no incentive for efficient operation.
C.
The organization is large and unwieldy.
d.
Steamship operators are idly standing by trying to
hold organizations together for the day when they
can again operate their own vessels.
e.
With the exception of a few directors, the best
skill of the industry is not vested in the present
CIC.
f.
There is no competition."
It is visualized by the Civil Transportation Section that the CiMC
eventually will be dissolved and its records transferred to the
General Maritime Eureau of the Japanese linistry of Transportation.
It is of interest to note that at the time the Civil Transporta-
tion Section prepared its recommendations on the decontrol of the
Japanese merchant marine, their findings indicated that the private
shipping companies were in difficult financial straits, and that the
return to private operation would require a "phased de-control" due
to the weakened position of the shipping companies. Continuation
of some forn of subsidy would be required, it was pointed out, in
order to adjust marine shipping rates more nearly in accord with
operating and construction costs, rail rates, and current price
levels.

levels. In this connection, it is of interest to note that at the time
of the June 30, 1948 revision of the marine freight rates by the C1MC,
which resulted in approximately a three-fold advance in then existent

With respect to ship  ersonnel, he press release states that the
shipowners may now say who shall work on their ships. However, we be-
lieve that this statement should he Talified to the extent that the
position and strength of the maritine union should not be overlooked.
In this conneetion, the above-mentioned staff study points out that
when the transition is made from Cmc to private enterprise, labor diffi-
culties will be encountered since the number of employees both at sea
and ashore is far in excess of requirements for the existing fleet.
Hence, the "disposition of this surplus personnel must be made without
incitin labor unrest or overburdening the shipowmers who cannot afford
to carry such surplus on co pany payrolls in their present financial
condition." The recommendation is made in the staff study that as soon
as decontrol is initiated, a 15 per cent surplus of seamen and officers
should be allowed each company, the balance being: transferred to the
rolls of the Ceneral Yaritime Pureau for ultimate disposition. The
Civil Transportation Sect ion has taken the position in this regard that
it considers inadvisahle the allocation of surplus seamen to the various
companies and the "waterin" of time charter rates to cover them.
In the opinion f this Yission it is too early as yet to determine
what effect the Japanese Mariners' Law (effective September 1, 1947),
14/
copies of whieh are enclosed, will have upon private shipping costs. To
mention lut a few of the orovisions, the Law provides for an 8-hour day,
48-hour week at sea for officers and ratings in the deck and en gine room
departments whose time is not divided into watches. Nor can a mariner
under 18 years of age or a female mariner do any work between 8 p.m. and
5 a.m. Provision has been made for overtime pay. To date this has pre-
sented no problem because of the large crews carried by merchant ships
(average surplus carried is estimated at 20 per cent). Furthermore, the
act states that every mariner is entitled to 25 days off with pay after
every year of continuous service plus 5 additional days for every addi-
tional three months of continuous service. However, seamen on coastwise
ships trading exclusively hetween domestic ports are entitled to 12 paid
days off in a year, and two additional days for every three months over
one year. Although the standards set by the ariners' Law may be con-
sidered as a desirable objeetive, we doult whether the Japanese Shipping
industry can now or in the near futur support a labor standard as high
as that established unless heavily subsidized.

one qualification was injected by the Civil Transportation Section.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS

Press Release:
2 September 1948
SCAP AUTHORIZED LIHITED PRIVATE OPERATION OF JAPANESE SHIPPING
Today, on the thiri anniversary of the surrender a directive
handed to the Japanese Bovernnent marked another milestone of prog-
ress in the transfer hack to private industry of responsibilities
assumed by the Government in time of war. The latest directive to
the Japanese hovernment permits shipovmers to again have a voice in
the operation of their own vessels.
As war in dapan pre ressed, shipouners were forced to relin-
quish progressively their rights to operate and husband their own
vessels. In 19h? the Japanese tovernment took over the manning of
ships and in Hovember 19hlt the repair and supplying of ships was
likewise transferred. Finally, in Hay 1945, through the Shipping
Control Association, the last vestige of industry operation was
lost when the Government assumed complete control over utilization
of all vessels.
According to SCAP's ivil Transportation Section, the directive
released this date, returns to the shipovmers, for the first time in
six years, the right to say who shall work on the ships and how re-
pairs shall be effected. Under the directive, the manning, supply-
ing, maintenance and repair of privately owned vessels, once more
become the responsibility of the individual shipowmer. The vessels
remain on a time charter basis to the Japanese Civilian Herchant
Larine Committee. Hovever, control of ve sel utilization, includ-
ing specifying vessel assignment and issuance of sailing orders,
remains with Occupation Porees through the Administrator Shipping
Control. Authority for Japan (SCAJAP).
The changes initiated are designed to introduce the incentive
of private operation of merchant vessels in order to more effi-
ciently utilize the xisting fl et for the benefit of the national
economy and to effect economies in the Japanese Fudget. The intro-
duction of private incentive will more fully utilize the skills and
capabilities of the shippin: industry and should increase the lift-
ing capacity of the fleet.
Only after the removal of concentrated econonic power as re-
quired by the Potsdam Declaration was the transfer of limited respon-
sibility to private owners possible. The purge of shipping execu-
tives, dissolution of Zaibatsu holding companies with subsequent
transfer of shares to the Japanese liquidation agency have now been
completed.
The directive
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 1 to
Tokyo:s No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-2-
The directive requires a change in the contractual arrangement
between the Japanese Government and the shipowmers. At the present
time, vessels are controlled by the Japanese Government on a bare
hoat charterage basis whereby the Government assumes all responsi-
bility for operation and maintenance of the vessel. By this arrange-
ment a nominal fee is paid the owner regardless of whether or not
his vessel is operating. The new time charterage arrangement for
carg vessels places on the owner the responsibility to crew, repair
and maintain the vessel and the owmer is paid only while the vessel
is operating. Each individual shipowner, therefore, has an incentive
to keep his ship in operating condition.
Miscellaneous ves els, fishing vessels, passenger ferries, and
vessels engaged in dredgin end similar special services, will be
under the full operational control of respective private owners.
Public vessels will be under the operationa control of respective
Japanese Ministries havin: jurisdiction.
Transferred back to private operations are limited responsi-
bilities for manning and repairing over 915 vessels of 1,367,200
gross tons. This is but a sad remnant of the once proud fleet of
2,71l: vessels of 6,327,000 ross tons which once made Japan the 3rd
'aritime Mation of the World. Japanes losses during the war of
3,129 vessels of 8,830,000 ross tons, attests the effectiveness
of Allied action against a common enemy.
Upon surrender, the Japanese Merchant Fleet was confined to
operations within the home waters. During the month of Oct. 1945,
a repatriation program returning Japanese citizens to Japan from
Far East areas was inaugurated in fulfillment of the Potsdam Decla-
ration. This proram is substantially completed, but a small per-
centage of the present lerchant Fleet is still utilized in this task.
Initially, vessels employed on repatriation constituted the
only Japanese-owned ships permitted to sail outside Japan. With the
introduction of the forein trade program, limited utilization of
Japanese-owned vessels was authorized. In each case where Japanese-
orned vessels proceed under SCAP authority beyond the boundaries of
the Ja anese home waters, they sail under a special flag, desig-
nated by the Supreme Commander or the Allied Powers. Return of
the Japanese flag to the high seas is a matter to be settled in the
Peace Treaty.

APO 500

STAFF STUDY
SUBJECT:
Return of Japanese Merchant Marine to private
Operation by phased-de-control from government
operation.
I. PROBLEM
1.
To remove government controls insofar as possible and
return Japanese Merchant ships to private enterprise. Such de-
Control to be carried out with the minimum interruption to the
industry. Sufficient incentive for private enterprise must be
established to create competitive efficiency for the fullestuti-
lization of vessels in Japanese Merchant Marine today.
II. FAGTORS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1.
Continuation of some form of subsidy is required as
marine shipping rates are not in proper adjustment to:
a.
Current price levels
b.
Rail rates
C.
Cost of shipping operation
d.
Cost of construction
2.
Financial position of private shipping companies is in
a choatic condition due to:
a.
War losses
b.
Cancellation of war indemnities
C.
Blocked accounts
d.
High cost and inferiority of war-built ships
3.
Steamship companies will require a phased de-control
due to their present weakened condition created by:
a.
Purge of shipping leaders.
b.
Liquidation of holding companies.
C.
Re-organization of restricted Zaibatsu concerns
and their subsidiaries.
d.
Release of shipping companies from "association
control" which completely dominated their companies.
e.
Inactivity of employees due to government control
and operation of vessels during the past few years.

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNC LASSIFIED
-3-
(COPY)
-2-
4. A transition of ship personnel must be made from the
present government Civilian Merchant Marine Committee (CMMC) to
the operators. This presents a serious problem as the number
of employees both at sea and ashore is far in excess of require-
ments for the existing fleet. Disposition of this surplus per-
sonnel must be made without inciting labor unrest or overburden-
ing the shipowners who cannot afford to carry such surplus on
company payrolls in their present financial condition.
5.
The present CMC operation is undersirable and should
be dissolved by phases because:
a.
Poor government control and operation of vessels.
b.
There is no incentive for efficient operation.
C.
The organization is large and unwieldy.
d.
Steamship operators are idly standing by trying
to hold organizations together for the day when
they can again operate their own vessels.
e.
With the exception of a few directors, the best
skill of the industry is not vested in the
present CIMC.
f.
There is no competition.
6.
Certain controls by the government must be kept for an
additional interim period because:
a.
Present economic conditions in Japan require
central booking and allocation of vessels to
lift cargoes necessary for the public welfare
regardless of whether such cargo is "good" or
"poor" cargo from a shipping viewpoint.
b.
Central allocation of vessels to obtain maximum
utilization of bottoms and prevent ships sailing
light due to excess tonnage in favored routes.
C.
Control of repair priority must be entralized.
d.
A central government agency to carry out the
instructions of Occupation Forces and duties
assigned in connection with the Occupation.
III. ASSUMPTIONS
That due to past controls exercised in restraint of
trade and control of competition by Saibatsu holding companies,
shipping

=== Page 179 === (confidence 1.00)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1943
UNCLASSIFIED
-4-
(COPY)
-3-
shipping associations, interlocking companies, and other mono-
polistic enterprises, the "big fellows" will follow the regular
pattern of trying to create some form of control in order that
they can still dominate the industry.
Every effort will be made by the industry to devise
means of obtaining maximum government financial assistance above
actual needs. The Japanese will endeavor to establish their claim
that vessels can be expected to operate just eight months out of
twelve based upon present percentage of the fleet constantly under
repairs and that charter fee should be sufficient to cover such
idle periods.
3.
In re-organizing, an effort will be made to eliminate
all companies not now in possession of ships "in being" and to
amalgamate as many shipping firms as possible into dominating
companies or organizations.
4. An effort will be made to retain for potential future
shipping all surplus seamen not actually employed in vessels.
Compensation for such seamen to be paid by the company initial-
ly but final collection to be made from government funds.
It is visualized that the ClaC will shrink in ratio
to our phased de-control until eventually it will be completely
dissolved and the records transferred to the General Maritime
Bureau (Min. of Transportation).
IV.DISCUSSION
GENERAL
1. By various SCAP directives, the Japanese shipping
industry has been instructed to make certain changes in com-
panies and in the shipping associations. Up to the present
time, most of these companies have made only a token attempt
to comply with such directives. In order to protect Civil
Transportation Section and other SCAP sections, it is felt that
vessels should not be returned to any shipping company for op-
eration under the interim plan until the company has cleaned
up and purified itself in accordance with instructions already
issued.
2. Before any ships are returned to a company, it is
deemed necessary that the following three steps in the order
listed should first be aosomplished:
a.
Screening by Imperial Japanese Government and re-
view by Government Section, GHQ, SCAP of all
persons in the shipping falling under the purge
memorandum.
b. Removal

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1943
UNCLASSIFIED
-5-
(COPY)
-4-
b.
Removal of all "control" authority and restraint
on trade by associations. New articles to be
submitted to Anti-Cartels Branch/ESS to confirm
that there is not objection by that branch of
such articles. Articles then to be registered
with the Japanese Government.
C.
The submitting of plans for re-organization of
each restricted company and the approval of such
plans by SCAP (ESS/AC).
3.
If vessels are returned to interim private operation
before the above is accomplished, we will be placed in the embar-
rassing position of reestablishing the shipping industry before
the terms of the Potsdam Declaration have been fulfilled. In
other words, we would be rebuilding with government funds on the
same structure that was condemned by the Allies and which other
SCAP sections are obligated to destroy.
LABOR
This will probably prove to be the most vexing problem
to be handled in connection with de-control of shipping. All em-
ployees are organized into unions and a wholesale unemployment pro-
spect will draw immediate reaction. This has been indicated clear-
ly in past dealings with the industry in Japan.
Personnel effected falls into two groups; men manning
ships and personnel ashore in C1C. As of 1 April 1947, the fol-
lowing was the status of ship personnel employed by CMiC.

The large number of seamen on the CMMC rolls came

4. It is considered inadvisable to accept Japanese
proposals of allocating these surplus men to the various ship-
ping companies to be carried on their payrolls in a surplus
status and to have time charter rates increased to cover.
The watering" of charter rates is not considered a clean

control, 15 percent surplus of seamen and officers be allow-

6. It is further preposed in order to take care of the
seamen that a central committee in Tokyo with branches in the
same divisions as each local Maritime Office shall be formed
with the following representation:
1 Representative - Shipowners
1 Representative - General Maritime Bureau
1 Representative - Seamen's Union
Each seamen not employed on a vessel and not classified in a
stand-by status by his company would then be required to
report periodically to the "RE-EMPLOYMENT COMMITTEE". If
this seaman was placed by the committee in employment for
which he was qualified and which was connected with his trade,
he would then cease to receive unemployment pay. If not em-
ployed, he may also be assigned to a re-training school or
re-education school. If there was neither employment nor
school assignment, he would then receive from the General
Maritime Bureau representative his unemployment compensation.
(In working out any such plan it is imperative that the union
be taken into the picture at an imperative that the union be
taken into the picture at an early stage in order that the
men will feel they had a hand in the creating of the committee.
To have them work with the shipowners and government from the
beginning will prove to be a great advantage and its value can-
not be overlooked.)

7.The Ministry of Transportation should establish certain

for the courses of education.

9. In addition to the surplus now on shore, we are advised

are overmanned by approximately 20 percent. Until such time as we
work out a definite level for shipping and can estimate the number
of men required to man such ships, it is considered advisable to

10. The Ministry of Transportation and the CMC are in the

following the sea. In the event they do not so desire, they are
being dropped from the payroll now handled by CMC. A number of
resignations have been submitted, approximately 1,000 per month.
Teams are expected to return to Tokyo with completed reports in
the next two weeks, at which time a recount will be made of the
actual surplus now existing).

SHIPPING INDUSTRY PERSONNEL PURGE
The original Purge Directive was covered by SCAPIN 550,
AG O91.1 (4 Jan 46) GS, and was implemented by the Imperial Japanese
Government under its Imperial Ordinances of 4 January 1947. This
series of ordinances covered, among others, persons in the economic
field who had responsible positions and were influential in creat-
ing the recent war. Persons of this category will be screened by
the Japanese Government first and the screenings will be reviewed
by Government Section of SCAP.
2. For ready information, thore are quoted below pertinent
sections of the ordinance outlining the extent to which the purge
will affect persons in the shipping concerns and related companies:
a.

Directors

who in fact, exercises the authority or in-
fluence or receives compensation commensurate
with that of any of the officials listed above.

ed as coming under this purge, including NYK, OSK, Yama-
shita, Mitsubishi, Mitsui, Kawasaki).

connection with handling of official affairs of poli-
tical activities of the latter, to establish or main-
tain, on behalf of him, the continuity of his influ-
ence, by giving instruction, advice or compensation
to or by communicating in any means with the latter."

CIVILIAN MERCHANT MARINE COMMITTEE (CMMC)
1.
The formation of the CMC is set forth in the following
excerpts from an exchange of radios:
a.
CINCPOA Ady. HQ desp. 32045 of 10 Sept. 1945 to SCAP
which reads in part: Ref CINCAFPAC O50853 (SCAP
Directive No. 2) Annex B Para. 4.
To exercise direction and supervision of operations
of Japanese Merchant vessels CINCPAC proposes to:
(1). Establish in Tokyo Fay area SCAJAP--
It is proposed that Japanese Government be directed
to appoint a Givilian Merchant Marine Committee whose
individual members would be satisfactory to SCAJAP
and whd would be representative of Japanese ship-
owners, bunkering agents, ship chandlers and Board
of Trade and through which as a body SCAJAP HQ
could most efficiently make known the overall
policy directives of SCAP and CINCPAC and exercise
appropriate direction and supervision of operations.
b.
SCAP concurred in 2117452 SCAP to CINCPAC as follows:
"Concur in your 100l232"
2.
The Japanese already had the Shipping Control Association
which fulfilled most of the requirements set forth by SCAJAP. As a
result, they secured permission to change its name and then utilize
the association for the continued control of Japanese shipping. In-
sofar as the Japanese are concerned, they still use the name of Ship-
ping Contol Association, however, when speaking to Occupation Forces
they call it CMC.
3。
The organization is not closely controlled by any other
Japanese governmental body, but has to go to the Ministry of Trans-
portation for its funds. It is an orphan agency working completely
for the operation of Japanese shipping under SCAJAP.
4. As the interim plan goes into effect and de-controls are
carried out, it is anticipated that CMC will have to be continued
under controls as are required due to economic conditions. Chiefly,
the following are mentioned:
a.
Central booking control
b.
Allocation of ships
C.
Control of repair priority.
d. Accounting

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.98)

September 9, 1948

-13-
(COPY)
-12-
d.

revenues and payment of charter fees etc. to
owners.
e.

It is visualized that CC will shrink in ratio to our
phased de-control until eventually it will be completely dissolved.
Innediate reduction in the organization can be expected when we
transfer responsibility for operation of the vessels and repairs
to the owners.
METHODS OF DE-CONTROL OF SHIPS
The present setup with ClMC has all vessels requisition-
ed by the government under bareboat charter. This virtually makes
all shipowners landlords collecting a small bareboat charter fee,
but having no hand in the operation of vessels. Such a device was
necessary for maximum control in time of war, hut must now be eli-
minated and the operatiors put back into business before they are
all forced to go out of existence. There is no substitute for the
interest and skill of the industry itself in the efficient opera-
tion and repair of vessels, however, an incentive of financial
ain must be injected into an interim program to make it function
on a competitive basis. The secret of success in our de-control
will be based upon simplicity of administration even though such
administration may be slightly higher in cost at the beginning
than a more complicated system.
2.
The various methods for affecting such de-control have
been carefully considered in relation to the present economic con-
dition of Japan and the government control of shipping operations.
A phased de-control has been outlined and is described in more
detail in the following paragraphs.
As a preparatory step to such decontrol, a committee
will be appointed to determine charter fees and berth agent hus-
bandry fees. This Committee to be knowm as the "CHARTER COMMIT-
TEE" and to he composed of appointees representing the following:
a.
Economic Stabilization Board.
b.
Ministry of Finance.
C.
Price Board.
d.
General Maritime Bureau (Ministry of Transportation)
e.
Civilian Merchant Marine Committee (CMMC)
f.
Shipowners.
g.
Neutral member in case of tie.
It is felt that any collusion of shipowmers in the maritime agencies
of the government will be offset by the presence of the Economic
Stabiligation Board, Ministry of Finance, and Price Board, on the
Committee, who will be interested in keeping down government ex-
penses as much as possible.
FIRST

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-14-
(COPY)
-13-
FIRST PHASE
1. A de-control by types of vessels as our first phase can
be regulated easily and the differential between freight rates and
operating costs covered by the charter fee. The entire fleet divides
itself into types that can be marked off as increments. As fast as
the industry itself can make the necessary adjustments to its renew-
ed activity in operation, just so fast can releases of step by step
be made. As each group is released, the manner in which the transi-
tion is accomplished can be watched and the next step regulated ac-
cordingly. Unforeseen difficulties arising after the first block
de-control will be visible and such problems handled. In this man-
ner, transition may be accomplished with the least danger of inter-
rupting the water transportation by endeavoring to create too great
a change at one time. In the implementation of this plan, the book-
ing of cargo and allocation of ships with sufficient freedom to en-
sure full utilization of vessels to the greatest advantage is
still centrally controlled.
2.
It is mandatory that the charter party contain certain
incentives and penalties to the operators to create the greatest
efficiency and competition. To accomplish this result, the charter
Party will be on a muoh broader scope of responsibility for the own-
ers than would be common practice in granting charters under normal
conditions or in a stabilized economy. Basically, the charter par-
ty shall include the following salient points:
a.
Vessel goes off hire when laid up for major repairs
or excessive time on running repairs.
b.
Demurrage to be assessed for delays in lay-time.
c.
Premiums to be paid as "dispatch" money for unusually
rapid turn-around.
d.
Repairs for responsibility of owners.
e.
Bunkering to be accomplished by owners.
f.
Manning, provisioning and upkeep account owners.
3.
The off-hire penalty in the charter party when vessels
are not working is desirable. If cargoes through central booking
do not keep the vessels employed, this will cause operators to
bring pressure against CMC. This factor should assist in the
shift of cargo from rail to water when bottoms are idle and bring
about the desired balance between rail and water freight.
4. Inasmuch as the operators will not have offices in all
ports and it is not intended to permit "Watering" charter fees to
permit excessive overhead, it is contemplated that owners may ap-
pointed as husbandry agents, companies have offices in the port
where their vessels may be assigned. Such berth agents will be
responsible for complete husbandry of the vessel and handling of
the cargo and will receive a flat fee for each vessel so handled.
Therefore,

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-15-
(COPY)
-14-
Therefore, profits hinge upon volume. Owners naturally will tend
to favor agents who secure "Premium dispatch money" rather than
"demurrage"penalties.
5.
A natural overlap will exist during this first phase
of the de-control in local offices of the CMMC and owmers. How-
ever, the gradual shrinking is estimated to permit a reduction
in the CMMC organization of about 2,000 personnel or 50 as a
result of this first phase.
Retention of these government offices in domestic trade
will be required for control of dispatch monies and payment of
handling charge. Vessels operating in overseas trade will continue
to handle expenses incurred in same manner as at present. In ad-
dition, the continuation of daily reporting of vessels movements
to Occupation Forces (SCAJAP) as a control measure is essential
to utilization of the vessels.
SECOND PHASE
1. After the first phase has been instigated and is pro-
gressing, a study of trade routes will be completed in prepara-
tion for the next phase. Once the amount of cargo regularly
moving on the routes has been established, further decontrol will
be started by areas, viz. Hokkaido trade, Kyushu Trade, Inland
Sea trade, then overseas trade. Competitive bedding by ship-
owners will be invited for movement of cargo by routes. Addi-
tional tonnage required to take up the slack will be moved by
vessels controlled by CMC under the first phase. As traffic
increases on the respective route, the shipping companies will
tend to firm up for normal economy and the number of vessels un-
der government control will diminish in ratio. Vessels not re-
quired under charter by CMMC may engage in independent trade
without subsidies.
THIRD PHASE
1. The third phase will be the establishment of rates
equitable with prices. Present policy to control inflation does
not permit over-all increases in waterborne rates due to the ef-
fect on over-all living cost. Until such time as the economy is
more stabilized, this phase cannot be completed.
FOURTH PHASE
1. The fourth phase will be the establishment of commodi-
ty subsidies on cargo which requires assistance due to lack of
balance between operating cost and revenue. These will become
very pronounced and will be bulk commodities, lending themselves
easily to a separate group on which such subsidies can be es-
tablished.
FIFTH

=== Page 191 === (confidence 1.00)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-16-
(COPY)
-15-
FIFTH PHASE
1. The last phase will be withdrawal of commodity sub-
sidies and final adjustment of such subsidies as are necessary un-
der total private enterprise. After this phase is developed, de-
control will be completed. Such controls as central booking, al-
location of vessels, and priority of repairs will be removed and
CMC dissolved. Records of this organization may then be trans-
ferred to the General Maritime Bureau.
2.
The foregoing is summarized by steps in Appendix I.
V. CONCLUSIONS
1. To increase the efficiency of vessel operation and to
expedite repairs, it is desirable and necessary to transfer operat-
ing responsibilities of the Japanese Merchant Fleet to shipowners
and operators.
2. A total return of vessels to private enterprise at
this time cannot be effected as certain centralized controls are
still required by the economic conditions existent in Japan today.
3.
Some form of subsidy of necessary as marine shipping
rates are not in proper adjustment to:
a.
Current price levels
b.
Rail rates
C.
Cost of ship operation
d.
Cost of construction
The present weakened condition of shipping companies
limit their ability to resume successfully private enterprise with-
out some form of government assistance.
5. The shipping industry is not prepared to receive full
private enterprise until such time as the individual restricted
companies obtain approval of reorganization plans and have compli-
ed with SCAP directives. They may, however, operate vessels be-
fore final adjustments are accomplished and be gaining strength
for full private enterprise.
6. A phased de-control can be regulated in ratio to the
ability of the shipping companies to assume additional respon-
sibility.
7.
Such de-control will tend to increase efficiency of
ships, shorten turn-around time, bring into operation a greater
number of bottoms and give the companies a chance to regain
operating skill.
8. By

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 3 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-17-
(COPY)
-16-
who have neither controlled nor operated their own vessels for
over eight years will have an opportunity to re-establish them-
selves and regain their operating skill phase by phase. As ad-
justments are accomplished and strength regained, they will be
prepared for their full responsibilities of normal commercial
ship operation with the minimum of financial aid from the
government.
VI RECOMMENDATION
It is recommended that action being taken to estab-
lished a modified form of private operation, designed to effect
economies in the Japanese budget and resumption of normal com-
mercial shipping enterprise, be approved and continued accord-
ing to the phases outlined in Appendix I.

Paul E. Richers

DECONTROL

DECONTROL OF VESSELS BY PHASES
FIRST PHASE
1.
Place first increment of vessels on broad time charter 15
July 1947 instead of present bareboat charter. Continue
by increment as the industry adjust itself.
RESULT :
We now have the operators running the ships in
increasing numbers. Full responsibility for
operation is with shipowners, however, central
booking, ship allocation and priority of repairs
still rest with CMMC for necessary control under
present economy.
SECOND PHASE
1.
Study the normal regular volume of traffic moving in the
various trade routes. Divide the areas into Hokkaido Trade,
Kyushu trade, and Inland trade. Taking one trade at a time,
prepare tonnage requirements against cargo requirements.
2.
Competitive bidding by shipowners invited for movement of cargo
by routes. As traffic increases continue solidifying tonnage
in the trade until all routes are firmed up.
THIRD PHASE
1.
As the economy permits, establish rates equitable with prices.
FOURTH PHASE
1.
To further effect decontrol, cancel charter parties established
in the phases preceding and in their place grant subsidies on
specific comodities where the disparity is great. NOTE: Such
controls as central booking, allocation of vessels, and prior-
ity of repairs will be removed and CMMC dissolved.
FIFTH PHASE
1.
Establish final requirements for subsidies of the shipping in
Japan.
2.
Subsidies as required to be granted and all emergency controls
on shipping to be cancelled.

(COPY)
THE JAPANESE SHIPOWNERS' ASSOCIATION, INC.
Tokyo, January 28, 1948.
A STATEMENT BY JUNSUKE ITAYA, PRESIDENT
The war-time shipping control ordinance, extended three time
after the end of hostilities, goes out of effect on March 31, 1948,
and the present C.M.M.C. organization will lose its legal basis on
the same day, unless an appropriate legislative step is taken before
the expiration date.
Realizing that C.M.M.C. has to be continued under occupation,
and also that an agency for a unified state control of maritime
transportation is called for under the existing economic condition
of the country, we favor and advocate the creation of a new agency
after April this year.
Such an agency should, in our opinion, be so constituted as
to be a temporary organ of a much simplified structure than the
present organization, as we shall eleborate at some length later
on. We should now like to state briefly the reasons why we are
in favor of such an agency.
In the first place, the operating efficiency of the existing
merchant marine must be increased to the utmost, as we have aften
stressed. There can be no dissenting opinion, we dare say, on
this score. We only wish to add that the need of a greatly enlarg-
ed transportation is all the more urgent and real in view of the
increased production program of all essential commodities for the
next fiscal year commencing on April 1 next, as witness 36,000,000
tons of coal to be mined as against barely 30,000,000 tons for the
current year. We have consistently maintained, that better trans-
portation performance can be gotton out of the present merchant
fleet only through a closer and smoother coordination of mainten-
ance, operation, and manning of ships. This in turn calls for a
larger role that shipowners should play, and a correspondingly
smaller role for the state agency for shipping control. In other
words, it means decontrol of shipping by stages in an orderly man-
ner.
Improved performance of the existing fleet will naturally
result in the lightening of fiscal burden falling upon taxpayers,
which is our second point. We cannot emphasize this point too
strongly. According to the estimate for the next fiscal year,
the budgeted expenses over and above freight and other incomes
of C.M.M.C. will run to the enormous amount of ¥5,400 million,
on the

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 5 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSDFIED
-2-
on the basis of commodity prices and wage scales of September last.
Of this huge sum, subsidy for the carriage of domestic cargoes, es-
timated at 12,600,000 tons, amounts to 3,900 million yen, or over
¥310 for every ton of cargo moved. Because of the sharp advances
in prices and wages since September last year, and the inevitable
further advances during the next fiscal year, the expenses will run
far in excess of the estimate just mentioned; indeed, the outlay will
exceed lo billion. Any saving that can be effected from better mana-
gement under increasingly private operation will be of greater impor-
tance to general taxpayer in exact proportion to the higher prices
and wages.
Thirdly a peace-time agency for the emergency state control of
shipping must be conceived and organized in such a way that ways will
be open and paved for the eventual restoration of the shipping indus-
try to private enterprise. A Japanese merchant marine privately owm-
ed and operated being the fixed policy of GHQ, SCAP, we shall not la-
bor this point. It may be added that the present freight rate struc-
ture must be re-examined and upward revisions made, because the exist-
ing rates are disproportionately low, in comparison both with the gen-
eral commodity price level, and the real cost of railway transporta-
tion as distinguished from the nominal rail freight rates in the
tariffs. The upward revision of marine freight rates is an indis-
pensable step toward decontrol of shipping and the ultimate elimina-
tion of subsidy on maritime transportation. Only by proper adjust-
ment of freight rates will it be possible to let the transportation
cost fall where it should, namely the beneficiaries.
In the fourth place, there is another problem of the utmost
urgency. And that is: How much longer can shipping companies re-
tain their organizations and talents, and patiently wait for the
happy days of shipping decontrol? In the face of mounting losses,
tight financial condition, the threatened flight of capital from
the industry, and the losses of able staff members due to small
pay and absence of bright future, return of shipping to private
hands can never go beyond a ptous hope, unless steps are taken,
and immediately, to enable shipping firms to keep in existencc.
And the only way of doing so is by giving back to them the work
which is primarily theirs.
We now come to our concept of the new government agency for
shipping control and its functions. Itemized, our concept is as
follows:
1).
A Shipping Control Committee to be brought into being by a
suitable legislation.
a).
This Committee to be a temporary organ, and to cease
to exist when a degree of economic stability is achiev-
ed. It shall be of a simplified structure which will
permit it to function in a democratic manner, free from
bureaucratic

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 5 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-3-
bureaucratic controls and red-tapism. The present C.M.M.C.
organization to be made use of in forming the new Committee
as far as necessary and useful.
b).
The Committee to be so organized as to be acceptable to
GHQ, SCAP as the C.M.M.C. under its directive.
c).
The Committee is to allocate ships to various trades, de-
termine the priority of ship repairs, take charge of neces-
sary labor management, and exercise other essential con-
trols.
Operation, maintenance, and manning are to be done by
shipowmers.
d).
The Committee to charter on time such ships as are neces-
sary for its purposes.
e).
Ships now under bare charter to the government to go on
time charter as soon as they complete their voyages or
repairs after the said Committee comes into being.
f).
The Committee to be empowered to make contracts of af-
freightment with shippers.
g).
Sea personnel to be employees of shipowners but such
portion of personnel not immediately employable or not
on owners' reserve lists to be taken care of by the
Shipping Control Committee.
h).
The Committee to receive its funds out of the budget for
the Ministry of Transportation.
2).
Sub-committees may be appointed within the Committee to ensure
efficient and smooth working, e.g.,
a).
Operation Sub-committee, in charge of ship allocation,
charter and freight rates, handling fees, etc.
b).
Labor Sub-committee, in charge of re-placement, re-educa-
tion, etc. of sea and shore personnel.
c).
Repair Sub-committee, in charge of priority in ship re-
pairs.
3).
Operating Agency Arrangement to be made with qualified ship-
ping companies by the Committee, as a temporary measure for
the period of transition towards the ultimate ship operation
by owners themselves at their own risk and account,- the form
that is certain to achieve the utmost in the economy of cost
of transportation and the maximum carriage of goods by sea.
The

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl. No. 5 to
Tokyo's No. 593
September 9, 1948
UNCLASSIFIED
-4-
The transition period to be divided into the following stages:
a).
First Stage, at which -
i). Operators to handle the actual operation of ships
on fee basis;
ii). The Committee to assume all the risk and account.
b).
Second Stage, at which -
i). The fee basis to continue as before, but bonus and
penalty system to be added:
ii).
Bonus and penalty to apply to improve monthly work-
ing of ships and to saving in the costs of operation.
iii).
For assessing the costs of operation of ships, stan-
dard costs to be determined, and bonus paid for better
results or handling fees deducted as penalty.
iv).
For assessing monthly working of ships, standard of
cargo capacity to be determined for each ship, and
that of the duration of each voyage to be determin-
ed; and both to be applied to determine the bonus
or penalty.
c).
Third Stage, at which -
i). Operation of ships to be assigned to owmers on a
competitive bid, the bidders to be wholly responsible
to perform the carriage of goods contracted for.
For further details of the method of effecting the necessary
change-over and allied subjects, we wish to refer you to the program
in our letter submitted to General Besson on September 25, 1947.
Having thus stated our views and outline plan briefly, we pray
that C.T.S. will assume at once a real and effective leadership to
bring about the much-needed reform in the method of shipping control
in this country. We know of no other authorities, GHQ Sections, or
the Government, legislative or executive, that can inspire the reform
and translate the reform scheme into action with force and effect
which your Section is capable of. When we look to your Section for
a genuine leadership in the reform, we know that we are asking in
perfectly good conscience for your aid to promote measures that are
wholly consonant with the GHQ policy and strictly in the national
interest of Japan.

CHAPTER V
SALARY, WAGES OR· OTHER RFMUNERATIONS
(Method of Fixing Salary, Wages or other Remunerations)
Article 52
The salary, wares or other remunerations of a mariner
shall be fixed in consideration of the particular nature of
maritime labor and according to his experience, capacity,
and service.
(Method of Paving Salary, Wages or other Remunerations)
Article 53
The salary, wares, or other remunerations shall be paid
direct to the mariner in currency and in full, unless other-
wise provided by law, orinnnce, or by collective agreement.
The salary, wages or other remunerations except those
provided by Ordinance shall be paid once or more each month
on the fixed day, or days.
Article 54
The shipowner shall without delay pay a mariner such
salary, wages or any other remuneration provided for in
paragraph 2 of the preceding Article even before the pay-
day in respect of every day of service, in the case faliing
under any of the following items:
1. If a mariner has been discharged, or has retired,
from his service;
2.
If a mariner has demanded his salary, wages or any
other remuneration to be paid in order that any of
them may be used by the mariner, or by any one of
his relatives who is living with him or by a person
who maintains his eristence upon the income of such
mariner, to mcet thc expense for mafriage or funeral,
childbirth or medical treatment, or the expense for
the recovery from an unforeseen accident.

Article 55
When the salary, wapes or any other remuneration of a
seaman is to be paid on board the vessel, the master himself
shall deliver the same to the seeman. When an unavoidable
necessity arises, however, the master may cause another of-

Article 56
The shipowner shell, if called upon by a mariner to do
so, pay the salary, wares, or any other remuneretion of the
mariner to one of his relatives who is living with him or to
a person who maintains his existence upon the income of such
mariner.
(Right to Demand Salary or Weges During Infury or Sickness)
Article 57
A mariner may, during the term of his engagement, Homand
his salary or wages and allowances provided for in Ordinance
even for the period during which he has not been in service
by reason of in jury or sickness, eycept where such injury or
sickness is due to a wiful act or gross default on the part

(Remuneration in Percentage)
Article 58
In case where the remuneration is paid in percentage,
the sum of such rmuneration shall not be less than the amount
fixed by the shipowner, cven if the monthly sum computed in
percentage does not amount to the fixed amount.
In the application of the provisions of Article 35 and
the preceding Article, the fixed amount of remuneration as
provided for in the preceding paragraph shall be deemed to be
salary or wages.
In cases where the remuneration of a mariner is paid in
percentage, the amount fiyed by the shipowner shall he deemed
to be the mariner's monthly salary or wages, in the application
of the provis1ons of Article 45, 46, 49 and 78.
The amount mentioned in the proceding paragraph shall
not he less than the fixed amount provided for in paragraph 1.
(Minimum Salary or Wages)
Article 59
The competent authorities may, if they deem necessary,
fix the minimum amount of salary, wages or any other re-
munerations in accordence with the provisions of Ordinance,
with the consent of the Labor Commission organized under the
Labor Union Law (hereinafter called the Mariners' Labor Com-
mission).
The shipowner shall not.employ a mariner at a salary,
wages or any other remuneration lower than the minimum amount
provided for in the preceding paragraph.

CHAPTER VII
HOLIDAYS WITH PAY
(Granting of Holidays With Pay)
Article 74
If a mariner has had continuous sea service of a year
on board the same vessel, (including service while the vessel
is being equipped or under repairs: the same applying cor-
respondingly to the following articles), the shipowner.shall,
within one vear from such duration of time, give the mariner
holidays with pay. If the vessel is on a voyage, however, the
shipowner may postpone bestowal of such holidays with pay
for a perind necessary for the voyage.

default on the part of the mariner and the period of inter-

Every meriner shall be granted twenty-five days off with

five days for every additional three months of continuous
service.
Every mariner on board the vessel navigating within the

food expense provided for in the preceding paragraph, in

CHAPTER X
COMPENSATIONS FOR ACCIDENTS
(Compensation for Medical Treatment)
Article 89
When a mariner has sustained an injury or contracted
a sickness in the performance of his duties, the shipowner
shall grant him medical benefits at the shipowner's expense
or pay him the cost of necessary medical treatment until he
has completely recovered from such injury or sickness.
When a mariner has, during the term of his engagement,
sustained an inlury or contracted a sickness without relation
to the performance of his duties, the shipowner shall grant
him medical benefits at the shipowner's expense or pay him the
cost of necessary medical treatment.for a period not exceed-
ing three months, except in cases where such in jury or sick-
ness is due to a wiful act or gross default on the part of
the mariner.
Article 90.
The medical henefits or treatment provided for in the
preceding article shall include each of the following items:
Modical examinations;
2.
Supply of drugs, medicines or medical appliances;
3:
Surrical treatments, operations or other remedies;
4.
Admission to hospitals or clinics or other places
outside mariners' homes necessary for medical treat-
monts (including supply of food);
5.
Sick-nursing;
6.
Transportetion.
21

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
MARINERS LAW
(Injury or Sickness Allowances and
Convalescence Allowances)
Article 91
When a mariner has sustained an inlury or contracted a
sickness in the performance of his duties, the shipowner shall
pay him, once a month, an injury or sickness allowance equiv-
alent to the monthly amount of the remuneration provided for
in ordinance (hereinafter called the standard remuneration)
for a period not exceeding four months and until the mariner
has completely recovered from such injury or sickness.
In cases where the mariner has not completely recovered from
such in jury or sickness even after the lapse of the four
months, the shipowner shall pay him, once a month, an injury
or sickness allowance equivalent to sixty per centum of the
monthly standard remuneration until he has completely recovered.
The shipowner shall, immediately after the mariner has
completely recovered from the injury or sickness provided for
in the preceding paragraph, pay him a convalescence allowance
equivalent to sixty per centum of the monthly standard remu-
neration.
The provisions of the two preceding paregraphs shall not
apply in cases where the in jury or sickness is due to a wiful
act or gross default on the part of the mariner.
(Accident Allowances)
Article 92
If, in cases where a mariner has completely recovered
from injury or sickness which he sustained or contracted in the
performance of his duties, he is still in a state of physical
breakdown, the shipowner shall, immediately after the recovery,
pay him an accident allowance equal to the monthly standard
remuneration multiplied by the number of months mentioned in
the Appended List according to the degree of the breakdown.
The same shall not, however, apply in cases where the infury
or sickness is due to a wilful act or gross default on the
part of the mariner.
(Bereavement Allowances)
Article 93
When a mariner has died in the performance of his duties,
the shipowner shall without delay pay a bereavement allowance
equivalent to the monthly standard remuneration multiplied by
thirty-six to the members of the bereaved family provided for
by ordinance. The same shall apply in cases where a mariner
has died from injury or sickness which he sustained or coni
tracted in the performance of his duties.
22

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
MARINERS LAW
(Funeral Expenses)
Article 94
When a mariner has died in the the performance of his
duties, the shipowner shall without delay pay a funeral ex-
pense eguivalent to the monthly standard remuneration multi-
plied by two to a member of the bereaved family, provided for
by ordinance, who holds the funeral services. The same shall
apply in cases where a mariner.has died from injury or sick-
ness which he sustained or'contracted in the performance of
his'duties.
(Relations to Other Benefits)
Article 95
If a person who is entitled. under the provisions of
Articles 89 to the preceding Article inclusive, to receive
medical benefits or expenses of medical treatments, allow-
ances or funeral expenses (hereinafter collectively called
the compensations for accidents), has come to receive, for
the same cause whoreby he is entitled to receive such com-
pensations for accidents, the insurance benefits provided for
in the Meriners' Insurance Law or such benefits corresponding
to the compensations for accidents as are provided for by
ordinance, the shipowner shall be relieved of his liability
for such compensations for accidents.
(Examination and Arbitration)
Article 96
When a person is.dissatisfied with the ascertainment of
1njury; sickness or death caused by the performance of duties,
with the method of medical benefits or treatments, with the
decision of making compensations for accidents or with any
other way of making compensations for accidents, he may call
upon competent authorities for an examination or arbitration in
the matter.
The competent authorities may, if they deem necessary, ex-
ercise'their authority in examinins or arbitrating in the matter,
The competent authorities shall not either examine or
arbitrate in the matter without siving a hearing to the master
or to other persons concerned.
The competent authorities may, 1f they deem necessary
for examination and arbitration in the matter, cause a doctor
to conduct a medical examination or an autopsy.
The call for the examinetion or the arbitration 1n the
matter mentioned in paregraph 1 and the commencement of the
examination or the arbitration in the matter mentioned in
paragraph 2 shall be regerded as a demand by way of fudicial
proceedings, in respect of the interruption of prescription.

(Procedures of Formulating Working Reguletions).
Article .98

working regulations containing elements which are in conflict
with laws, ordinances or collective agreements.

repulations to be. inappropriate,.order alteration thereof with

Article 100
Such portion of a contract of engagement as contains provi-
sion for working conditions failing short of the standard
fixed in the workinf regulations, shall be invalidated. In
such case the contract shall, in respect of the portion thus
invalidated, be deemed to contain provision for working con-
ditions coming up to the standard fixed in the working re-
gulations.

IHy dear Lir. Draper:

As set forth in our letter of lay 26, and as I statod to
you at the time of our meeting, Anericn stoanship owners are con-
siderably concernod about our national policy, and perhaps the
matter of an international policy, respocting the future of Jap-
anese shipping in the international trades. You were kind onough
to advise ie that no definite policy from a national standpoint
had been decided upon, and you invited exprossions from American
steamship owmers repre. ntel y this Foderation concerning such
a policy.
The purposo of this letter is to present such views as are
presently in the minds of the American steamslip owners we ropresent,
comprising about two-thirds of all privately ovned American flag
ships.
We understand there is no certainty at the moment as to
where the final decision will be reached, i. e., whether by the

the Far Eastern Commiss n; or some other. American governmental or

In the first instance, may we say that the interests we
represent are not unmindful of the task which confronts the United
States and/or the Allied liaticns endeavoring to restore the Jap-
anose national to a level of reasonable living standards and of
reasonable economic stability. lo are equally clear, hovever, that

As stated to you, American shipping interests have no ambi-

The United States has
never attained a maritino position conmensurate with its geographic
and economic status, anu as a leader in the United Nations Organi-
zation. No nation which has failed to maintain its flag upon the
seas has long retained a position of influence and leadership in
world affairs. liistory is replete with examples to the contrary.
The Congress of the United States in passing the ierchant
Marine Act of 1936 stated it to be a national policy that the United
States shall have a merchant marine to carry its domestic water-
borne commerce, a substantial portion of its foreign conmerce, and
capable of serving as a naval and military auxiliary in time of war
or national emergency.

In implenenting this national policy we envision a merchant
fleet of modern combination and cargo vessels under the United
States flag manned by Aierican officers and sealen, transporting all
of our domestic coastwise and intercoastal and territory commerce
and at least one-hal of our inports and exports, and backed by a

With the coming of Perry and the restoration of the Mciji

its development. Individual loyalty was transferred from tho feudal
lords to the erperor. The succeeding regime was under a typo of
monarchical control, and in a substantial degree industrial deve-

it is thought that the onarc held financial interest. Through
all these periods the individual vas competely suoservient to the
State. Personal freedom and dignity were not part of the Japanese
order.
le now cone to a new epoch in Japan's history with the
establishment of constitutional denocratic governnent and the con-
cept of human rights and liborty. No one can foresee how the
Japanese people vill react to their new environment. Past history
and statistics of a completely different goverriental and economic
system provide no reliable guide. It would be a rash policy to use
past milestones to measure future progress, and to plot a completely
theoretical course under which Japanese agriculture, industry and
comerce would develop.

Among the reasons why we believe such an increase in Japan-
ese shipping at this time would be unsound, are the following:
1. Ve suggest that the peace of the world would best be
served by a policy which restricts Japanese shipping primarily to
the domestic Japanese trades and to service between Japan and
nearby continental and island areas.
2. That Japanese shipping net income as represented by
its ratio to net national income, plays a small part in the overall
Japanese economy, representing less than 1%. Therefore, it is not
an offective means of balancing Japanese finances or of raising
living standards.
3. Ve question the justification of raising Japanese par-
ticipation in international shipping to a relatively higher level
than is enjoyed by the United States, particularly when such stimu-
lation of Japanese economic recovery must be at our expense.
4. Ve question whether it is fair and just, at our expense,
to improve the economic stability and living standards of our recent
enemy to a point substantially superior to that of our recent allies
in the same geographic area.
5. Due to complete changes in government, physical condi-
tions and psychology, the past achievements and statistical records
of Japan are not reliable guides to the future production, overseas
commerce and finanees of the nation. No one can foresee the future
of Japanese industry as related to world trade. We suggest it

certain tonnages in imr rts and exports will develop. The general

suBject: Request by the National Federation of American Shipping for Hearing
on the Japanese Merchant arine.
Ir. Norton's memorandum of October 12 on the above subject
raises the qucstion of cocrdination anong all interested agencies
of the U.S. Government of policy toward the Japanese merchant
marine, with specific regerd to coordination between O and T. EP
agrees as to the desirability of such coordination and draws
attention to the past history of intra-departmental and inter-
agency consultations on the subject.
As noted in TRC/S' memorandum quoted by Mr. Norton, the
development of postwar policy toward Japanese shipping goes back
to the war period when "competent spokesmen for the Maritime Com-
mission and the ar Shipping Administration made various public
statements to the general effect that postwar ship operation and
shipbuilding activities in Japan should be depressed or stopped
TW -89485
and that American shipping should be encouraged to assume a large
share of the work done by pre-war Japanese shipping." I have no
lmowledge as to whether these statements were cleared in advance
with those agencies, namely the State, army and Navy Departments,
which were smogamtly charged with responsibility for development
of U.S. policy for Japan uner the occupation.
Vhen U.S. policy for a retention level for Japanese shipping
and shipbuilding vas under development in 1946 and 1947, full con-
sideration was given to two major factors: security, and the peace-
ful needs of the Japanese people. ith regard to security, the
10-1248
Department  depended fully on the views of the Army and
Navy Departments. On the matter of the peaceful needs of Japan, it
may be pointed out that the U.S. Government was bound by the pro-
vision of the Potsdam Declaration, which states that "Japan shall
be permitted to maintain such industries as will sustain her
economy." Full consultation among the Departments of State, Army,
Navy, SCaP, the Pauley Mission and the Strike Mission, in the
spring of 1947, determined the appropriate levels under these
concepts, for shipbuilding capacity, as 150,000 gross tons annually,
with a provision barring capacity for building individual vessels
in excess of stated tonnage limits. It was agreed at the same time
hat the existing Japanese merchant fleet should be cut back to
2,000,000 gross tons, and that all vessels in excess of stated
ton age and speed limits slould be removed. It was recognized,
RM/R
Cat

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
2
SECRET
however, that the Japenese would subsequently need more than
2,000,000 gross tons of merchant shipping in order to meet their
peaceful needs.
Mr. Deimel, TRC, in May, 1947, commented in a memo to Mr. Hodge,
JK, on the U.S. policy outlined above, but only with reference to
the possible diseconomy of restricting Japanese tankers to 3,000
lighweight tons or less.
In October 1947, E drafted a SWNCC paper proposing in effect
that the U.S. renounce its interest in the nine vessels which
would become available for reparations from Japan under U.S.
policy toward the existing merchant fleet. SD cleared the proposed
draft, provided the Maritime Commission concurred. OE consequently
presented the matter to the Commission, together with the necessary
background material, including the papers on U.S. policy regarding
levels for Japanese shipping and shipbuilding. The Commission
approved the draft SiNCC paper and made no comment regarding U.S.
policy on shipbuilding capacity or the size of the merchant fleet.
In April 1948, after CI had recommended & 1evel of 400,000
gross tons for Japanese shipbuilding, Mr. Whitman, E, sent a memo-
randum to Mr. Radius, TRC, informing him that (1) existing U.S.
policy on Japanese shipping and shipbuilding was based on security
considerations no longer considered valid; (2) that OE was then
recommending a 300,000 gross ton capacity figure for shipbuilding
with no removals to be made from the existing fleet; and (3)
that Japan needs a merchant fleet of at least 3,000,000 gross
tons to avoid a net loss on shipping account. To my knowledge,
E received no indication that ThC objected to the new proposal.
SCAP, since that time, has recommended a level of 500,000
gross tons for Japanese shipbuilding, and the Johnston Committee
has proposed around 650,000 gross tons. Hegotiations on the
shipbuilding issue have, in recent months, been conducted at the
Secretarial level between State and Army and under such a high
security classification that it has been difficult at times for
EP to keep its own staff informed. It has not been possible for
EP, on its own authority, to seek the views of other divisions of
the Department.
Since May of this year, when unofficial announcement was made
in Japan regarding building of vessels for foreign account in Japan,
considerable interest has been displayed in this matter by FEC

shipping interests of Norway, Denmark, the Philippines and France
total more than 125,000 deadweight tons and their firm offers
amount to approximately 500,000 deadweight tons more.

SUBJECT:
Attached Memorandum Regarding Future Status of Japanese

The attached copy of a memorandum from Mr. Norton to
Mr. Saltzman regarding the interest of the National Federation
of American Shipping in the determination of a post-war level
for Japanese shipbuilding and shipping raises two questions,
one of substance and one of procedure.
With regard to the substantive matter raised, Mr. Norton's
office maintains that this Government is committed, as a result
of statements made during the war and subsequently by the
Files
DC/R
mis Document Must Be Returned lo
Maritime Commission and the War Shipping Administration to the
policy that post-war ship operation and shipbuilding activities
should be encouraged so assume a large share of the work done
by pre-war Japanese snipping. Mr. Norton's memorandum also
8
points out that Congress has decided, in the case of a projected
94.8
ERP plan to charter or sell Us Government-owned ships to foreign
interests, that it is more importent to promote the American
Merchant Marine than to reduce the burden on the American tax-
payer which such a transfer of US-owned ships would effect. It
1s contended that the same argument would prevail in the case of
Japanese shipping and that the policy of protecting and promoting
the American Merchant Marine 1s of higher consideration than the
O
possible saving to the American taxpayer from the development of
the Japanese Merchant Marine.
6
With respect to the procedural matter, Mr. Norton's memorandum
alleges that the responsibility for formulating Us policy toward 8
Japanese shipping has, up to the present, been assumed "elsewhere
in the Department without reference to the Department's accumulated,
specialized knowledge on the subject." It is also stated that
"it is important that there be one American shipping policy for
all intergovernmental organizations, rather than unrelated
fragments of it that tend to contradict each other." Mr. Norton,
therefore, requests that a meeting be set up in order to resolve
differences of opinion between the various offices concerned
and suggests that this meeting be held jointly with Mr. Lovett.
He maintains there is a certain urgency about the matter in view

that it be consulted with regard to this matter before any fingl
decisions are made.

With regard to the substantive portions of Mr. Norton's
memorandum, it is not necessary at this time to go into them in
detail except to point out briefly the following factors:
1. Any artificial restriction placed upon the development
of Japan's post-war shipping would be contrary to the
estεblished Us policy that the viotors in the late war
should not use their positions to gain economic advan-
tage over their former enemies. (The only reason for
1gnoring this principle would be in case Us security
might be threatened and in this instance the Navy has
ruled that the revival of Japan's shipping and ship-
building industries would not be a security threat to
this country.)
2.
According to a dispatch from USPOLAD, Tokyo, dated
May 29, 1948, wh1ch enclosed a statement by the Japanese
Ministry of Foreign Affairs on Japanese shipping,
tonnage has dropped from a peak of 6,329,000 tons in
1941 to 1,540,000 tons at present; and approximately
66 per cent of the existing ships are of wartime design,
considered inefficient and uneconomical by commercial
standards, while the remainder have an average age of
22 years. It 1s evident, therefore, that it will be
a considerable time before the Japanese are able, even
under the most favorable circumetances, to rebuild their
shipping to anywhere near its pre-war level, and that
during this time American and other shipping industries
will have an opportunity to consolidate their own
positions in the Pacific maritime trade with little
or no effeotive Japanese competition. It is also
pointed out in the dispatch under reference that in the
opinion of the Japanese Ministry of Foreign Affairs the
provisions of the Seaman's Law, enacted in Japan in
1947, are even more favorable to the worker than those
of the International Convention on Maritime Labor. If
this is so, the fear of cheap Japanese labor competing
with highly paid American seamen should not be the
bogey it once was.
3.
In the dispatoh referred to it is stated that USPOLAD
is now completing a definitive study of the problems
connected with Japan's post-war Merchant Marine and
shipbuilding industry and hopes shortly to forward it
to Washington with appropriate recommendations. It 1s
believed that no final decision should be made here on
the matter until receipt of this report.
