Subject: Japanese lierchant liarine
My dear Lir. Draper:
Reforring to my letter of May 26; your response of June 1,
and to our subsequent conversation with respect to postwar plans
of Japaneso merchant shipping.
As set forth i: our letter of May 26, and as I stated to
10-1948
you at the time of our meeting, Anerican steamship owners are con-
siderably concernod about our national policy, and perhaps the
matter of an international policy, respecting the future of Jap-
anese shipping in the international trades. You were kind enough
to advise me that no definite policy from a national standpoint
had been decided upon, end you invited expressions from American
SECRE
steamship owners represented by this Federation concerning such
a policy.
The purpose of this letter is to present such views as are
1
presently in the minds of the American steamship owners we represent,
comprising about two-thirds of all privately owned American flag
ships.
We understand there is no certainty at the moment as to
where the final decision will be reached, i. e., whether by the
Department of the Army; the Suprene Cornander of Allied Powers;

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.97)
William H. Draper, Jr.
- 2 -
Septembe1 22, 1948
the Far Eastern Conmission; or some other. American governmental or
international tribunal. We also understand that if some agency
other than the Department of the Army is to determine these ques-
tions you will advise us so that our representations may be made
before the determinativo body.
In the first instance, may we say that the interests we
represent are not urmindful of the task which confronts the United
States and/or the Allied Nations endeavoring to restore the Jap-
anese national to a level of reasonable living standards and of
reasonable economic stability. le are equally clear, however, thet
the effect of these efforts upon our own national economy and upon
those of the Alliod llations cannot be ignored.
As stated to you, Anerican shipping interests have no ambi-
tion to monopolize Pacific Ocean shipping. The United States has
never attained a maritime position cormensurate with its geographic
and economic status, and as a leader in the United Nations Organi-
zation. No nation whioh has failed to maintain its flag upon the
seas has long retained a position of influence and leadership in
world affairs. liistory is replete with examples to the contrary.
The Congress of the United States in passing the Merchant
Marine Act of 1936 stated it to be a national policy that the United
States shall have a merchant marine to carry its domestic water-
borne commerce, a substantial portion of its foreign commerce, and
capable of serving as a naval and military auxiliary in time of war
or national emergency.

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
William H: Draper, Jr.
二3
September 22, 1948
In implernenting this national policy we envision a merchant
fleet of modern combination and cargo vessels under the United
States flag manned by Anerican officers and seanmen, transporting all
of our domestic coastwise and intercoastal and territory commerce
and at least one-half of our imports and exports, and backed by a
fleet of modern passen or-carrying vessels providing transportation
and conmmunication betveen the United States and important world
areas. his in effect is the target hich is in the minds of certain
American shipping interests and we believe of a substantial portion
of the American public.
We subnit that it is not an overarbitious policy to envision
such an merican merchant fleet. The carriage of one-half of our
on coninerce is a oal which has not, however, been reached since the
early Cclonial days when our forebears exceeded this achievement by
building and oporating the finest type of vessels the world had
theretofore kmowm.
During thc period prior to World War II the American Mer-
chant .arine was transporting less than 30% of our ovm imports and
exports. During the typical period 1930-1934, which apparently has
been selected to measure the level of Japanese restoration, the Jap-
anese merchant marine was transporting 65g of its farseas exports
and imports, 92 of its nearseas and 100% of its domestic commerce.
While contemplating the necessities of our erstwhile enemies and
with every hunane and compassionate feeling, it would seem to us
paradoxical to elevate those who have recently been restrained by
supreme military effort and who were important factors in disturbing
the peace of tho world to a position in world shipping relatively

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.98)
William H. Draper, Jr.
一4一
September 22, 1948
superior to that enjo: ed by our own American ships prior to World
War II, or in the foreseeable future.
Ve recognize your statenent, ir. Secretary, that strenuous
measures will be necessary to restore to Japan reasonable living
and e conomic standards. We suggest that it is not an easy accom-
plishment in other parts of the world. .e suggest consideration
as to whether we may with equity and juctice, and at our expense,
elevate the living standards of our recent enemies to a higher
level than those of our allies in the same area during the same ccr-
flict.
There appears to be a misconception regarding the histori-
cal earnings of the Japanese merchant fleet. A Departrnent of Com-
merce publication shows that in 1937 the ratio of Japan net shipping
receipts to its exports was 4.7%. This is to be compared with the
ratio of 36.5% for Horway; 24.1% for Greece; 13.4% for the United
Kingdom; 9.2% for the Hethe lands and 7.1% for Denmark. We use
the year 1937 as the only record available to us. We assume it to
be representative.
Hore important, hovever, is the ratio of Japanese net incoms
from shipping to the total Japanese national income. The sane
source shows that net shipping incoe represented less than 1% of
the total national income of Japan for the year 1937. To be exact,
the figure is 0.T. This may be compared with the shipping income
ratio to national incono of other naritime nations as follows:
Norway 11.2%; Denaark 2.5%; Netherlands 2.4%; United Kingdom 1.3%;
and Sweden 1.1%. It is, therefore, obvious that the item of net

September 22, 1948
William H. Draper, Jr.
Assistant Secretary of the Army
Department of the Army
Washington 25, D. C.
Subject: Japanese Merchant Marine
My dear Mr. Draper:
Referring to my letter of May 26; your response of June 1,
and to our subsequent conversation with respect to postwar plans
of Japanese merchant shipping.
As set forth in our letter of May 26, and as I stated to
you at the time of our meeting, American steamship owners are con-
siderably concerned about our national policy, and perhaps the
matter of an internaticnal policy, respecting the future of Jap-
anese shipping in the international trades. You were kind enough
to advise me that no de inite policy from a national standpoint
had been decided upon, and you invited expressions from American
steamship owners represented by this Federation concerning such a
policy.
The purpose of this letter is to present such views as are
presently in the minds of the American steamship owners we represent,
comprising about two-thirds of all privately owned American flag
ships.
We understand there is no certainty at the moment as to
where the final decision will be reached, i.e., whether by the
Department of the Army; the Supreme Commander of Allied Powers;

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.98)
William H. Draper, Jr.
- 2 -
September 22, 1948
the Far Eastern Commission; or some other American governmental or
international tribunal. We also understand that if some agency.
other than the Department of the Army is to determine. these ques-
tions you will advise us so that our representations may be made
before the determinative body.
In the first iistance, may we say that the interest we
represent are not unmindful of the task which confronts the United
States and/or the Allied Nations endeavoring to restore the Jap-
anese national to a level of reasonable living standards and of
reasonable economic stability. We are equally clear, however, that
the effect of these efforts upon our own national economy and upon
those of the Allied Nations cannot be ignored.
As stated to you, American shipping interests have no ambi-
tion to monopolize Pacific Ocean shipping. The United States has
never attained a maritime position commensurate with its geographic
and economic status, and as a leader in the United Nations Organi-
zation. No nation which has failed to maintain its flag upon the
seas has long retained a position of influence and leadership in
world affairs. History is replete with examples to the contrary.
The Congress of the United States in passing the Merchant
Marine Act of 1936 st ted it to be a national policy that the United
States shall have a merchant marine to carry its domestic water-
borne commerce, a substantial portion of its foreign commerce, and
capable of serving as a naval and military auxiliary in time of war
or national emergency.

=== Page 285 === (confidence 0.98)
William H. Draper, Jr.
- 3 -
September 22, 1948
In implementing this national policy we envision a merchant
fleet of modern combination and cargo vessels under the United
States flag manned by American officers and seamen, transporting all
of our domestic coastwise and intercoastal and territory commerce
and at least one-half of our imports and exports, and backed by a
fleet of modern passenger-carrying vessels providing transportation
and communication between the United States and important world
areas. This in effect is the target which is in the minds of certair
American shipping interests and we believe of a substantial portion
of the American publi.
le submit that it is not an overambitious policy to envision
such an American merchant fleet. The carriage of one-half of our
own commerce is a goal which has not, however, been reached since the
early Colonial days when our forebears exceeded this achievement by
building and operating the finest type of vessels the world had
theretofore known.
During the period prior to World War II the American Mer-
chant Marine was transporting less than 30% of our own imports and
exports. During the typical period 1930-1934, which apparently has
been selected to measure the level of Japanese restoration, the Jap-
anese merchant marine was transporting 65% of its farseas exports
and imports, 92% of its nearseas and 100% of its domestic commerce.
While contemplating the necessities of our erstwhile enemies and
with every humane and compassionate feeling, it would seem to us
paradoxical to elevate those vho have recently been restrained by
supreme military effort and who were important factors in disturbing
the peace of the world to a position in world shipping relatively

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.98)
William H. Draper, Jr.
- 4 -
September 22, 1948
superior to that enjoyed by our own American ships prior to World
War II, or in the foreseeable future.
We recognize your statement, Mr. Secretary, that strenuous
measures will be necessary to restore to Japan reasonable living
and economic standards. We suggest that it is not an easy accom-
plishment in other parts of the world. We suggest consideration
as to whether we may with equity and justice, and at our expense,
elevate the living standards of our recent emenies to a higher
level than those of our allies in the same area during the same con-
flict.
There appears to be a misconception regarding the histori-
cal earnings of the Japanese merchant fleet. A Department of Com-
merce publication shows that in l937 the ratio of Japan net shipping
receiptsto its exports was 4.7%. This is to be compared with the
ratio of 36.5% for No: vay; 24.1% for Greece; 13.4% for the United
Kingdom; 9.2% for the Netherlands and 7.1% for Denmark. We use
the year 1937 as the only record available to us. We assume it to
be respresentative.
More important, however, is the ratio of Japanese net income
from shipping to the total Japanese national income. The same
source shows that net shipping income represented less than 1% of
the total national income of Japan for the year 1937. To be exact,
the figure is O.7%. This may be compared with the shipping income
ratio to national income of other maritime nations as follows:
Norway 11.2%; Denmark 2.5%; Netherlands 2.4%; United Kingdom 1.3%;
and Sweden l.1%. It is, therefore, obvious that the item of net

Japanese people will react to their new environment. Past history
and statistics of a completely different governmental and economic
system provide no reliable guide. It would be a rash policy to use
past milestones to measure future progress, and to plot a completely
theoretical course under which Japanese agriculture, industry and
commerce would develop.

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.98)
William H. Draper, Jr.
● 9 -
Soptember 22, 1948
While the Japanese people must learn something of the mean-
ing of retribution, and measures must also be adopted to prevent
their again disturbing the peace of the world, every element of
the American people favor a humane and realistic course under which
by industry and intelligence they may regain a properly balanoed
position in world affairs wd th reasonable living standards. All
this must, however, be accomplished with a full realization of its
impact and effect upon our own financial and economic affairs.
Sound judgment would dictate a cautious procedure.
To say in what industries and to what extent the new order
in Japan is most adaptable, and would be most successful, appears
precarious. Because he Japenese merchant marine of 1930-1934 con-
sisted of about four million gross tons, to now accept as sound
policy a recommendation advanced by certain survey authorities that
a merchant marine of equal size should forthwith be provided, com-
pletely ignores the historical background and changed Japanese and
world conditions, and goes far into the field of simple conjecture.
We suggest that there is no reliable information to indicate
the size and volume of future Japanese exportable surpluses and
that the statistical records of past production of factory, mines
and agriculture furnish no reliable yardstick of the commodities
and of the volume which may be available in the future for shipment
overseas. Nor is there any dependable data as to what foreign mar-
kets are available if such production can be accomplished. To
arbitrarily set up a Japanese shipping fleet of a given capacity
with so little knowle ge of the future industrial development of
Japan would be an unwise course to pursue.

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)
William H. Draper, Jr.
-10 -
September 22, 1948
The merchant marine policy should rather be integrated
with the overall industrial development as it occurs, taking into
account the many national and international complications. When
the Ear Eastern Commission on January 23, 1947 decided as a matter
of policy that the peacetime standard of living of the Japanese
people should be set at the level prevailing during the years
1930-1934, they specified that acceptance of this policy should not
be interpreted to mean acceptance in advance of a specific level
for any particular industry. There is some justification in seleo-
ting these years as the statistical record shows evidence that it
was just prior to extraordinary expenditures for military purposes.
Japanese industries which must largely furnish export sur-
pluses not only suffered by bombing and other war activities, but
the sources of raw material and fuel with which to feed these indus-
tries have not been reestablished. Therefore if a fleet of four
million gross tons (1.7 million in farseas trading carrying 65%
of Japanese imports and exports) under conditions existing in 1930-
1934 was sufficient to meet Japan's waterborne transportation needs,
it would appear obvious that some lesser amount wdll suffice until
Japanese export production has been restored and overseas markets
developed.
Perhaps because shipping is in an exposed position and its
activities are more visible, there is a tendency to resort to it as
a means of supplying anything which is lacking in the make-up of
economic problems. T.e effect upon our economy and the resulting
effect upon our merchant marine appears to have been overlooked.

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.99)
William H. Draper, Jr.
-11 -
September 22, 1948
The occupational forces in Japan who waited so long for United
States merchant ships to bring them supplies and trensportation
when they were driving the Japanese from the territories which they
had invaded, should be the first to recognize the vital necessity
of maintaining an adequate active merchant marine fleet at all
times.
It had not occurred to us until reading certain of the
reports prepared for the Department of the Army, and made available
for public information, that the Japanese merchant fleet of the
future would be allowed to engage to a substantial degree in world
trading. e had gained the impression that Japanese ships of the
future would carry the domestic commerce of those islands and be an
important factor in the short trades namely, between the homeland
and Korea, Formosa, Philippines and the immediate Asiatic mainland.
Ve thought there would be very careful restrictions against allow-
ing Japanese tonnage in the long overseas routes to the Western
Hemisphere and European destinations, and perhaps to some degree
to S. E. Asia. The experiences of disturbing incidents caused by
Japanese activities i. foreign areas seemed to us to support logi-
cally such a viewpoint.
For the reasons we have endeavored to present, American
steamship owners who are members of this Federation respectfully
ask that recommendations which have been made for the immediate
rebuilding of the Japanese merchant fleet to a level equal to that
of 1930-1934, namely, approximately four million gross tons, be
deferred until there is better evidence that such action would be
in conformity with a sound national and international policy.

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
William H. Draper, Jr.
-12 -
September 22, 1948
Among the reasons why ve believe such an increase in Japan-
ese shipping at this time would be unsound, are the following:
1. We suggest that the peace of the world would best be
served by a policy which restricts Japanese shipping primarily to
the domestic Japanese trades and to service between Japan and
nearby continental and island areas.
2. That Japanese shipping net income as represented by
its ratio to net national income, plays a small part in the overall
Japanese economy, representing less than 1%. Therefore, it is not
an effective means of balancing Japanese finances or of raising
living standards.
3. We question the justification of raising Japanese par-
ticipation in international shipping to a relatively higher level
than is enjoyed by the United States, particularly when such stimu-
lation of Japanese economic recovery must be at our expense.
4. We question whether it is fair and just, at our expense,
to improve the economic stability and living standards of our recent
enemy to a point substantially superior to that of our recent allies
in the same geographic area.
5. Due to complete changes in government, physical condi-
tions and psychology, the past achievements and statistical records
of Japan are not reliable guides to the future production, overseas
commerce and finances of the nation. No one can foresee the future
of Japanese industry as related to world trade. We su gest it
would be incautious to provide Japan at this time with a merchant
marine of a given capacity upon a purely conjectural theory that
certain tonnages in imports and exports will develop. The general

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
William H. Draper, Jr.
- 13-
September 22, 1949
and gradual improvement of a cross-section of Japanese industry is
a more logical approach and as has been shown will better provide
the means of meeting any financial deficit.
6. Japanese shipping although built and operated by cheap
labor has not been required by its government to compete upon a
free trade basis in international commerce, but has been substan-
tially subsidized. Japan has not followed the principle set down
by the U. S. in the Merchant Marine Act of 1936 of making subsidy
payments only in an amount which will offset the low wages and
operating costs of competing foreign vessels thereby establishing
a condition of economic parity in competition.
When the subject matter of this letter is considered by
the Department of the Army, by the Far Eastern Commission or by
other national or international agencies, this Federation will
greatly appreciate the opportunity of presenting further considera-
tions.
Very truly yours,
Frazer A. Bailey

PROTEST AGAINST RESTORATION OF JAPANESE MERCHANT MARINE
On Cctober 8, 1948, the shipping representatives from the various
districts of China assenbled at the Second Annual National Convention held
in Shanghai to discuss numerous problems relating to shipping and their
attention wasdrawn particularly to the question of restoration of Japanese
merchant marine.
It will be noted that in your letter sent on September 22, 1948 to
Maj. Gen. William . Draper Jr., Assistant Secretary of Army, you filed six
points protesting against the so called Johnston Report proposing to restore
Japanese Merchant fleet to 4,CC0,CC0 tons. Your timely and far-sighted
protest has elicited no small amount of universal applause; a resolution is
therefore passed at the National Convention to give active and effective
support to your protest by calling the attention of our respective Governments
concerned to the serious consequences, which will inevitebly follow, should
the Japanese merchant marine be resuscitated.
Considering the heavy losses sustained during the World War II by
the llies, we cannot but attribute the holocaust to the power of the Japanese
merchant fleet which enabled her to wage the war of aggression and it is only
natural to take precautionary measures restricting their tonnage so as to
prevent fut ure resurgence.
In connection with Japanese reparations problems We noticed that in
May 1947, the Pauley Plan reconmended the retention by Japan of 1,500,000 tons
which, in the opinion of com et ent observers, is considered too lenient in
view of the fact that 5c0,ooc tons would be enough for her domestic commerce
and that 1, Cco, Coc tons would robably be more than double her actual needs.
However, in Mareh 1948, r. lifrord Strike submitted to the Assistant Secretary
of Mar a report proposing an increase of Japanese merchant tonnage to 3,000,000
tons, thus draving strong protests from the various quarters of the earth who
were participants of the War II. How to the surpris an ammzement of all the
worla, the Draper Plan as compiled by Mr. P. Johnston gives recommendation for
restoring Japan's tonnage to the prenr level of ,, tons! Needless to
say, this is merely a proposition ani not a decided policy, the formulation of
which will take into account the mutual and conmion interests of the Allies,
particularly Chine, which cannot be oblivous to the terrific suferrings and
devastations wrought during the var that has just ended. And how can the
American Government and people forget the Pearl Harbour?
500-37-10

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
The Chinese nation, being peace-loving and magnanimous, has no
inclination towards revenge and does not intend to lower the standard
of living of the Japanese people. In the interest of permanent world
peace, however, we beg leave to put forward the following concrete
proposals:
(1) To maintain the spirit of the Potsdam Declaration by restricting the
Japanese merchant fleet to one million tons.
(2) The activities of Japanese ships shall be restricted to her coastal
trade excluding them from overseas commerce which will be solely
undertaken by the Allied Powers.
(3) The surplus tonnage and shipbuilding equipments shall be apportioned
among the Allies as reparations; the Chinese claim for half million
tons of Japanese ships should be approved forthwith. It is to be
noted that not a single ton has so far been handea over to China.
(4) The economic recovery of the Allies is deemed more important than
Japanese recovery; the Chinese shipping, now only 1,200,000 tons,
falls far short or her normal requirements. China, therefore, has
good reason to claim the assistance and cooperation of the United
States in her program of reconstruction.
(5) Early convocation of Peace Conference with Japan for the reasonale
settlement of various outstandin problems such as reparationsJapans
industrial requirements and her merchant marine restrictions.In the
latter case we are definitely opposed to the "Strike" and "Draper"
plans.
The Chinese shipping representatives as assembled here
are
resolved to exert themselves in the promotion of their shipping industry
with the assistance of friendly nations in order that the standard of
living of the Chiuese people may be improved and that Chinese economic
reconstruction may be accomplished. China, beins her ally in war, has
many interests in common with the United States. The tragic error of
the British foreign olicy vis-a-vis Germany following the World War I
is a stern lesson to the resent policymakers. The Memoirs of Cordell
Hull has already brought into light the mistakes comnitued by the United
States in underestimating the strength of Japan. We, therefore, in all
sincerity an. truth, endorse and support your protest against
the
retoration of Japanese merchant marine, based as it is on the deep and
clear understanding of the question at issue. There can be no doubt that
the Chinese and American peoples engaged in shipping business will in
their tracitional cordiality and cultural inter-relationships cooperate
to fight for the reservation of justice end peace, for the welfare of
the two countries, and for the prevention of future dang rs of aggression.

On behalf of the National Association of Chinese Ship-

Federation of American Shipping protesting US policy of
restoring the Japanese Merchant Marine. The letter argues
that Japan's ships be restricted to coastal trade, that
Japanese shipbuiiding equipment be made immediately available
as reparations, that the Japanese fleet be reduced to one
million tons.
FE action on this despatch presumably calls for trans-
mission of this letter to the Federation. However, I can
see no justification for the transmisslon through Us Govern-
ment channels of a letter from an association in China to a
private federation in this country. In this case it 1s
particularly inadvisable in view of the Chinese Association's
position which is inimical to our interests but which might
be regarded by the American Federation as sympathetically
viewed by the State Department, inasmuch as the latter grat-
uitously undertook to transmit the letter.
I recommend that the letter be returned to Amembassy,
Nanking, with instructions that private correspondence not
be sent through Department channels. I have checked this
point with Mrs. Kent of S/S-CR. Action should be taken by CA.

(COPY)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
1330
28 October 1948
SCAP REPORTS PROGRESS IN REPAIR OF MERCHANT MARINE
SCAP transportation officials report that considerable pro-
gress has been made by the Japanese in the conversion and repair
of the Japanese Merchant Marine Fleet during the three years of
Occupation. Prospects for continued recovery have been bright-
ened by the return of vessels to private owners.
The achievements of the past three years are a major step
in the right direction, but there remain many problems still to
be resolved. The Japanese Merchant Marine today is made up of
a conglomeration of over-age vessels and vessels built during
the war, when numbers rather than quality counted. As a matter
of expediency vessels without propulsion have been utilized
under tow.
The Japanese Fleet which previous to the war had only 10
percent of the Merchant Fleet in a repair status at any one time,
an average which was high in comparison with that of other mari-
time nations, now averages between 25 and 30 percent of its fleet
under repair. Factors other then the physical condition of the
fleet, which have contributed to this high repair ratio, have
been lack of personal supervision by owners, inexperience of
crews, limited materials and power shortages.
The Japanese Government, with the assistance of SCAP trans-
portation officials, developed a program to solve the numerous
problems posed by the condition of the fleet. A Repair Commit-
tee was formed, comprising SCAP and Japanese officials, to con-
sider and make recommendations for a shipping and shipyard pro-
gram to reduce the ratio of operable merchant vessels under re-
pair at any one time. The present goal calls for repair ratio
of anproximately 15 percent of the commercial fleet and, if a-
chieved, will provide for vessel utilization of loz months
annually. The Committee is studying the means whereby the time
expended in intermediete docking end on annual inspections will
be reduced from the presently required one month period.
As another constructive step it has been proposed that ves-
sels which are to be returned to private owners be first placed
in an operable condition. After return of the vessels it is
anticipated that the owners, because of their great interest in
keeping vessels at sea, will do much to decrease the present
repair ratio.
Salvage has also played an important part in the rebuilding
of the Japanese Merchant Marine. Salvage operations have re-
refloated

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.97)
RESTRICTED
Enclosure to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 718
November 9, 1948.
-2-
refloated a majority of the vessels sunk during the war. Two-
thirds of these have been returned to operation and those re-
meining are being repaired on an established schedule. Concom-
itantly with the salvage program. a major repairs program for
laid up vessels was undertaken and is nearing completion.
All programs of the Japanese Government, with respect to
the Merchent Marine, are aimed at one major objective-that of
developing a fleet capable of supporting the minimum require-
ments of the Japanese economy.

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.98)
Brief of and Commentary on
National Federation of American Shipoing Letter of
22 Sep 1948 Addressed to Ass't Secretary of Army Draper
Paras 1 - 4 inclusive. U.S. steamship owmers are concerned
with postwar policy on Japanese shipoing, and desire to present the
views of U.S. private owners for consideration by that agency,
(Dent of Army, SCAP, FEC, or other) which will determine final policy.
Comment. None
Par 5. U.S. ship o ners are mindful of the difficulty of restoring
Japanese economy to reasonable stability but are firm in maintaining
that effects on U.s. national economy of efforts to restore.Japanese
economy mu t be consiered.
Comment. None.
Pars 6 - 7. U. S. has never attained the eminence in the shipping
world comparable to U.S. zeogra hic and economic status. Congress has
specified
as a na tional poliey thet U.s. Merchant marine should carry
domestic waterborne cargo, a substantial portion of foreign commerce, and
b capable of servin as a novel ana military auxiliary in time of war or
national emergency.
Comment. The restriction on American ship ing in foreign commerce
has been imposed by the inability to compete on a cost basis, and to
restrictions imposed by other governnents.
894.8
Pars 3 - 9. In implementation of this policy, U.S. ship owners
envision a U.. merchant fleet to carr all coastal, intercoastal, and
territorial commerce, and at least one-half imports and exports. Backing
this cargo fleet should be a fleet of modern passenger vessels providing
transportation and communication between U.S. and important world centers.
Comment. Concur as far as coastwise and intercoastal shipoing is
concerned. Reference to territorial commerce is not clear. This could
include Alaska, Porto Rico, T. H., or wider scope including islands such
as Pacific islands.
Par 10. Prior to "orld War II, U. S. vescels were carrying less than
30 percent of our onn imports and exports. Durin typical period of
1930-34 Japan was carrying 65% of far-seas exports and imports, 92% of near-
seas, and 100% of domestic comnerce.
Comment. U.S. like Japan was carryin 100% of its domestic commerce
(coastwise and intercoastal), and on the balance of the seaborne trade,
each country competed on the basis of its ability to furnish sercice at an
acceptable price within the limits imoosed by interested governnents.
COPY

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
Par ll. Acknowledges difficulties of restoring stable and
reasonable economy in Japan, but suggests consideration whether U.S. may
with equity and ju tice elevate Japanese living standards above those of
our former allies.
Comment. U. S. has undertaken to establish in Japan a stable self-
upporting government. The comparison of Japan to any other oriental people
is not part of the discussion.
Pars 12 - 13. a. There is a misconception regarding historic
earnings of the Japanese fleet. In 1937, the ratio of net ship ing
receipts to exnorts was 4.7% while ratio of net income from shipuing to
total Japanese national income was 0.7%
b. Ihese figures are so low that U.S. shipowners feel that
raising general productivity of Japanese industry is a more feasible approach
to balancing the budget than xploitation of shipoing capabilities.
Comments. a. First, "net shioping receipts" is not the best
measure of contribution of Japanese shipoin. In an economy deficient in
raw materials, surfeit with labor, and with adequate power, the prime re-
quisite is for foreign exchange to buy raw materials. The importance of
Japanese shipping to the economy can then be measured simply in terms of
combined savinrs and earning in foreign exchange. A Department of Commerce
study ("Japan Trade Studlies. The Shipoing Industry of Japan" prepared
for the Enemy Branch, Foreign Economy Administration, by J. D. Travis, U.S.
Tariff, Commission dated July 1945) indicates thst in 1934, if the Japanese
merchant fleet had not been permitted to engage in foreign trade, Japan
Would have been required to balance her import-export program additional
foreign exchange amounting to 5% of the value of the import-export progran.
Since the import and export prograns are of about equal value, that means
that without a merchant fleet plying in far-eas trade, Japan would have been
10% short of foreign exchange required to support the import progran.
b. With respect to increasing overall national productivity 0.7%
in order to balance the budget, it is to be noted again that the require-
ment is not for increase in national productivity, but for increase in foreign
exchange. Any reduction in potential earnings from shipping, or increased
recuirement for foreign exchange to pay for Japanese goods moved in ships
other than Japanese, must be balanced by increased sale abroad of other
Japanese goods or services -- for example, the sale of textiles. It would
probably be difficult to find any means of increasing Japanese foreign ex-
change that will not impinge on some trades. Restricting shipping merely
throws the pressure on some other interest.
Pars 14 - 16 inclusive. Discuss balance of earnings fron Japanese
shipping and Japanese methods of comoutation thereof. The Japanese method
of valuing imports C.I.F. and exports F.A.S. is criticized as over-stating
any trade deficit by an amount ecual to total transportation charges on
imports.
2

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
Comment. None
Pars 17 - 18. Outline broadly the steps taken by Japan to subsidize
shipoing and shipbuilding from 1870 to the outbreak of World War II.
Comment. None
Par 19. Based on Japanese Economic Rehabilitation Plan, trade
deficit in 1952 is only 1l million dollars.
Comment. This figure is questionable as the above plan was
prepared by the Japanese with such haste that it is not acceptable to the
various agencies of their on governnent. A new study is presently being
made.
Par 20. Figures are quoted to show that present caloric intake
compares favorably with prewar figures.
Comment. This statement has little pertinent value when
consideration is given to the fact thst the Japanese economy is heavily
subsidiged by the U.S. and there is no desire or intent on the part of
the U.S. to maintain this subsidy indefinitely.
Pars 21 - 24. Review Japanese history and conclude that in view of
the Japanese new form of government and the establishment of new concepts
of human rights and liberty, the future is indefinite and the use of past
milestones to measure theoretically future progress would be a rash policy.
Cautious procedures are urged.
Comment. Although definite final form of government can not be
stated, the decision to establish a stabilized economy in Japan should not
be further delayed.
Pars 25 - 27. Outline the cuestionable value of extrapolating existing
inadequate dat into a future economic pattern, and suggest that Japanese
Merchant marine policy should be integrated with industrial development as
it occurs, with due consideration for national and international complications.
Comment. There is justification for the est blishment of a
Japanese Merchant Marine commensurate with the known need for earnings
resulting from foreign commerce.
Par 28. Since Japanese production of surpluses for export is below
the 1930-34 level, it is apparent thet a fleet smaller than the 1930-34
fleet of 4,000,000 gross tons is adequate for present conditions.
Comment. The implication that the size of the fleet should be tied
to surplus is an effort to impose irrelevant limitations and can not be
admitted. The size of the fleet should be based on Japanese economic need.
3

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
Par 29. Because shipping is finite, there is a tendency to over-
simplify and accept exploitation of shipping as a ready remedy for economic
ills. Overlooked is the effect on our own national economy.
Comment. If the U.S. could hope to monopolize the field vacated
by Japanese shipping, severe restrictions on Japanese shipping might be an
acceptable U.S. colicy. Since many other nations must share the results
of such a program, the U.S. can not expect significant benefits from restric-
tions on Japanese shipping.
Par 30. U.S. ship owmers had never considered that Japanese ships
Would be allowed to engage in world trading except in short trade routes
to Korea, Formosa, Philippines, and immediate Asiatic mainland. Knowledge
that certain reports prepared for Department of the Army propose less
restrictive trading areas is of grave concern to U.S. shipowners.
Comment. Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers has no knowledge
of a policy that establishes the ultimate areas of trade in which Japanese
vesels will be permitted to engage.
Par 31. For reasons outlined above, U.S. shipowners request action
looking toward building & Japanese merchant fleet of 4,000,000 tons be
deferred until thece is better evidence that such action is in conformity
with U.S. national policy.
Comment. While there has been no definite policy decision limiting
the size or composition of Japan's post-war merchant fleet, it is essential
in the interest of Japanese economy to establish an interim policy which
will permit a phased shipring rehabilitation program and allow Japan to
engage in a controlled, limited, far-seas trade.
Par 32. Summa ry of reasons why an increase in Japanese shipning is
considered unsound.
1. Peace of the world will best ve served by restricting Japanese
shipoing to domestic Japanese trade areas and to service between Japan and
nearby continental and island areas.
Comment. Ther is no indication that the size of the Japanese
merchant fleet will have any appreciable effect upon world peace. Japan
does not now appear to be a major factor in present or near future world
conflicts. As indicated elsewhere, no decision has been reached concerning
areas of linitations of the Japanese fleet.
2. "That Japanese shipping net income as represented by its ratio
to net national income, plays a small part in the overall Japanese economy,
representing less than 1%. Therefore, it is not an effective means of
balancing Japanese finances or of raising living standards."
4

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.99)
Comment. For Japan, the value of shipping must be rechoned
in terms of foreign exchange and not in terms of national income.
3. "Vie question the justification of raising Japanese partici-
pation in international shipping to a relatively higher level than is
enjoyed by the United States, particularly when such stimul&tion of
Japanese economic recovery must be at our expense."
Comment. The restricting of Japanese shipping will be at U.S.
expense, and the benefits therefrom, by and large, will be enjoyed by
other nations.
4. "Wie question whether it is fair and just, at our expense, to
improve the economic stebility and living standards of our recent enemy to
a point substantially superior to that of our recent allies in the same
geogrephic aree."
Comment. Japan should be given minimum reasonable living standards.
5. "Due to complete changes in government, physical conditions and
psychology, the past achievements and statistical records of Japan are not
reliable guides to the future production, overseas commerce and finances
of the naticn. No one can foresee the future of Japanese industry as related
to world trade. We sugee t it would be incautious to provide Japan at this
time with a merchant marine of a given capacity upon a purely conjectural
theory that certain tonnages in inports and exports wili develop. The
general and gradual improvement of a cross-secticn of Japanese industry
is a more logical aporoach and as had been showm will better provide the
means of meeting any financial deficit."
Comment. It is agreed th t specification of a fixed tonnage is
economically unsound. However, Japanese shipping should be permitted to
contribute its share to & stable economy.
While it is recognized any extrapolation is unsafe, neverthe-
less it is necessary that some decision be made to permit progress toward
stability.
6. "Japanese ship ing although built and operated by cheap labor
has not been required by its government to compete upon a free trade basis
in intern&tional commerce, but has been substantially subsidized. Japan
has not followed the principle set down by the U.S. in the Merchant Marine
Act of 1936 of making subsidy payments only in an amount which  ill offset
the low wages and operating costs of competing vessels thereby establishing
a condition of economic parity in competition."
Comment. What has occurred in the past when Japan was an
independent nation has no bearing on this discussion.

5
Subject: Imbalance between Movement of Freight Intra-Japan
by Rail and Water.
The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to rejer to
894.85/1
comments made in this Mission's despatch no. 655 of October 4,
1948 on the subject of "Japan's Need for an Overseas Merchant
Marine", regarding the present character of Japan's coastwise
shipping and the desirability of effecting an early return to
the pre-war practice of moving the majority of bulk cargoes
inter-island by water rather than by rail. In this connection,
extracts (unclassified) from a GHQ FEC Public Information Office
press release of January 1l, 1949, regarding statements on this
subject made by Mr. Edward B. Juliber, of the Civil Transportation
Section of this Headquarters, are enclosed.
4
6
Mr. Juliber stresses the need for a major revision of
Japanese freight rate schedules (particularly rail) and points
out that officially depressed freight rates have caused a serious
dislocation in the relationship. between freight haulage by rail
and by water. Whereas pre-war water freight rates were only about
60 per cent of comparable rail rates, Mr. Juliber states that
"today in some instances water costs to the shipper are more
than twice that of rail" and that this explains to a great extent
the overburdening of the railroads while marine shipping suffers
from short loading. This Mission understands that the disparity
is largely due to lighterage and handling charges at water
terminals rather than to excessive water freight rates. As is
indicated in the enclosure, neither marine nor rail freight rates
cover costs of operation.
The despatch cited above pointed out that bulk cargoes, such
as coal, ore, and lumber, which moved within Japan by water before
the war, and which by necessity had to be moved by rail as the
war progressed, are to a large extent still being carried by a
greatly overburdened rail system. This is largely for the reasons
indicated by Mr. Juliber in his press release.
DMR
During an informal conversation subsequent to the press
release, Mr. Juliber indicated to an officer of this Mission that
the Civil Transportation Section does not anticipate a major
increase
CONFIDENTIAL
 1949

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 44,
January 21, 1949.
-2-
increase in rail or water freight rates in the near future. It
is understood that the Economic and Scientific Section of this
Headquarters fears that if such rates are increased substantially
an upward adjustment of prices will be required and for that
reason prefers to hold freight rates down. On the other hand,
Mr. Juliber admits that the Japanese railways are carrying a
personnel overhead of two to three times the total number required
to operate the lines efficiently. (This problem, essentially
welfare in nature, is one that will also face Japanese industry
when effecting a reduction in operating costs at the time a
single rate of exchange for the Japanese yen is established).
This is not true, he states, in the case of coastwise shipping.
Inasmuch as the Civil Transportation Section does not foresee
a major increase in rail freight rates in the near future, this
Mission understands that that Section is considering the possibil-
ity of placing an embargo upon the movement of certain bulk items
by rail in order to correct the present imbalance existing between
freight haulage by rail and by water. If the imbalance is
corrected, Mr. Juliber has indicated to this Mission that he
believes that it will necessitate an increase in Japan's present
coastwise shipping tonnage.
This Mission considers Mr. Juliber's statements to be
pertinent, and believes that an early return to the pre-war
practice of moving the majority of bulk cargoes inter-island
by water is in the interest of Japan's domestic economy, although
necessitating thereby either an increase in rail and water freight
rates, or the application of an embargo to the movement of
specified bulk cargoes by rail, as well as an increase in Japan's
coastwise shipping.
Enclosure:
Extracts from Press Release,
dated January 11, 1949.

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 44 dated
January 21, 1949 from the United
States Political Adviser for Japan,
CONFIDENTIAL
Tokyo, on the subject, ""Imbalance
between Movement of Freight Intra-
Japan by Rail and Water."
EXTRACTS FROM A GHQ FEC PUBLIC INFORMATION
OFFICE PRESS RELEASE OF JANUARY 11, 1949.
(COPY)
Since the start of the Occupation, Japan's railway system
has increased its line haulage nearly 50 percent, Juliber stated.
He estimated that 130 million tons of freight were hauled during
1948 as compared with 84 million tons moved in 1945. Rails move
the equivalent of the entire Japanese population every four or
five days, he noted and remarked that the Japanese today are
running fewer trains than any time during the past ten years.
Because freight rates today are not compatible with values
of commodities, a major revision of rate schedules is needed,
Juliber averred.
"Depressing of rates during the war caused a disparity in
relationship between rates and commodity prices. This disparity
has been aggravated by the inflationary period Japan has seen
during the past three years. Officially depressed rates as
contrasted with increased costs of operation of the transportation
plant has brought it close to bankruptcy and has ceused serious
dislocation in the relationship between rail and water. Where
formerly movement of freight by water cost the shipper approximate-
ly 60 percent of that of reil, today in some instances water
costs to the shipper are more than twice that of rail. This
condition explains to a great extent the overburdening of the
railroads while marine shipping suffers from short loading,"
Juliber said.
Ships built during the war at high cost must earn high
freight rates to bring adequate returns on the investment they
represent, according to Juliber.
"Marine freight rates today do not cover the cost of
operation," he said.
"Rail freight rates no longer bear any sound relationship to
commodity prices," said Juliber. Freight is moved at a loss. For
every l00 yen expended only 46 yen are received back in the form
of revenue."

SUBJECT: Legal Aspects of "Ex-Enemy" Merchant Ships Calling at
United States Ports
1. Your reply of 25 October 1948 to our memorandum dated 18
August 1948 wdth respect to possible legal restrictions against
entry of Japanese merchant ships into United States ports stated
that although there were no legal restrictions to such entry, there
were policy considerations to be taken into account before any
decision was reached with regard to entry of Japanese ships. The
Department of the Army, in further exploration of this problem, has
not found any policy which would preclude entry of Japanese ships
into United States ports. If you agree that this tentative finding
of the Department of the Army is correct, please inform us. If
this tentative finding is incorrect, please inform us as to the
specific policies which preclude Japanese ships from entering
United States ports to discharge and take on passengers and cargo.
2. Receipt of your reply by the close of business on 26
January, if possible, would be greatly appreciated inasmmch as
this matter may be a subject of discussions by the Secretary of
the Army during his forthcoming visit to Japan.

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.96)
RESTRICTED
Executive Officer, Civil Affairs Division,
Department of the Army
Offiee of Assistant Secretary of State for Occupied Areas
Policy Aspects Regarding Entry of Japanese Merchant Ships
into United States Ports
Reference is made to your memorandum of 24 January 1949 in whi
you request that the Department of State advise your office if ther
are any policy matters which would preclude Japanese ships from
entering United States ports.
The rehabilitation of the Japanese economy is an important part
of our long-range policy, and the Japanese merchant marine was, and
will undoubtedly be, a significant factor in Japanese economie reviv
Implementation of this policy would imply eventual approval of the
entry of Japanese merchant ships into ports of the United States and
other nations. It is our feeling, however, that the time factor is of
primary importance and that such action can be contemplated at a
later date as our program of redevelopment of Japan unfolds, although
planning for such an eventuality should be undertaken now. n other
words, final approval of this action should be given as a part of
integrated overall planning and only after full public understanding
of the program to assure maximum support.
It is anticipated that the calling of Japanese merohant ships
at our ports in the immediate future would result in vigorous protests
on the part of U. S. interests. Any such action would be aggravated
by the present condition of unemployment that now exists with U. S.
seamen. The reaction of groups directly concerned should, however,
be considered in the light of the general public interest. Before
final approval on such action is taken, it is believed that there
should be consultation with labor and industry on this matter, as
well as a coordinated review of the problem by the interested agencies
of the U.S. Government and consultation with appropriate members of
the Congress and with other interested governments. A beginming
toward this coordination could be obtained by a referral of the
matter to the Interdepartmental Shipping Committee with the sug-
gestion that it consult with labor and industry.
152
H. V. Hulick
Executive Assistant to the

CONFIDENTIAL
Lt. Col. Handrick, Civil Affairs Division,
Department of the Arzy
Office of Assistant Secretary of State for Occupied Arees
Requirement that Allied Representatives Accompany Japanese-Owned
Vossels
Reference is made to our telephone conversation on the subjeet,
with particular reference to the request of sCAP in his C-67493 of
31 Janmary that the previous requirement that Allied representatives
894.8
accompany Japanese-owned vessels be terminated.
The Department of State proposes that a roply to SCAP's C-67493
might be made along the following lines:
State Department is of the opinion that Allied repre-
a
sentatives may be removed fron Japanese-owned tankers and
2-949
dry cargo vessels participating in agreed trade arrangoments
provided that prior approval is obtained from the govermment
at whose ports the vessels will call; otherwise, possibility
exists vessels might be detained under various local regula-
tions or seised under claim. In this connection, Japanese
still enemy nationals from viewpoint former Allied countries.
It is pointed out, for example, that in case of oil
arrangement with Bahrein and Saudi Arabia, those countries
were advised that Allied representatives would be aboard the
Japanese tankers, and that approval from those countries of
new arrangement would therefore be required. It is presuned
that SCAP will obtain the necessary governmental clearances
unless U. S. Government assistance specifically requested.
CS/JEC
It is presumed vessels will continue to fly the SCaJAP
flag and to operate under close SCAP control.
Harold W. Moseley
Cleoves in Drofs with!

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.97)
CONFIDENTIAL
TO
: Mr. Saltzman
DATE: March 1, 1949
Mr. Claxton
tile
sUBJECT: Restrictions on Japanese Shipbuilding and Shipping.
From a strictly legal standpoint there is no effective policy
restriction on SCAP's authority in using Japanese shipbuilding capacity
as he desires, nor is there any effective legal restriction on the size
of vessels which may be constructed.
From a moral standpoint, however, any decision to expand Japanese
shipping or shipbuilding capacity should take into account the intent
of the Far Eastern Commission to place restrictions on these industries.
But for the failure of the Far Eastern Commission to reach agreement
Central
DC/B
reparations and related problems, definite restrictions on shipbuilding
and the size of the Japanese merchant marine would undoubtedly be in
effect.
8
394.85/3-149
The following are the FEC approved policy decisions on this subje
which should be taken into account in considering any expansion of
Japanese shipping or shipbuilding capacity:
1. Policy on Interim Reparations Removals (FEC-O59/4),approved
by the FEC May 23, 1946,stated that there should be made available for
reparations removals under the interim program, that portion of Japan's
shipbuilding capacity which is in excess of that necessary to build
150,000 gross tons of merchant shipping annually and to repair a mer-
chant fleet aggregating 3,000,000 gross tons. This policy was quali-
fied by a statement to the effect that no facilities should be made
available for claim until such time as Japan's merchant fleet is
restored to a level which in the judgment of SCAP is sufficient to
meet the needs of the occupation. Implementation of this policy has
also been prevented by the failure of the FEC to agree on reparations
shares.
2. Policy Statement on Reduction of Japanese Industrial War
Potential (FEC-O84/21), approved August 18, 1947, provides for:
(a) A definition of plants and establishments building
merchant vessels above a size to be determined by the FEC as
"primary war facilities" which should not be reestablished
during the occupation and which should be made available for
claim as reparations.
(b) A definition of the steel merchant shipbuilding and
repair industry as a "war-supporting industry" and provides
that that portion which is in excess of that necessary to meet
Heo
the peaceful needs of the Japanese people, as defined by the c,
should be made available for removal as reparations.
19
52
CONFIDENTIAL
X 100171

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.96)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
are
(c) Similarly defined as a "war-supporting industry", the
merchant marine, fishing, whaling and cannery fleets, and pro-
vides for a similar definition to be made by the FEC with respect
to that excess which should be made available for reparations.
This FEC policy paper also provides that the capacity levels to be
decided upon by the FEC should constitute the permissible maxima in
the shipbuilding industry until the end of the present phase of the
occupation of Japan or until October 1, 1949, whichever is the earlier.
The FEC has not yet been able to reach agreement on any of the determina-
tions referred to in (a), (b), and (c), above.
However, there is pending before the FEC a policy proposal which
does attempt to define capacity and the permissible size of the merchant
marine, fishing, whaling and cannery fleets. This proposed policy
(FEC-297/10), dated November 2, 1948, provides for:
(a) A designation as primary war facilities those facilities
capable of building merchant vessels measuring more than 6,000 gross
tons and provides for the reparations removals of such facilities.
(b) Capacity in excess of the amounts specified below should

will sustain her economy and permit the exaction of just reparations in
kind, but not those which would enable her to re-arm for war".

The Department of the Army is of the opinion that as a matter
of considerable urgency the United States should determine its policy
with respect to Japanese shipping and shipbuilding.
Accordingly, an initial staff study on this subject has been
prepared which has already been informally discussed at an exploratory
Files

is inclosed.

National Security Council, it is proposed that the Departments of
State and Army form an ad hoc group to complete work on the matter
before an appropriate deadline.
