SECRET
JAPANESE MERCHANT MARINE
1. To determine the action which should be taken with respect
to reviving the Japanese merchant marine and opening foreign ports
consistent with (a) the accomplishment of the objectives as outlined in
the Potsdam Declaration, (b) the recent Economic Stabilization Directive,
and (c) NSC policy.
FACTS
2. The Potsdam Declaration of July 1945 which defined the terms of
Japan's surrender provided that Japan should be permitted:
(a) access to raw materials,
(b) that eventual Japanese participation in world trade re-
Fw8
lations should be permitted.
3. As a result of Japan's defeat in the war she was stripped of her
5
territorial possessions thereby losing the most important source of her
X
51
natural resources. The loss of these colonies has accelerated her need
51
for conversion of raw materials and home labor into finished exportable
goods. Japan at the present time is dependent for raw materials on:
f
(a) The four homeland islands which are poor in resources
and inadequate to meet the needs of her huge population,
(b) raw materials imported from the U.S. which in the final
analysis are paid for by the American tax payer because
of the postwar adverse Japanese balance,
(c) raw materials imported from other nations. Such imports
are excessive in cost because in many instances, the trans-
portation charges for shipping in foreign bottoms is
greater than the cost of the raw material.
4. Merchant Marine. The Potsdam agreement permitting Japan to have
SECRET

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
access to raw materials contains the inherent implication that Japan
shall be allowed the necessary ocean transportation to carry the raw
materials from foreign ports to the home islands. Since pre-war Japan
was a maritime nation, dependent largely for her existence on water
transportation and foreign trade, shipping occupied a vital position in
the following phases of Japanese economic life: (a) foreign trade, (b)
internal communications and transportation and (c) employment. Each of
D
these phases will be briefly discussed as follows:
(a) Foreign Trade. Available statistics show that in point of
value of Japanese trade, Japanese ships carried 74% of the export trade
and 63% of the import trade during the years 1930-34; and that during the
same period Japanese ships carried 64% of the total tonnage which entered
and left Japanese ports. In brief, Japanese ships carried 23,700,000 tons
or 65% of the total tonnage during the 1930-1934 period.
A major elenent in importance in Japan's foreign trade was the
income in foreign exchange which was derived from her carrying trade, e•g.,
Japanese ships making cargo hauls between India and South America. These
net shipping receipts made a significant contribution towards meeting
Japan's deficits in the commodity trade. According to the latest Blue Book,?
during the period 1930-34, net shipping receipts averaged approximately 119
million yen, a sum slightly in excess of the average deficit in commodity
trade of 1l3 million yen.
(b) Internal Communications and Transportation.
Ships in Japan are the major means of inter-insular com-
munications and transportation. Coastwise shipping provides important
SECREI

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
services in conjunction with the railway net system. In general, the
Japanese railroads specialized in passenger traffic. Coastwise shipping
carried the main portion of the heavy bulk cargo since the railroads are
not designed for handling vast quantities of freight over long hauls.
This can best be illustrated with respect to coal production in Kyushu
and Hokkaido, where, in order to c arry this coal to the principal con-
sumption centers during the 1930-34 period, shipping was utilized to
carry 43% or 14,500,000 tons of the nanual coal output. The estimated
overall tonnage carried between Japanese ports during the same period was
20,000,000 tons.
(c) Employment. With respect to employment both in the mer-
chant marine as well as shipbuilding, an estimated one million persons
and their families were dependent on the shipping industry for their
livlihood.
5. Because of the war, Japanese shipping was reduced from the 1941
peak of 6,329,000 tons to the present figure of approximately one and one-
quarter million tons--a tonnage equal to the total Japanese tonnage for
the year 1913. In the period 1930-34, Japan had a population of approxi-
mately 65 million and a merchant fleet of steel ships which had a ratio
of one gross ton for every sixteen persons in Japan. The estimated
Japanese population in 1953 will be approxinately 86 million persons,
yet the Japanese merchant marine has been reduced from the pre-war total
of three million gross tons to the present total of about one and one-
quarter million tons, a postwar ratio of one gross ton for every 68
persons.
6. The present carrying capacity of Japanese ships based on actual
1947 records show that the amount of tonnage carried between Japanese
SEGISH

=== Page 366 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
ports ranged from 850,000-950,000 tons monthly or an annual total of
11,400,000 tons probably less than half of Japan's requirements for
inter-island and eoastwise under conditions of a self-supporting Japanese
economy. Inter-island and coastwise shipping estimated at 20 million gross
tons per annum for 1930-34 period.
7. The present merchant marine fleet consists largely of substandard
ships built during the war and of pre-war vessels with an average age of
25 years. Many of these ships will be out of service within the next ten
years. It has been estimated that there are only eight Japanese vessels
capable of "far-seas" ocean voyages at the present time. Both of the
technical missions which were sent to Japan, the Overseas Consultants and
the Johnson Committee, recognized the vital importance of ship construction
to the Japanese economy and, therefore, recommended that the retention level
of ship building pacity should be increased.
8. At the present time there are no valid binding international
agreements which place definite limitations on Japanese shipbuilding
either for domestic or foreign account. A summary of the existent
policy decisions with respect to the level of shipbuilding industry to
be retained in Japan follows:
(a) FEC 059/4, 23 May 1946, "Interim Reparations Removals".
Shipbuilding stipulates that Japan's shipbuilding capacity (other than
naval ship yards) which is in excess of that necessary to build 150,000
gross tons of merchant shipping annually and to service and repair a
merchant fleet of 3 million gross tons should be made available for claim.
This is, however, limited by the provision that no facilities are to be
made available for claim until the Japanese merchant marine has been
-4-
SEGHE

=== Page 367 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
1.
restored to a level which, in the judgment of SCAP, would meet occupation
needs.
(b) U.S. Proposal to FEC
The United States made a proposal to the FEC on 9 April 1947
that the size of the merchant vessels to be constructed in Japan for the
Japanese merchant marine should not exceed 3,o00 light displacement tons
(later amended to 6,000 gross tons).
(c) FEC Policy, 14 August 1947, "Reduction of Japanese Industrial
War Potential".
Primary war facilities which were designed to include"plants
and establishments building merchant vessels above the size to be deter-
mined by the FEC" Should be impounded and machinery and equipment made
available for reparations. This policy was transmitted to SCAP as JCS
Directive No. 87. This policy as yet does not limit Japanese ship build-
ingcapacity because the provision in the policy referring to the deter-
mination of size by the FEC has never been agreed upon.
(d) Recent State-Army Reparations Discussions
The latest conferences between State and Army with respect
to Japanese reparations do not mention the gross tonnage of ship build-
ing capacity which is to be retained in Japan.
9. At the present time, there are no FEC policies or international
agreements limiting the size, number or speed of ships to be constructed
in Japan for their domestic use or foreign account. It should be noted,
however, that such limitations are included in U.S. proposal of 9 April
1947 to the FEC which is still before the commission. Also earlier SWNCC
policy (not yet rescinded) would make Japanese ships larger than 6,000
gross tons having a speed in excess of 15 knots available for reparations
SECRET

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
(SWNCC 236/43 and 236/48). Although it is highly important that the
U.S. should withdraw its policy proposal to the FEC, there is no appar-
ent necessity for SANACC rescinding the early SWNCC policy decisions
since adoption by the NSC of a statement on reparations has the legal
effect of automatically nullifying these early SWNCC policies.
10. There is a present need to reduce that portion of the present
Japanese adverse balance of trade which is being expended for the trans-
portation charges on both exports and imports being carried in foreign
(non-Japanese) vessels. An estimated twenty percent or $150,226,000 of
the total 1947 import value of $526,130,000 was paid to foreign shipping
for transportation charges. According to information in the "Blue Book"
with respect to the 1949 outlook for shipping, it has been estimated that
"only about 5 percent of Japan's imports will be carried on Japanese bottoms—
largely because of political factors militating against the entry of Japanese
shipping in foreign ports in any significant volume." It may be anticipated
that objections will doubtless be raised similar to those which have been
encountered in the passage of ECA legislation with respect to the use of
foreign bottoms for shipments of goods purchased with appropriated funds.
However, the issue must be joined. An enlarged and active Japanese merchant
marine is vital to restoring Japan to a self-supporting basis. The only
remaining alternatives are the continuance of Japan on a dole (financed by the
American taxpayer) or relinquishment of Japan to internal disorder.
11. A vital factor affecting the entire Japanese shipping problem
is the return by SCAP of Japanese shipping now under its control to private
operators. The latest statistics from SCAP, as of 4 April 1949, show
that 63l eargo vessels which were under compulsory barboat charterage
SECRET

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRE
to the Japanese government were released to the owmers on 1 April
(information with respect to the number of vessels still under control
is being requested from SCAP by cable). By this action ship owners for
the first time since 1942 have been given the responsibility for manning,
operating, supplying and repairing their own ships. Unless there is a
definite objection from SCAP, the process of returning such shipping should
be accelerated and completed at the earliest practicable time.
DISCUSSION
12. Revival of erchant Marine.
Japan needs Japanese operated ships to carry both internal and
international trade, exports and imports as a means of earning foreign
exchange. These ships must be either built, purchased or chartered. The fol-
lowing paragraphs are discussions of the comparable situation with respect
to each of these sugested methods.
13. Although there are no binding policy restrictions on ship con-
structions for either domestic or foreign account, sharper opposition may
 be anticipated from (a) other FEC nations, particularly the British (who
have already indicated their disapproval of Japanese ship construction for
foreign account in their aide memoire of 9 June 1948 to this government),
(b) domestic shipbuilding, merchant marine and union labor interests who
have steadily expressed their opposition through Congressional legislation
toward any resurgence of Japanese maritime power. (Examples of such
special interest pressure on Congress is the 1948 law prohibiting the
sale, loan or charter of any war built vessels to foreign citizens,
thus preventing the Japanese from chartering Liberty vessels which are
now idle. Another example of the fears which special interest exert
SEGRET

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
on Congress is Public Law 472 passed in April 1948 which stipulates that at
Jeast fifty percent of ECA aid must be transported in U.S. bottoms.)
Especially illustrative of opposition by special interest within this country
is the letter recently received by Mr. Draper from Mr. Frazer Bailey, President
of the National Federation of American Shipping (an organization claiming
to represent two-thirds of all privately owned American flag ships.) In this
letter, Mr. Bailey has outlined the views of American steamship owners with
respect to U.S. policy toward the future of Japanese participation in inter-
national trade. (Since the letter contains many of the arguments generally
advanced by special interests, the letter with SCAP's pertinent comments are
attached herewith as Annex #1). An additional advantage resulting from
initiating a ship construction program for domestic use which should receive
strong emphasis is that such a program will serve as means of increasing
employment and utilizing the present supply of available skilled labor and
facilities.
14. In arriving at a decision on our national policy with respect
to ship construction in Japan, both for domestic and foreign account, it
will be necessary to strike a balance between our interest in favoring U.S.
shipbuilding and U.S. merchant marine operations and national defense
(maintaining a adequately trained merchant marine) on the one hand and
our interest in the attainment by Japan of a status of self-support on the
other hand (achievement of which will reduce the tax burden on the U.S.)
Considering the problem of Japan's domestic shipping requirements; first,
it is certain that an increase in Japanese shipbuilding to meet the needs
of internal transportation is an immediate necessity in an area in which
the Japanese are primarily concerned and in which foreign shipping
interests have no concern. Therefore, there could be no justifiable
SECRET

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
opposition to initiating forthwith a domestic ship construction pgram
which will raise shipping to a level which will be adequate to meet the
needs of inter-insular trade.
15. United States interests probably are neither interested in this
trade nor in a position to compete on a profitable basis with Japanese ship
operators. If Japan is not prohibited to revive her merchant marine and
operate it in world trade: (a) the U.S. merchant marine will carry Japan's
foreign trade at a price in excess of the cost for which the Japanese or
other foreign nations could carry the same tonnage. This will repre-
sent an economic loss and will be reflected in a continuing tax burden
on the American people. (In effect, a subsidy to the U.S. merchant marine
for which the Japanese "get the blame" for elements of the high cost of
U.S. occupation). (b) Japanese foreign trade, if offered on a competitive
basis will result in such trade being carried by nations other than U.S
or Japan, thus strengthening a foreign power without benefit to the
"defense" fitness of the United States merchant marine or the full
utilization of Japan's most ample resource-labor.
16. The epartments of Army and State, by virtue of the latest approved
U.S. policy toward Japan contained in NSC 13/2, have the strongest possible
position and authority to approach and secure approval of other governmental
agencies which may be opposed to any proposed revival of Japanese maritime
power (or to act without making such approach and without such approval).
NSC 13/2 "Recommendations with Respect to U.S. Policy toward Japan"
specifically stipulates in the paragraph on economic recovery the following,
"Economic recovery should be stressed as one of the primary objectives of
United States policy in Japan for the coming period. It should be sought
through a combination of United States aid program envisaging shipments
SECBE

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
2
and/or credits on a declining scale over a number of years, and by a
vigorous and concerted effort by all interested agencies and departments
of the United States Government to cut away existing obstacles to the
revival of Japanese foreign trade, with provision for Japanese merchant
marine shipping, and to facilitate restoration and development of Japan's
exports. In developing Japan's internal and external trade and industry,
private enterprise should be encouraged . . ." This policy statement approved
by the President grants to the Department of the Army the necessary auth-
ority to initiate immediate action for the acceleration in the enlargement
of the Japanese merchant marine and of its operations in international trade.
17. A factor which will have an adverse effect on the willingness of
Japanese entrepreneurs to invest in construction of ships for domestic
use is the ever-present possibility that such ships may be demanded as
reparations at the Peace Conference. It is understandable that Japanese
entrepreneurs will be extremely reluctant to invest their money in build-
ing ships which may be allocated for reparations. One suggested solution
to allay any hesitancy in this regard, would be a guarantee by the Japanese
government to private Japanese entrepreneurs that in the event their ships
are removed for reparations, that such investors will receive yen compen-
sation for their losses. It is expected that a single Japanese exchange
rate will soon be in effect. The Japanese entrepreneur, therefore, will
八
have a greater expectation that his original investment can be returned
to him in a relatively stabilized currency.
18. The possibility of securing legislation which will permit the
loaning of war-built Liberty vessels to Japan should be explored throughly
as a means of rapidly reviving the Japanese merchant marine. Such loans
10
SECRE

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.98)
SEGRE
or charters are prohibited by law at the present time. Although informa
tion with respect to the exact number of such ships now idle is not avail-
able; it is believed that there is a substantial number which could be loaned
to Japan on a charter basis if that were legally permitted. Public Law 423,
80th Congress, 2nd Session prohibited the chartering of any warbuilt vessel
to any person who is not a citizen of the United States. This law which was
to expire on 1 March 1949 was extended until 30 June 1949 by Public Law 12
which was approved on 28 February 1949. Hearings before Congress will un-
doubtedly be held with a view toward additional extension of the law. If
possible, prior to any Congressional hearings, the Departments of Army and
State should meet with the Maritime Commission and secure their agreement
to an elimination of the provisions in the present law which prohibit
chartering of war-built wessels.
19. In view of latest U.S. policy on economic stabiligation and on
Japan, generally, and in view of the present situation in Japan, it ap-
parently is necessary to direct SCAP to arrange for maximum ship construction
for foreign account and for such construction for domestic account as can
be s cheduled under limitations of scarce supplies. Private Japanese in-
vestors should be encouraged to make investments in ship construction in
Japan for domestic account.
20. Entry of Japanese Ships into Foreign Ports. The United States
government should assist SCAP and the Japanese government in securing agree-
ments from other nations to allow Japanese ships to enter their ports and
should establish a precedent in this respect by allowing Japanese ships
to call at U.S. ports. Japan's rights with respect to foreign trade were
both recognized and guaranteed by the Potsdam Declaration which stated
-11-
SEGRE

=== Page 374 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
that Japan was to be granted access to, but not control of, raw materials.
(It should be emphasized that for the right of the Japanese to use their
vessels to secure access to raw materials is viewed by the Dept of the Army
as inclusive of the right to carry cargo to such ports and to compete for
cargo with the merchant fleet of any other nation.) Since the use of Japanese
vessels to secure access to such raw materials was not prohibited by the
provisions of the Potsdam Agreement, any decision which would prohibit Japan
from engaging in foreign trade with her maritime fleet, could be considered
as contrary to the Agreement. As an aid to securing other nations' approval
of Japanese ships calling at their ports, the United States mhould permit
Japanese ships to enter our ports since we cannot plead for port privileges
for the Japanese in the ports of other nations if we do not permit such entry.
21. The matter of Japanese entry into U.S. ports has been under dis-
cussion between the Departments of Army and State for several months. In
reply to an inquiry by the Department of the Army to the Department of
State in January 1949, requesting their views with reference to any policy
matters which would preclude Japanese entry, the State Dept replied that
the final approval of such entry should not be given until the question had
been referred to the Interdepartmental Shipping Committee, followed by sub-
sequent coordination with labor, industry, and other interested agencies
of the government. It was felt by representatives of the Civil Affairs
Division, that this recommendation should not be accepted, insofar as it
concerns the Interdepartmental Shipping Committee. That Committee is
believed to have been revived at the insistence of a former Assistant
Secretary of the Department of State as a means of acquainting him of
-12-
SEGRET

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
certain shipping activities, e spe cially thosedhandled by the Maritime
Commission. Referral of this question to that commission would imply
that it has authority to deal with the problem. In view of the recently
approved NSC policy toward Japan, the Departments of State and Army
should advise SCAP that Japanese ships may call at U.S. ports to take on
and discharge cargoes and passengers. Every effort should be exerted to
secure the agreement of other departments and of U.S. private interest
that such an arrangement is appropriate and necessary.
22. SCAP's authorization of continuous operations of Japanese ships
in and out of U.S. ports should be contingent on the following: (a)
establishment of a single Japanese Exchange rate, (b) completion of the
return to private control of Japanese merchant shipping now held by SCAJAP
and (c) assurance by the Japanese government that no aid, either direct or
indirect, will be given shipping interests so as to constitute unfair com-
petition with shipping interests of other maritime nations.
fngde.
SECRET

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
CONCLUSIONS
23. Japan probably will not be able to attain a status of self-
upport if denied the available means of increasing its shipping income!
to a maximum. To achieve the goal of maximum shipping income thers
must be a peaceful revival of the Japanese Merchant Marine and re-entry
by the Japanese in world trade on a competitive basis.
24. The United States should withdraw its proposals including
that of 9 April 1947 to the FEC which specified the size of the merchant /
vessels which should be removed as reparations. (The early SWNCC
policies need not be rescinded, since adoption by the NSC would serve
to nullify these early decisions.)
25. SCAP should continue his present policy of securing the
maximum number of contracts with foreign powers for the export of
ships constructed in Japan.
26. Ship construction for domestic account should be accelerated
and enlarged to include ships capable of operating on the "far seas"
(i.e. North and South America, Europe and Africa). SCAP should arrange
with the Japanese Government for the passage of legislation which will
serve to encourage entrepreneurs to invest in the construction of
merchant ships. Such investments should be safeguarded by guarantees
that in the event any ships produced by such construction are removed
as reparations that the owners will be compensated in yen for the actual
value of the ships if taken.
eliminate the present restrictions in Public Law 423 prohibiting charter-
ing of United States war built vessels to Japan.
SECRET

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
28. SCAP should be advised that, after establishment of the single
exchange rate, and after assurances have been received that Japanese
shipping will not engage in unfair competition with respect to other mari-
time nations, and the return of ships to private control, Japanese ships
should be permitted to call at United States ports to discharge and re
ceive cargo and passengers on a continuous basis as business demands
indicate.
29. The Department of State should assist SCAP in diplomatic dis
0k
cussions with other foreign nations toward securing agreement to the opera-
tion of Japanese ships in and out of their ports for general competitive
trade purposes.
RECOLMENDATION
30. That the Assistant Secretary of the Army take steps to establish
a State-Army working group to draft a policy on Japanese shipping and
shipbuilding which will cmbody the conclusions of this study for submission
to the ational Security Council.

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.99)
NATIONAL FIDERATION OF AMERICAN SHIPPING, INC.
1809 G Street, Northwest
COPY
Washington 6, D. C.
September 22, 1948
William H. Draper, Jr.
Assistant Secretary of the Army
Department of the Army
Washington 25, D. C.
Subject: Japanese Merchant Marine
My dear Mr. Draper:
1. Referring to my letter of May 26; your response of June 1, and
to our subsequent conversation with respect to postwar plans of Japanese
merchant shipping.
2. As set forth in our letter of May 26, and as I stated to you at
the time of our meeting, American steamship owners are considerably con-
cerned about our national pelicy, and perhaps the matter of an inter-
national policy, respecting the future of Japanese shipping in the
international trades. You were kind enough to advise me that no definite
policy from a national standpoint had been decided upon, and you invited
expressions from American steamship owners represented by this Federation
concerning such a policy.
3. The purpose of this letter is to present such views as are
presently in the minds of the American steamship owmners we represent,
comprising about two-thirds of all privately owned American flag ships.
4. We understand there is no certainty at the moment as to where
the final decision will be reached, i.e., whether by the Department of
the Army; the Supreme Commander of Allied Powers; the Far Eastern Com-
mission; or some other American governmental or international tribunal.
S
We also underatnd that if some agency other than the Department of the
Army is to determine these questions you will advise us so that our repre-
sentations may be made before the determinative body.
5. In the first instance, may we say that the interests we repre-
sent are not unmindful of the task which confronts the United States and/or
the Allied Nations endeavoring to restore the Japanese national to a
level of reasonable living standards and of reasonable economic stabilityl
We are equally clear, however, that the effect of these efforts upon our
2
own national economy and upon those of the Allied Nations cannot be ignored.
6. As stated to you, American shipping interests have no ambition
to monopolize Pacific Ocean shipping. The United States has never attained
a maritime position commensurate with its geographic and economic status,
and as a leader in the United Nations Organization. No nation which has
failed to maintain its flag upon the seas has long retained a position of
influence and leadership in world affairs. History is replete with ex-
amples to the contrary.
ANNEX #1

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.99)
7. The Congress of the United States in passing the Merchant Marine
Act of 1936 stated it to be a national policy that the United States shall
have a merchant marine to arry its domestic waterborne commerce, a sub-
stantial portion of its foreign commerce, and capable of serving as a
naval and military auxiliary in time of war or national emergenoy.
Comment -- Paras 6 - 7. The restriction on American shipping in
foreign commerce has been imposed by the inability to compete on a
cost basis, and to restrictions imposed by other governments.
8. In implementing this national policy we envision a merchant fleet
of modern combination and cargo vessels under the United States flag manned
by American officers and seamen, transporting all of our domestic coast-
wise and intercoastal and territory commerce and at least one-half of our
imports and exports, and backed by a fleet of modern passenger-carrying
vessels providing transportation and communication between the United States
and important world areas. This in effect is the target whidh is in the
minds of certain American shipping interests and we believe of a substantial
portion of the American public.
9. We submit that it is not an overambitious policy to envision such
an American merchant fleet. The carriage of one-half of our own commerce
is a goal which has not, however, been reached wince the early Colonial
days when our forebears exceeded this achievement by building and operating
the finest type of vessels the world had theretofore knowm.
Comment -- Paras 8 - 9. Concur as far as coastwise and inter-
coastal shipping is concerned. Reference to territorial commerce
is not clear. This could include Alaska, Porto Rico, T.H., or
wider scope including islands such as Pacific Islands.
10. During the period prior to World War II the American Merchant
Marine was transporting less than 30% of our own imports and exports.
During the typical period 1930-l934, which apparently has been selected
to measure the level of Japanese restoration, the Japanese merchant
marine was transporting 65% of its farseas exports and imports, 92% of
its nearseas and lo0% of its domestic commerce. While contemplating the
necessities of our-erstwhile enemies and with every humane and compassion-
ate feeling, it would seem to us paradoxical to elevate those who have
recently been restrained by supreme military effort and who were important
factors in disturbing the peace of the world to a position in world ship-
ping relatively superior to that enjoyed by our own American ships prior
to World War II, or in the foreseeable future.
Comment -- Para 10. U. S. like Japan was carrying l00% of its
domestic commerce (coastwise and intercoastal), and on the bal-
ance of the seaborne trade, each country competed on the basis
of its ability to furnish service at an acceptable price within the
limits imposed by interested governments.
11. We recognize your statement, Mr. Secretary, that strenuous
measures will be necessary to restore to Japan reasonable living and
economic standards. We suggest that it is not an easy accomplisiment
in other parts of the world. We suggest consideration as to whether we
may with equity and justice, and at our expense, elevate the living

=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.99)
standards of our recent enemies to a higher level than those of our
allies in the same area during the same conflict.
Comment -- Para ll. U. S. has undetaken to establish in Japan
a stable self-supporting govefnment. The comparison of Japan to
say any other oriental people is not part of the discussion.
12. There appears to be a misconcention regarding the historical
earnings of the Japanese merchant fleet. A Department of Commerce public-
ation shovms that in 1937 the ratio of Japan net shipping receipts to its
exports was 4.7%. This is to be compared with the ratio of 36.5% for
Norway; 24.1% for Greece; 13.4% for the United Kingdom; 9.% for the
Netherlands and 7.1% for Denmark. We use the year 1937 as the only record
available to us. We assume it to be representative.
13. More important, however, is the ratio of Japanese net income
from shipping to the total Japanese national income. The same source
shows that net shipping income represented less than 1% of the total
national income of Japan for the year l937. To be exact, the figure is
0.7%. This may be compared with the shipping income ratio to national
inccme of other martime nations as follows; Norway 1l.2%; Denmark 2.5%;
Natherlands 2.4%; United Kingdom 1.3%; and Sweden 1.1%. It is, therefore,
obvious that the item of net shipping income is not the proper approach
to balancing the budget or increasing the living standards in Japan. It
should be borne in mind that this result was obtained when Japan was
carrying approximately 65% of her farseas import and export commerce.
It would appear that a more feasible method would be to raise the general
productivity of Japanese industries and that only a very small increase
is necessary to accomplish the necessary result.
Comment -- Paras 12 - 13. a. First, "net shipping receipts"
is not the best measure of contribution r Japanese shipping.
In an economy deficient in raw materials, surfeit with labor, and
with adequate power, the prime requisite is for foreign ex-
change to buy raw materials. The importance of Japanese ship-
ping to the economy can then be measured simply in terms of combined
savings and earning in foreign exchange. A Department of Com-
merce study ("Japan Trade Studies. The Shipping Industry of
Japan" prepared for the Enemy Branch, Foreign Economy Admini-
stration, by J. D. Travis, U. S. Tariff, Commission dated July
1945) indicates that in 1934, if the Japanese merchant fleet
had not been permitted to engage in foreign trade, Japan would
have been required to balance her import-export program addi-
tional foreign exchange amounting to 5% of the value of the
import-export program. Since the import and export programs
are of about equal value, that means that without a merchant
fleet plying in farseas trade, Japan would have been l0% short
of foreign exchange required to support the import program.
b. With respect to increasing overall national productivity
0.7% in order to balance the budget, it is to be noted again
that the requirement is not for increase in national productiv-
ity, but for increase in foreign exchange. Any reduction in

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.99)
in potential earnings from shipping, or increased requirement
for foreign exchange to pay for Japanese goods moved in ships
other than Japanese, must be balanced by increased sale abroad
of other Japanese goods or servides -- for example, the sale of
textiles. It would probably be difficult to find any means of
increasing Japanese foreign exchange that will not impinge on
some trades. Restricting shipping merely throws the pressure
on some other interest.
14. The balance of payments earnings from shipping as compiled by
the Japanese Department of Finance in the period 1930-1934 averaged 119.26
million yen per annum. This is the equivalent of 42.8l million dollars
per annum. Even this figure, as will be later shown, overstates the
balance of payments earnings because of the unusual and incorrect method
of reporting. Further it fails to take into account certain erpenditures
necessary to support shipping operations which must be made in foreign
currencies.
15. As an example: Japan is almost entirely devoid of domestic fuel
oil. When purchased in Japan by Japanese shipping, such expense has not
been adjusted against the net balance-of-payment earnings from shipping.
Obviously the expenses of acquiring such fuel oil abroad and importing it
into Japan for the use of Japenese ships should be deducted against the
balance of payments earnings from shipping.
16. In the past the Japanese have adopted a method of reporting
balance of payments under which imports are • valued CIF and exports are
valued FAS -- a procedure which distorts and overstates any balance of trade
deficit by an amount equal to the total transportation charges on imports.
This unucual procedure has resulted in errors by competent economists, in-
cluding some who have made surveys for the Army. For a more complete
study of what is termed the "chronic bias" in the Japanese balance of pay-
ments figures, we respectfully refer you to "The Industrialization of
Japan and Manchukuo, 1930-1940, by Schumpeter, Allen, Gordon and Penrose,
Pages 908 to 925.
17. The Japanese from the very beginning of their modern epoch
offered Japanese shipping something more than the opportunity to compete
in free trade with low cost labor. About sixteen years after Perry's
second bisit, the Mitsubishi Shipping Company was organized in 1870.
Government subsidies of 250,000 yen per annum were then granted. Addi-
tional government subsidies were granted other shipping companies as they
came into existence.
18. In 1896 the Japanese not only offered flat sum subsidies to
operators of foreign routes but also formalized into legislation a plan
for postal subventions and for shipbuilding subsidies in substantial
amounts. The postal subventions and the shipping subsidies were con-
tinued to World War II. The shipbuilding subsidies were discontinued
in 1918 but were again resumed in 1932. The Japanese also provided
indirect subsidies for their shipping by subsidizing their steel in-
dustry to the extent that it produced steel for ships, and be relieving
shipbuilding steel, shipbuilding tools, etc..from import duties. A
detailed explanation of these subsidies may be found on pages 3l9 to 351
- 4 -

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
of Shipping and Shipbuilding Subisidies published in 1932 by the U. S.
Department of Commerce.
191 The Japanese themselves do not estimate that they will need
any large income from shipping to balance their economy in "1952".
Their "Economic Rehabilitation Plan" has set exports of 1,646 million
dollars and imports of 1,657 million dollars as the goal for the year
"1952" even after the overstated method of valging imports CIF. Their
balance of trade doficit, although distorted by overstatement, is thus
only ll million dollars per annum, if their goals are reached.
Comment -- Para. 19. This fugure is questionable as the above plan.
was prepared by the Japanese with such haste that it is not acceptable
to the various agencies of their own government. A new study is pre-
sently being made.
20. Fearing the impression may prevail that the living standards
presently prevailing in Japan are more sub-standard than actually exists,
we feel it desirable to state a few high-light facts. The average daily
caloric intake in rural areas exceeded 2250 calories per day in November
1947, and about 5% less for the 1987 annual average, according to SCAP's
Summation of Non-Military Activities in Japan for May 1948. According
to the same source, the urban, areas average was 2oo0 calories per day
in the same month and averaged about 93% of that figure for the full year.
This is to be compared with an average daily caloric intake prewar of
approximately 2250 according to the calculations of the Japanese and 2000 for
1940 and l94l according to the calculations of the Medical Division of
the U. S. Strategic Bombing Survey. Our information is that the caloric
intake of Bizone Germany at the present time is between 2100 and 2300
calories per day and of France approximately 27o0 calories per day, ex-
clusive of alcoholic beverages. We consider this a most creditable show-
ing by the authorities in the occupation areas.
Comment -- Para. 20. This statement has little pertinent
value when consideration is given to the fact that the Japanese
economy is heavily subsidized by the U. S. and there is no de-
sire or intent on the part of the U. S. to maintain this sub-
sidy indefinitely.
21. The future position of Japan in world affairs and commerce
cannot be foreseen. Japanese authorities assure us that no one in
Japan expected the surrender to lead to a complete change in the form
of Government. They expected a constitutional monarchy. It must be
remembered that for more than 2o0 years prior to Commodore Perry's
visit in 1853 Japan's foreign commerce had been strongly banned and
the country had been living in a feudal state ruled over by the Tokugawa
Shogunate. The Emperor's authority was so far eclipsed that the first
American consul was ignorant of even the existence of such a personage
for two years after his arrival.
22. With the coming of Perry and the restoration of the Meiji
dynasty, Japan entered upon a new and entirely different phase of its
development. Inidividual loyalty was transferred from the feudal lords
- 5 -

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.99)
to the emporer. The succeeding regine was under a type of monarchial
control, and in a substantial degree industrial development wad dominatedsā
by cartels or family group holdings, in which it is thought that the
monarchy held financial interest. Through all these periods the in-
dividual was completely subservient to the State. Personal freedom and
dignity were not part of the Japanese order.
23. We now come to a new epoch in Japan's history with: the establish-
ment of constitutional democratic goverment and the concept of human
rights and liberty. No one can foresee how the Japanese people will re-
act to their new environment. Past history and statistics of a completely
different governmental and economic system provide no reliable guide. It
would be a rech policy to use past milstones to measure future progress,
and to plot a completelytheoretical course under which Japanese agriculture,
industry and commerce would develop.
24. While the Japanese people must learn something of the meaning
of retribution, and measures must also be adopted to prevent their again
disturbing the peace of the world, every element of the American people
favor a humane and realistic course under which by industry and intelli-
gence they may regain a properly balanced position in world affairs with
reasonable living standards. All this must, however, be accomplished with
a full realization of its impact and effect upon our own financial and
economic affairs. Sound judgement would dictate a cautious procedure.
Comment -- Paras 21 - 24. Although definite final form of
governement cannot be stated, the decision to establish a stabil-
ized economy in Japan should not be further delayed.
25. To say in what industries and to what extent the new order in
Japan is most adaptable, and would be most successful, appears precarious.
Because the Japanese merchant marine of 1930-1934 consisted of about
four million gross tons, to now accept as sound policy a recommendation
advanced by certain survey authorities that a merchant marine of equal
size should forthwith be provided and completely ignores the historral
background and changed Japanese and world conditions, and goes far into
the field of simple conjecture.
26. We suggest that there is no reliable information to indicate
the size and volume of future Japanese exportable surpluses and that the
statistical records of past production of factory, mines and agriculture
furnish no reliable yardstick of the commodities and of the volume which
may be available in the future for shipment overseas. Nor is there any
dependable data as to what foreign markets are available if such pro-
duction can be accomplished. To arbitrarily set up a Japanese shipping
fleet of a given capacity with so little knowledge of the future in-
dustrial development of Japan would be an unwise course to pursue.
27. The merchant marine policy should rather be integrated with
the overall industrial development as it occurs, taking into account
the many national and international complications. When the Far Eastern
Commission on January 23, 1947, decided as a matter of policy that the
- 6 -

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.99)
peaceful standard of living of the Japanese people should be set at
the level prevailing during the years 1930-1934, they specified that
acceptance of this policy should not be interpreted to mean acceptance
in advance of a specific level for any particular industry. There is
some justification in seledting these years as the statistical record
shows evidence that it was dust prior to extraordinary expenditures for
military purposes.
Comment -- Paras 25 - 27. There is justification for the
establishment of a Japanese Merchant Marine commensurate with the
known need for earnings resulting from foreign commerce.
28. Japanese industries which must largely furnish export sur-
pluses not only suffered by bombing and other war activities, but the
sources of raw material and fuel with which to feed these industries
have not been reestablished. Therefore if a fleet of four million gross
tons (1.7 million in farseas trading carrying 65% of Japanese imports
and exports) under conditions existing in 1930-1934 as sufficient to
meet Japan's waterborne transportation needs, it would appear obvious
that some lesser amount will suffice until Japanese export production
has been restored and overseas markets developed.
Comment -- Para. 28. The implication that the size of the
fleet should be tied to surplus is an effort to impose irretevant
limitations and cannot be admitted. The size of the fleet should
be based on Japanese economic need.
29. Perhaps because shipping is in an exposed position and its
activities are more visible, there is a tendency to resort to it as a
means of supplying anything which is lacking in the make-up of economic
problems. The effect upon our economy and the resulting effect upon our
merchant marine appears to have been overlooked. The occupational forces
in Japan who waited so long for United States merchant ships to bring
them supplies and transportation when they were driving the Japanese
from the territories which they had invaded, should be the first to
recognize the vital necessity of maintaining an adequate active merchant
marine fleet at all times.
30. It had not occurred to us until reading certain of the reports
prepared for the Department of the Army, and made available for public
information, that the Japanese merchant fleet of the future would be
allowed to engage to a substantial degree in world trading. We had
gained the impression that Japanese ships of the future would carry the
domestic commerce of those islands and be an important factor in the
short trades namely, between the homeland and Korea, Formosa, Philippines
and the immediate Asiatic mainland. We thought there would be very care-
ful restrictions against allowing Japanese tonnage in the long overseas
routes to the Western Hemisphere and European destinations,and perhaps
- 7 -

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.99)
to some degree to S. E. Asia. The experiences of disturbing incidents
caused by Japanese activities in foreign areas seemed to us to support
logically such a view point.
Comment — Para 30. Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
has no knowledge of a policy that establishes the utlimate areas
of trade in which Japanese vessels will be permitted to engage.
31. For the reasons we have endeavored to present, American steam-
ship owners who are members of this Federation respectfully ask that
recommendations which have been made for the immediate rebuilding of the
Japanese merchant fleet to a level equal to that of 1930-1934, namely,
approximately four million gross tons, be deferred until there is better
evidence that such action would be in conformity with a sound national and
international policy.
Comment — Para 31. While there has been no definite policy
decision limiting the size of composition of Japan's post-war mer-
chant fleet, it is essential in the interest of Japanese economy to
establish an interim policy which will permit a phased shipping re-
habilitation program and allow Japan to engage in a controlled,
limited, far-seas trade.
32. Among the reasons why we believe such an increase in Japanese
shipping at this time would be unsound, are the following:
1. We suggest that the peace of the world would best be
served by a policy which restricts Japanese shipping primarily to the
domestic Japanese trades and to service between Japan and nearby con-
tinental and island areas.
Comment — Tere is no indication that the size of the
Japanese merchant fleet will have any appreciable effect upon
world peace. Japan does not now appear to be a major factor
in present or near future world conflicts. As indicated else-
where, no decision has been reached concerning areas of limita-
tions of the Japanese fleet.
2. That Japanese shipping net income as represented by its ratio
to net national income, plays a small part in the overall Japanese economy,
representing less than 1%. Therefore, it is not an effective means of balanc-
ing Japanese finances or of raising living standards.
Comment — For Japan, the value of shipping must be reckoned in
terms of foreign exchange and not in terms of national income.
3. We question the justification of raising Japanese partici-
pation in international shipping to a relatively higher level than is
enjoyed by the United States, particularly when such stimulation of Japanese
economic recovery must be at our expense.
Comment — The restricting of Japanese shipping will be at U.S.
expense, and the benefits therefrom, by and large, will be enjoyed by
-8-

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)
other nations.
4. We question whether it is fair and just, at our expense, to
improve the economic stability and living standards of our recent enemy to
a point substantially superior to that of our recent allies in the 'same
geographic area.
Comment — Japan should be given minimum reasonable living standards.
5. Due to complete changes in government, physical conditions and
psychology, the past achievements and statistical records of Japan are not
reliable guides to the future production, overseas commerce and finances of
the nation. No one can foresee the future of Japanese industry as related
to world trade. We suggest it would be incautious to provide Japan at this
time with a merchant marine of a given capacity upon a purely conjectural
theory that certain tonnages in imports and exports will develop. The general
and gradual improvement of a cross-section of Japanese industry is a more
logical approach and as has been shown will better provide the means of
meeting any financial deficit.
Comment — It is agreed that specification of a fixed tonnage is
economically unsound. However, Japanese shipping should be permitted
to contribute its share to a stable economy.
While it is recognized any extrapolation is unsafe, nevertheless
it is necessary that some decision be made to permit progress toward
stability.
6. Japanese shipping although built and operated by cheap labor has
not been required by its government to compete upon a free trade basis in
international commerce, but has been substantially subsidized. Japan has
not followed the principle set down by the U.S. in the Merchant Marine Act
of 1936 of making subsidy payments only in an amount which will offset the
low wages and operating costs of competing foreign vessels thereby establish-
ing a condition of economic parity in competition.
Comment — What has occurred in the past when Japan was an inde-
pendent nation has no bearing on this discussion.
33. When the subject matter of this letter is considered by the
Department of the Army, by the Far Eastern Commission or by other national
or international agencies, this Federation will greatly appreciate the
opportunity of presenting further considerations.
Very truly yours,
/s/ F. A. Bailey
Frazer A. Bailey

Executive Officer, Civil Affairs Division,
January 26, 1949
Department of the Army
Office of Assistant Secretary of State for Occupied Areas
Policy Aspects Regarding Entry of Japanese Merchant Ships
into United States Ports
Reference is made to your memorandum of 24 January 1949 in which you
request that the Department of State advise your office if there are any
policy matters which would preclude Japanese ships from entering United States
ports.
The rehabilitation of the Japanese economy is an important part of our
long-range policy and the Japanese merchant marine was, and will undoubtedly
be, a significant factor in Japanese economic revival. Implementation of
this policy would imply eventual approval of the entry of Japanese merchant
ships into ports of the United States and other nations. It is our feeling,
however, that the time factor is of primary importance and that such action
can ve contemplated at a later date as pur program of redevelopment of Japan
unfolds, although planning for such an eventuality should be undertaken now.
In other woras, final pproval of this action should be given as a part of
integrated overall planning and only after full public understanding of the
program to assure maximum support.
It is anticipated that the cal lins of Japanese merchant ships at our
ports in the imnediate future would result in vigorous protests on the part
of U.s. interests. Any such action would be aggravated by the present
condition of unemployment that now exists with U.S. seamen. The reaction
of groups directly concer ed snould, however, pe considered in the light
of the eneral public interest. efore final approval on such action is
taken, it is believed that there should ce consultation with labor and in-.
dustry in this matter, as well as a coordinated review of the problem by
the interested agencies of the U.S. tovernment and consultation with ap pro-
SECR
priate members of the ongress and with other interested governments. A
beginning toward this coordination could be obtained by a referral of the
matter to the Interdepartmental hipping Committee with the suggestion that
it consult with labor and industry.
r
C. V. Hulick

FROM : NA - R. FeareyRAr
sUBJBCT: Attached Memorandum(C  F l i)
The following brief and preliminary comments are offered
on the Discussion and Conclusions of the attached Department
of Defense Policy Toward Japanese Industry:
Paragraph 6c: The meaning and purpose of this paragraph
are not clear. It would seem that whether or not there is
"enduring peace between the U.S. and USSR," Japan should not
be permitted to have any military establishment or primary
war facilities which would constitute a threat to the security
of any country.
Paragraph 8. second sentence: This sentence indicates that
the Army Department envisages only the eventual establishment
of limited Japanese armed forces. The JCS position in the
last paragraph of General Bradley's memorandum 1s still vague
as to the time when such defense forces should be authorized.
Files
Central
This Document Must Be Returned To
Paragraph 9, sixth sentence: The statement that "Civil
air transport has become as much an essential service as rail or
road transport and therefore is necessary to Japan in promoting
trade and accruing foreign exchange" seems a little strong.
8
The reference to foreign exchange might indicate that the
5
Defense Department envisages authorizing Japanese overseas air
/10-25
operations.
Paragraph 10. (1) and (2): In line with the comment
immediately above, there seems to be some question from an
all-around point of view whether Japan should be permitted
4
to manufacture civil air craft.
6
Paragraph loc: The Army never seems to understand that
we cannot unilaterally withdraw papers put into the FEC
machinery. We can only state that we no longer support them.
Paragraph lod: I would recommend against our submitting
new "level of industry" proposals to the FEC on the basis of
the Department of Defense's position. We did, of course,
promise to submit such proposals in our May 12 statement, and
it might be argued that to do so now would help to condition
the FEC countries to the treaty proposals we hope to make in a
couple of months. It seems highly doubtful, however, whether
the
TLED
TOP SECRET
X 100003

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP BECRET
-2-
the FEC in the state to which it has fallen, and with the
present personnel available, would be the proper place to
announce and argue our position. Also, we could be fairly cer-
tain that that position would not remain confidential within
the FEC. It wiil be noted, further, that the Department of
Defense's views as stated in paragraph 10 would require consider-
able further definition before they could be made a basis of
new U.S. proposals. The wiser course would seem to be to hold
our fire until we are prepared to discuss a treaty draft embody-
ing our position with the British and other friendly governments
on a high-level, government-to-government basis.
The second sentence of the second paragraph of the JCs
memorandum 1s unclear to say the least. It 1s interesting also,
though I suspect it is an oversight, that the JCs in summarizing
their earlier stated views in Nsc 49 make no mention of the
retention of U.S. forces in Japan after a treaty.

1949
FROM :TRC
Walter A. Radius
DIRECTOR
Department of State
suject: Comment on Lepartment of Defense's Proposals with Respect
to the Pre and Post Treaty Eras
TRC comments on the subject document are presented
below in the form of a proposed statement by the Department
of State to the Department of Defense, should that procedure
be agreed:
The Department of State concurs in the conclusion of
the Department of Defense and the Joint Chiefs of Staff
that for security reasons the maritime industries of Japan
(i.e., merchant shipbuilding, merchant ship repairing, and
merchant shipping) should not be subject to restrictions in
the post-treaty period.
The Department of State desires to point out, however,
Files
DC/R
Ii..s Document Must Be Returned 
that it has an obligation to the member agencies of the
Shipping Coordinating Committee to seek a unified Govern-
ment position with respect to the subject in question, par-
ticularly since the Maritime Commission has repeatedly ex-
4.8
pressed the desire to have the matter placed on the Committee's
agenda. It is important to our foreign policy to avoid the
possibility of having to face a Congress hostile to this
conclusion with a divided Executive viewpoint.
5
While recognizing the overriding security considerations
involved, and the importance of economic recovery to the
success of reoresentative government in Japan, and the as-
1
sistance such recovery will give in alleviating the financial
2
burden of the United States taxpayer, the Department of State
08
desires to point out certain other consequences that flow
49
from this decision which present future difficulties for
United States foreign policy, and therefore indirectly for
United States defense:
1.
Size and speed limitations have been imposed on the
maritime industries of G ermany for the Occupation
period, the restrictive effects of which wili undoubt-
edly carry over into the post-treaty period because of
the long-term nature of investments in capital equipment
of the maritime industries. In the Prohibited and Re-
stricted Industries Agreement for Germany, such restric-(
tions were justified on security grounds and without
regard to economic competitive factors. The Department
8
ILED
a letter
19
52
TOP SECRET
X 100010

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRET
- 2 -
a letter from Francis P. Matthews, Secretary of the
Navy, to the Secretary of Stete, July 15, 1949, which
reads in part:
"As stated in a previous letter of 1l February 1949
from the Under Secretary of the Navy to Mr. Saltzman,
the primary interest of the Depertment of the Navy in
the matter of German shipping and shipbuilding lies in
the effect t hat any increase in German shipping and
shipbuilcing might have on the security of the United
States. The size of the effective shipbuilding indus-
try permitted Gerrany is the major factor of the poten-
tial threat to our security since there is no apparent
way in which the skills and facilities required to build
men-of-war can be made materially distinct from the
skills and facilities required to build seagoing mer-
chant vessels. A relaxation of the present limitations
on shipbuilding capacity, of the surveillance and in-
spection of that capacity, or of the prohibition on
construction or acquisition of warships, will result in
increased hazard to the security of the United States."
The removal of all restrictions on the Japanese mari-
time industries would imply either that we are not con-
cerned about conparable factors in the maritime indus-
tries of Japan, particularly shipbuilding; or that the
security arguments as applied to Germany are specious.
It would seem as if the disadvantage to Germary arising
from a restrictive treatment in the pre-treaty maritime
industries, or to put it the other way, the advantage
that will accrue to Japan from not having comparable
restrictions, may have been purely fortuitous.
2.
To the extent that the security of the United States
rests on the assumption of an unrestricted shipbuilding
program for Japan, it would seem that the present policy
of having Japan's shipbuilding industry devoted to
foreign account in order to obtain foreign exchange
should be reversed in the pre-treaty period, and that
her building program might better be directed to her
own needs. This would also heve the adventage of reduc-
ing the competitive pressure on the shipbuilding in-
dustries of the European Recovery nations, even if it
would reduce the foreign exchange for Japan in the in-
terests of her own security.
3.
An important part of the foreign policy of the United
States is the rehabilitation of the participating
nations
ROP SECRET

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRET
- 3 -
nations in the European Recovery Program. Invisible
earnings from the maritime industries represent a
significant item in their individual and collective
balance of payments accounts. This will be even more
true in the period immediately following the termination
of E.C.A. aid. To the extent that they may be confronted
with unrestricted competition from Japan at that critical
period in their post-war development, there will be an
unavoidable reduction in the respective shipping earn-
ings of all the recovery nations.
4.
The impact of unrestricted Japanese maritime industry
competition on India's and Pakistan's desire t o achieve
a merchant marine of their own will be seriously re-
sented by those nations. In view of the Department's
objective of maintaining friendly relations with these
nations, concern for their reactlon to this policy can-
not be entirely eliminated.
5.
The economic justification for adding to an existing
and projected world surplus in merchant shipping ca-
pacity is dubious on grounds other than the principle
of having comparative national efficiencies work them-
selves out in free competition. However, the adjustment
of over-supply to demand in the shipping industry is a
much slower process than in many other industries be-
cause of the long-term investment (20 to 30 years) in
capital equipment, and because of the inevitable in-
volvement with naticnal and defense interests. There-
fore, additional inorements to an existing surplus from
whatever source may have painful repercussions on the
future of international trade - primarily by precipi-
tating increases in national flag discriminations by
way of a defense rationele in order t o hold trade once
captured in a less competitive market. The Department
looks with concern upon the future impact on foreign
policy of such a contingency.
6.
The Lepartment is bound to call a ttention to the pro-
visions contained in Section 1ol of the Merchant Marine
Act of 1936:
"It is necessary for the national defense and develop-
ment of its foreign and domestic commrce that the
United States shall have a merchant marine sufficient
to carry its domestic water-borne commerce and a sub-
stantial portion of the water-borne export and import
foreign commerce of the United S tates......"
The post-war
TOP SECRET

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP SECRET
-4-
The post-war efforts of the United States shipping in-
dustry and maritime labor to hold their wartime gains -
even to increase them - have been spectacularly suc-
cessful in Congressional legislation. The Department
is on notice from both industry and labor that offsets
will be sought against the competition from the maritime
industries of the defeated powers. To the extent that
such offsets would take the form of additional legisla-
tive preferences, subsidies, and freight rate differen-
tials for United States flag shipping, our foreign
policy would be adversely affected by the inevitable
retaliations of other countries. Moreover, while it
may be argued that the financial burden to the United
States taxpayer will be alleviated by the recovery of
the maritime industry of Japan, it ay well be that the
financial savings in this instance would he transferred
in the form of subsidies to the pvican maritime in-
austries whose cometitive pos tion would be affected
iy such unrestricted co petition. No other industry
his presented quite the same problen to our post-war
foreign policy as has the shipping industry of the
United Jtates.
It is conceded by the Department that the economic con-
siderations outlined above are secondary to the defense con-
siderations outlined by the Department of Defense and the
Joint Chiefs of Staff in the dossier accompanying General
Carter Maruder's Memorandum to the Assistant Secretary of
State for Far Eastern Affairs, dated cctober 21, 1949.
Nevertheless, they are detailed herein for the purpose of
transmittin the Department of State's experience with the
recent legislative history of preferential treatment for
American shipping by the Congress of the United States.
In making the decision to place no pre- or post-treaty re-
strictions on the Haritime industries of Japan, the Depart-
ment of Defense and the Joint Chiefs of Staff should be
aware of the probable strong opposition from the Congress
not only with respect to this particular section of a
proposed peace treaty, but to the proposal to lift the re-
strictions prior to the peace treaty. It is for this reason
that the Department considers it essential to have the problem
discussed in an executive session of the Shipping Coordinating
Comnittee in order to obtain a unified United States position.
When such a position is obtained, it would appear equally
essential to hold similar discussions with at least the
Foreign Relations Comnittees of the Congress.

November 8, 1949
MEMORANDUM FOR FILE:
 Subject matter of Mr. Radius' memorandum was discussed at a
meeting in my office this afternoon at which were present Mr. Radius,
Mr. Howard, Mr. Thayer from NEA, Mr. Fearey and myself. During the
course of the discussion, Mr. Radius told us that the subject was to
be considered at an Executive Session of the Shipping Coordinating
Committee at which time the Maritime Commission would have an oppor-
tunity to put forward such views as it might have. Mr. Howard and
I stressed our view that we should let the Defense Department carry
the ball in explaining its conclusion that no restrictions on Japanese
shipping were necessary from the security point of view. We also
said that we thought that Mr. Butterworth or Mr. Merchant, one of
whom Mr. Radius said would be present at the meeting, would probably
6
take the position that from the point of view of general commercial
4
policy, the U. S. did not favor imposing restrictions on ship build-
ing activities. I said that,in addition, FE on general policy grounds
8
opposed any restrictions on Japanese merchant shipping or ship build-
5
ing not required for security reasons. Mr. Radius showed some desire
to have some of the views expressed in his memorandum communicated to
the Defense Department. Mr. Howard and I felt that there was no need
for this, and that the whole matter would be taken care of adequately
by the meeting of the Shipning Coordinating Committee to be held next
849
Tuesday. Mr. Radius with some reluctance assented.

Discussion
6. Although Fara ranh 4, Ns0 13/3, states that a final U.8. position
concerning the ost treaty arrangements for Janenese military seourity
should nct be formulated until the peace negotiations are upon us, and it
should then be formulated in the light of the prevailing international
situation an the degree of internal stability aohieved in Japan, neverthe-
less to assist in solving the present problem it is necessary to make theee
basic assumntions:
a. that the nited States will not permit Japan to become
Soviet dominaled either throush conquest or subversion.
4
TOP SECHET

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP SEORET
b. That, 1n the event of war between the United States and
the U.S.S.R., Japan will be a country occupied by U.S. forees, a friendly
non-belligerent, or an active ally of the United States.
o. That, 1f there sh uld be an enduring peace between the
United States and the U.3.3.R., Japan would not be permitted to have
any military establishment or primary war facilities which would constitute
a threat to the seourity of any country.
7. Obviously, 1f the Uni ted States 1s unwilling to take all steps
necessary to satisfy the first two assumptions, Japan could go to the
U.S.S.R. by default and would become a serious threat to the seourity of
the United Stetes and to all ther non-Sovlet nations. If the United
States should decide not to protect Japan, then from a strategie point
of view U.S. intereste would best be served by seeking the olosest
aporoximat1 n possible to a denopulated and devastated Japan. Otherwise,
any Japanese potentlal would be available for use against the United
States and our friends. However, failure to protect Japan would be
illogical and such a course is not c nsidered here.
8. An economically stron2, friendly and western-oriented Japan
would be of reat value to the security of the nited States. Consider-
ation will need to be  iven to the eventual establishment of limited
Jananese armed forces to maintain internal security and to assist in
looal defensive action in event of en emergency.
9. "Seoond only to U.. seourity interests, economic recovery should
be made the primary objective of United States policy in Japan." The two
objectives to hand in hand. As long as the nited States is responsible
for Jepan, any defiolts in its economy will ultimately be paid for in
dollars by the U.s. taxpayer. Janon has been stripped of 1ts colonies,
and access has been closed to many of its nearest and best markets. Many
large, fast ships are needed to reech more distant markets and sources of
raw materials. Civil nir transport in  modern ecciety has become as
much an essential service as rail or road transnort and therefore is
necessary to Janan in promotin trade snd nccruing foreign exchange. If
a reas nable prosperity can be created, no: only vill the burden on the
U.3. taxnayer be removed, but representative government in Japan will have
a more fertile field in which to row.
Concluaions
10. It is concluded that:
a. There should be no limita ions or restriotlons on Japen's
commerce, communications or veaceful industries. In answer to specifio
questions couched by the Department of stste, the position of the Depart-
ment of Defense is as follows:
5

TOP SECRET
(1) Manufacture of civil airoraft--Manufacture should
be limited to types and numbers whioh would not constitute a
threat to the seour1ty of the United States.
(2) Operation of civil airoraft--Cperation should be
limited to an extent which would not constitute a threat to
the seourity of the United States.
(3) Merohant marine--No limitations.
(4) Merohant shipbuilding and ship repair--No limitations.
b. So far as praotioal, the foregoing should apply both to
the present and the post-oooupation period.
o. All pending policy papers which have been introduoed by
the United states into the FEC which are inconsistent with the foregoing
should be withdrawn.
d. The Un1ted States should propose to the FEC, regardless
of the 1mprobability of favorable consideration, that FEc policy decisions
which are inconsistent with the foregoin: should be amended to conform or
be repealed.
e. All SWNCC and SAlACC policies inconsistent with the fore-
going should be superseded by new controlling doouments.
f. Where incons1atent with the foregoing, NsC 13/3 should be
amended.

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.96)
SECRET
TO
: The Acting Seoretary
DATE: November 9, 1949
Through : S/s
FROM : FE - Mr. Butterworth
SUBJECT:
Measures to Stop Unwarranted Seizures of Japanese Fishing
Vessels
xR
Fil'es
Central
RM/R
874.628
Discussion:
During 1948 and 1949, a total of sixty-seven' seizures
of Japanese fishing vessels both inside and outside the
SCAP-authorized fishing area has been reported. Mest of
these seizures have been by the Chinese who in manys
instances have retained the vessels for operations in
the civil war and in seme instancee, thgy have resorted
to sinking the vessels and killing the crews.ome
the vessels have been seized by the Soviets  but the
latter seizures have consisted-only or those vessels
operating beyond the authorized-area Numerous protests
have been lodged by SCAP with the Chinese Mission in Tok
to no avail. SCAP feels that the seizures are threateni
to reduce marine food production and that he has an.oblig
tion to protect the fishing fleet. Pursuant to SCAP's
request, the Secretary of Defense has requested the State
Department to approach the Chinese Government directly
in an effort to secure the cessation of further seizures
by Chinese patrols and a release of all vessels and their
crews now held by Chinese authorities (Tab A). Instruo-
tions to this effect have been sent to Chungking.
The suggested reply to the Secretary of Defense's
letter is attached herewith. In this reply, it has been
considered advisable to emphasize to the Secretary of
Defense that any further eteps to protect Jepanese fish-
ing vessels should only be taken after advance consulta-
tion with the Department of State. The reason for this
emphasiε is as follows:
APR
I
The Department's attention has been informally callod
to two plane which are being considered by SCAP and the
E
Service Departments to prcteet Japanese fishing-veseels
(Tab B). The first relates to the establishment of a
55
regular U.S. naval patrol of the fishing area in the East
China Sea, following trial sweeps last month of this area
by U.S. Navy units. The second measure is one recommended
D
by the Chief of Naval Operations for the establishment of a
Japanese-manned patrol force under SCAP, and for the use
4
by that force of the score or more armed frigates which are
being returned to the US at Japanese ports by the USSR.
It
6
SECRET

=== Page 430 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
-2-
It is my preliminary view that, provided the US patrol
acted under certain opcrating instructione designed to mini-
mize the poseibility of international incidente, the patrol
of the East China Sea hy our naval vessels might prove a
desirable and effective method of protecting Japanese fishing
operations. Nevertheless it is a measure to which we probably
ehould not have recourse unless depredations against Japanese
fishing vessels continued deepite our formel protects to the
Chinese Government. As for the second suggested couree of
remedial action, I believe the operetion of frigates by the
Japanese would be contrary to the spirit and letter of FEC
policy decisions and of the Potedam Declaration, although
there might be no specific legal prohibition againat turning
over these frigates, disarmed, to the Japanese Fieheries
Inspection Fleet. Furthermore, in view of the present status
of our lend-lease negotiations with the USSR, it is doubtful
that the Department can approve transfer of these frigates
to Japanese hands in the immediate future.
Recommendation:
It is recommended that you sign the attached letter to
the Secretary of Defense.
Concurrences:

Dear Sir:
The receipt is acknowleuged of : r letter of
November 10, 1949, inquiring if there re any restrictions
or law preventing a United States corporation from con-
tracting for the construction of a vessel in a Japanese
shipyard for sale to Portugese interests and for
registration under the Portugese flag, without first
placing same under United States registry and flag.
The Naritime Commission would have no jurisdiction
over such transactions during peace times. You might,
however, wish to consult the State Department in the
matter.

HANOVER 2-4037-4038
November 10, 1949
United States Maritime Commission
Washington, D. C.
Gentlemen:
A client of mine, a New York corporation, is engaged
in negotiations with a shipyard in Japan for the con-
struction of an l800 ton steamer. My client also has a
commitment from a Portugese corporation for the sale of
that steamer with the understanding that the vessel can
be registered under the Flag of Portugal. With a
realization that Japan is still under occupation by the
United States, I thought it best, before proceeding with
negotiations, to ascertain if there are any restrictions
against a transaction such as above described or if it is
lawful for an American corporation to contract for the
construction of a vessel in Japan with the understanding
that it is not to be placed under the American Flag, but
is to be resold to Portugese interests for registry under
Portugese Flag.
I shall appreciate your response to this letter at
your earliest convenience.
Very truly yours,
GEORGE A. CONROY
GAC:DR

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.98)
OFC2319
In reply refer to
NA
My dear Mr. Conroy:
The receipt 1s acknowledged of your letter of
November 21, 1949, to which are attached cop1es of
your letter of November 10 to the U.S. Maritime
Commission and the Cammission's reply to you.
It is noted that your client, a New York corpora-
tion, is engaged in negotiations with a shipyard in
Japan for the construction of an 1800 ton steamer, and
that your client has a comnitment from a Portuguese
corporation for the sale of the vessel subject to the
understanding that it can be registered under the flag
of Portugal. The Department is not aware of any restrio-
tions, based on United States law, which would bar an
American corporation from contracting for construotion
abroad of a vessel which is to be sold to Portuguese
interests end placed under Portuguese flag without first
being laced under American registry and flag.
2147
For your information, the occupation of Japan is
administered by the Supreme Commender for the Allied
Powers who derives his authority not from the United
States alone but from its Allies as well. Non-Japanese
nationals and firms are permitted to engage in inter-
national trade or other business aotivity in Japan
pursuant to and in compliance with laws and regulations
of the Japanese Government and the Supreme Commander.
It is
Mr. George A. Conroy.
15 William Street,
New York S, New York.

FROM: Taipei, Taiwan No. 140 December 27, 1949.

U/FW
John J. Macdonald, American Consul General, Taipei
SUBJECT: Action taken on letter under reference.
7311
Receint is acknowledged of the Department's instruction
of November 29, 1949 on the seizure of Japanese fishing
vessels by Chinese patrols which requested this office to
894.85/12-2749
make an approach to the appropriate Taiwan authorities
along the lines suggested in the attached instruction.
The problem has been brought to the attention of
General CHEN Cheng in his capacity of South East Defense
Commander, the emphasis having been placed on the desire
for the release of the seized Japanese fishing vessels.
In as much as General Chen Cheng is not in a position to
determine the policy of the National Government concerning
the extent of the power of SCAP, this facet of the problem
was not emphasized to him.
General Chen stated that he would investigate the
causes of the SCAP complaint and discuss the matter
further after study.
In a subsequent informal conversation Admiral KWEI
jokingly admitted the Chinese Navy possessed several
Japanese fishing vessels which they could not and would
not release in as much as they were vitally necessary
to the current military operations based on Taiwan and
extending to the coastal islands of Kinmen and the
Chusan Archipelago.
